Improved Turbo Message Passing for Compressive Robust Principal Component Analysis: Algorithm Design and Asymptotic Analysis
Zhuohang He, Junjie Ma, and Xiaojun Yuan

Abstract
Compressive Robust Principal Component Analysis (CRPCA) naturally arises in various applications as a means to recover a low-rank matrix low-rank matrix 𝑳 \boldsymbol{L} and a sparse matrix 𝑺 \boldsymbol{S} from compressive measurements. In this paper, we approach the problem from a Bayesian inference perspective. We establish a probabilistic model for the problem and develop an improved turbo message passing (ITMP) algorithm based on the sum-product rule and the appropriate approximations. Additionally, we establish a state evolution framework to characterize the asymptotic behavior of the ITMP algorithm in the large-system limit. By analyzing the established state evolution, we further propose sufficient conditions for the global convergence of our algorithm. Our numerical results validate the theoretical results, demonstrating that the proposed asymptotic framework accurately characterize the dynamical behavior of the ITMP algorithm, and the phase transition curve specified by the sufficient condition agrees well with numerical simulations.



I
Introduction

Modern high-dimensional data usually possess low intrinsic dimensionality due to the natural dynamics or human influences.
To identify the low-dimensional structure of high dimensional data is a universal problem in statistical signal processing. Principal component analysis (PCA) is a widely used statistical procedure that produces a low-dimensional representation of high-dimensional data. However, such a representation provided by PCA is sensitive to the outliers contained in the data. To address this issue, robust
principal component analysis (RPCA)
[
2
]
has been proposed as a promising solution to suppress sparse outliers. The RPCA problem can be formulated as to recover a low-rank matrix
\boldsymbol{L}
from grossly corrupted measurements

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}=\boldsymbol{L}+\boldsymbol{S}+\boldsymbol{N}

(1)

where
\boldsymbol{S}
accounts for sparse outliers,
\boldsymbol{N}
accounts for the additive noise with the elements having relatively small magnitudes, and
n_{1}
(or
n_{2}
) is the number of rows (columns) of
\boldsymbol{X}
.
This model and its variants have been extensively applied in addressing various real-world problems, including but not limited to:

•

Community Detection:
RPCA plays a pivotal role in identifying clusters or communities within networks, where nodes exhibit higher connectivity within the group than with the broader network. These methods are especially useful in detecting evolving or temporal communities efficiently
[
3
]
.

•

Network Traffic Anomalies:
In network security and performance monitoring, RPCA is applied to detect abnormal patterns or behaviors in traffic data. This anomaly detection is crucial for identifying potential security threats and ensuring stable network performance
[
4
,
5
]
.

•

Acoustic Separation:
RPCA is widely used in the field of audio processing, particularly for separating overlapping sound sources. This is vital for improving sound quality in applications such as speech enhancement and audiovisual separation
[
6
,
7
]
.

•

Computer Vision:
In video and image analysis, RPCA is employed to decompose visual data into a low-rank component representing the background and a sparse component representing the foreground, such as moving objects. This has numerous applications in object tracking, video surveillance, and scene analysis
[
8
,
9
]
.

•

Recommendation Systems:
RPCA has been applied in recommendation systems to predict user preferences based on sparse and incomplete data, a problem commonly known as collaborative filtering. The low-rank matrix models typical user preferences, while the sparse matrix accounts for anomalies or errors in the data. This helps improve the accuracy of recommendations in online platforms like movie or product recommendation systems
[
10
]
.

•

Face Recognition:
RPCA is also used in face recognition, where face images can be represented by a low-rank matrix due to their inherent low-dimensional nature. Imperfections such as shadows or lighting variations are modeled as sparse errors. Successfully separating these components can significantly enhance the performance of face recognition systems, even when images are occluded or distorted
[
11
]
.

In a mathematical form, the problem can be formulated as:

\displaystyle\operatorname{minimize}

\displaystyle\operatorname{rank}(\boldsymbol{L})+\lambda\|\boldsymbol{S}\|_{0}

\displaystyle\text{ subject to }

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}+\boldsymbol{S}=\boldsymbol{X},

where
\operatorname{rank}(\cdot)
denotes the rank and
\|\cdot\|_{0}
denotes
\l_{0}
.
The RPCA problem is known as NP-hard in general
[
12
]
. Approximate approaches have been proposed in the literature to solve the RPCA problem
[
2
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
.
The following convex relaxation to RPCA, which is referred to as Principal Component Pursuit (PCP), has been extensively studied
[
17
,
18
,
19
]

\displaystyle\operatorname{minimize}\|\boldsymbol{L}\|_{*}+\lambda\|\boldsymbol{S}\|_{1}\quad\text{ subject to }\boldsymbol{L}+\boldsymbol{S}=\boldsymbol{X}.

(2)

Here,
\|\cdot\|_{*}
denotes the nuclear norm, and
\|\cdot\|_{1}
denotes the
\ell_{1}
norm. The PCP problem in (
2
) can be solved by various iterative methods, such as the alternating direction method
[
19
]
, augmented Lagrange multiplier
[
18
]
, and iterative thresholding
[
20
]
, among others. These algorithms typically involve a singular value decomposition (SVD) step in each iteration, leading to high computational complexity for large-scale problems.

Non-convex approaches have been explored to alleviate the computational burden of SVD and improve recovery performance. For example, the authors in
[
21
]
propose to reparametrize the low-rank matrix as the product of a tall matrix and a wide matrix (i.e.,
\boldsymbol{L}=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}
) and then perform alternating minimization on the reparametrized model. This reparametrization technique is also considered in
[
22
]
, where the projected gradient method is applied to the factorized spaces spanned by
\boldsymbol{U}
and
\boldsymbol{V}
. In
[
23
]
, a generic projected gradient descent algorithm for general loss functions is proposed with the factorization
\boldsymbol{L}=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}
. In
[
24
]
, the authors improve the statistical performance guarantee compared to the methodology developed in prior work
[
25
]
.
In
[
26
]
, the authors consider a matrix identification problem, where
\boldsymbol{S}
is a matrix of outliers that is nonzero in only a fraction of its columns. The proposed algorithms proceed with a two-step adaptive sampling and inference procedure to identify the locations of the nonzero columns of
\boldsymbol{S}
.

To exploit the prior information of
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
, another line of research aims to solve the RPCA problem based on probabilistic models from a Bayesian perspective. For example, a sparse Bayesian learning algorithm was proposed in
[
27
]
for RPCA, where marginal distributions are approximated based on mean-field variational Bayes
[
28
]
. Early work by Chen et al.
[
29
]
introduces a variational Bayesian approach for sparse component estimation and low-rank matrix recovery. In this work, the low-rank matrix is modeled as the product of two random Gaussian matrices, which may not align well with real-world scenarios. A similar variational Bayesian framework is also adopted in
[
30
]
for sparse additive matrix factorization. However, the assumption of Gaussian distribution for the additive dense noise may not be entirely appropriate for many practical applications. In
[
31
]
, the authors further leverage realistic sparse distributions that go beyond simple sparsity by exploiting structural dependencies between the values and locations of the sparse coefficients in real applications, such as face modeling experiments and background subtraction.
In
[
32
]
, the authors present an adaptive regularizer learning strategy that improves low-rank and sparse matrix decomposition by addressing the limitations of the traditional
\ell_{1}
-norm, which tends to over-penalize. By leveraging a Bayesian framework and an Alternating Direction Method of Multipliers (ADMM) optimization algorithm, the proposed method achieves superior results in image denoising and foreground-background extraction. In
[
33
]
, the author proposes a robust sparse linear regression objective function to address the RPCA problem, equivalent to the fundamental “rank+sparsity”
minimization objective, with MAP-EM applied to solve the Bayesian inference problem. BiG-AMP (bilinear generalized approximate message passing)
[
34
]
recasts the RPCA problem as a probabilistic inference problem, constructs the corresponding factor graph, and derives an approximate message passing algorithm based on the sum-product rule. In this algorithm, the low-rank matrix
\boldsymbol{L}
is treated as the product of two random i.i.d. matrices, which may be unrealistic for certain applications. Recently, a Bayesian algorithm
[
35
]
developed for general bi-linear models, based on the framework of variational inference and the UAMP algorithm
[
36
]
, can be applied to the RPCA problem.

As inspired by the success of compressed sensing
[
37
,
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
]
,
compressive robust principal component analysis (CRPCA) aims to recover low-rank matrix
\boldsymbol{L}
and sparse matrix
\boldsymbol{S}
from noisy compressive measurements of the mixture
\boldsymbol{L}+\boldsymbol{S}
. Mathematically, the noisy compressive measurements can be modeled as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}=\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{X})+\boldsymbol{n}=\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{L}+\boldsymbol{S})+\boldsymbol{n}

(3)

where
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a linear operator with
m\ll n_{1}n_{2}
,
\boldsymbol{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is a rank-
r
matrix with
r\ll n_{1},n_{2}
,
\boldsymbol{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is a sparse matrix with sparsity level
\rho
(i.e., a fraction
\rho
of the elements of
\boldsymbol{S}
are nonzero),
and
\boldsymbol{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times 1}
is an additive white Gaussian noise with mean zero and variance
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{I}}
. In (
3
), “compressive” means that the number of measurements (i.e.,
m
) is much less than the number of unknown variables (i.e.,
n_{1}n_{2}
). The goal of CRPCA is to recover
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
from the noisy compressive measurements in
\boldsymbol{y}
.

Similarly to PCP, a convex program, termed Compressive Principal Component Pursuit (CPCP)
[
42
]
, was proposed to solve the compressive RPCA problem:

\displaystyle\operatorname{minimize}

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{L}\|_{*}+\lambda\|\boldsymbol{S}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\text{ subject to }

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{L}+\boldsymbol{S})=\boldsymbol{y}.

The optimization methods introduced in RPCA models for PCP are also applicable to CPCP with some necessary modifications
[
43
,
23
,
24
]
. A greedy algorithm
[
44
]
, named SpaRCS, combined the advantages of CoSaMP
[
45
]
for sparse signal recovery and ADMiRA
[
46
]
for low-rank matrix recovery.
Building upon SpaRCS, R-SpaRCS
[
47
]
further accounted for the connectedness of the support of the sparse foreground component in videos, resulting in improved performance. In
[
48
]
, the authors address the CRPCA problem in an online setting. Unlike the offline scenario, previous frames provide additional prior information that aids in recovering the
t
-th frame. The proposed COCRPCA algorithm leverages this prior information for both the sparse and low-rank components. The proposed
n-\ell_{1}
minimization problem with adaptive weights is solved by the proximal gradient methods. Following
[
48
]
, a cluster-based formulation is added to the objective function of the sparse component in
[
49
]
. With this constraint, the proposed CODA algorithm outperforms COCRPCA, as demonstrated in their experiments. Theoretical guarantees regarding the number of measurements required for convergence are provided in
[
50
]
.

To alleviate the high computational complexity involved in the above convex methods, a multilevel optimization algorithm
[
51
]
was proposed based on the Frank-Wolfe thresholding method developed in
[
52
]
.

To facilitate comparison with the previous algorithms, we have created a table to help readers easily reference the differences.
Table
I
in Appendix.
G
includes the details and provides a clear comparison between our proposed method and existing approaches.

In this paper, we tackle the CRPCA problem from the perspective of Bayesian inference. We establish a probability model for the problem, and then develop an improved turbo message passing (ITMP) algorithm based on the sum-product rule
[
53
]
and appropriate approximations
[
54
]
. The proposed ITMP algorithm consists of three denoisers, namely, a linear denoiser that handles the noisy linear observation
\boldsymbol{y}
, a sparse denoiser that handles the sparsity of
\boldsymbol{S}
, and a low-rank denoiser that handles the low-rank constraint of
\boldsymbol{L}
. The three denoisers are iteratively executed until convergence is achieved. Compared to previous work
[
54
,
55
]
on TMP algorithms for CRPCA, we provide a new interpretation for deriving the CRPCA algorithm, along with algorithmic analysis. Our analysis demonstrates that, under proper assumptions, the parameters in the dynamic algorithm converge asymptotically to an analytical form. This convergence ensures the validity and accuracy of certain empirical approximations within the algorithm. Furthermore, based on our asymptotic analysis, we establish a guarantee of algorithmic convergence. Such rigorous analysis, along with the derived asymptotic parameter forms, has not been available in prior work.

The main contributions of our work are summarized as follows:

\bullet

Contribution 1.

(A new interpretation of TMP for the CRPCA problem)
: To facilitate the calculation of messages, we introduce two basic assumptions to model the input errors of the sparse denoiser and the low-rank denoiser as independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) sequences in each iteration. These two assumptions allows us to readily calculate the statistical coefficients (such as means and variances) required in turbo message passing. Interestingly, our analysis shows that the operation of the ITMP algorithm in each iteration preserves the uncorrelation between the signal to be recovered and the corresponding error at the input of the sparse/low-rank denoiser. Since independence implies uncorrelation, the two assumptions are to some extent self-consistent from iteration to iteration.

\bullet

Contribution 2.

(State evolution (SE) analysis)
: We further establish the state evolution to characterize the asymptotic behaviors of the proposed ITMP algorithm in the large-system limit. Concretely speaking, under certain regularity conditions, we analyze the asymptotic behaviors of the denoisers, and derive the corresponding asymptotic mean-square error (MSE) transfer functions. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first time to establish an analytical expression for the asymptotic MSE transfer function of the spectral denoiser (a family of popularly used low-rank denoisers) under arbitrary i.i.d. input noise with zero mean and bounded variance.

\bullet

Contribution 3.

(Convergence analysis)
: Based on the developed state evolution, we analyze the global convergence of the proposed ITMP algorithm. We provide a sufficient condition for the global convergence of the proposed algorithm based on the monotonicity of the MSE transfer functions. The phase transition boundary predicted by the sufficient condition agrees well with the empirical phase transition curve obtained from numerical experiments.

I-A

Prior Work Related to Contribution 1

Message passing and its variants have been widely used to recover structured signals with near-optimal performance
[
53
,
56
]
. For example, approximate message passing (AMP)
[
57
]
, initially developed for the recovery of sparse signals in underdetermined linear systems, is a popular compressed sensing algorithm due to its low complexity. The AMP algorithm consists of two recursively executed steps: One step is to produce a coarse estimate of the signal to be recovered based on the observed linear measurements; the other step is to refine the estimate based on the sparsity of the signed to be recovered. The AMP algorithm has been extended to solve various statistical estimation problems, including sparse signal reconstruction with generalized linear models
[
58
,
59
]
, low-rank matrix recovery
[
60
,
61
,
62
]
and
signal reconstruction with more sophisticated signal structures
[
63
]
. It is known that the AMP algorithm has guaranteed performance only with a Gaussian or sub-Gaussian measurement matrix
[
64
,
65
]
, where the elements of the measurement matrix are independently drawn from a Gaussian or sub-Gaussian distribution.

Compressed sensing applications, however, usually employ non-i.i.d. measurement matrix based on fast orthogonal transforms, such as the discrete Fourier transform (DFT) and the discrete cosine transform (DCT).
Similarly to AMP, the TMP algorithm has been extended to solve various structured signal reconstruction problems
[
66
,
67
,
68
,
69
,
54
]
. The state evolution of TMP has been rigorously established when the measurement is a right-orthogonally invariant linear (ROIL) operator
[
70
]
. The above message passing algorithms are all established based on a critical assumption, i.e., the input errors of the non-linear denoisers are assumed to be Gaussian
[
66
,
67
,
68
,
69
]
. It has been shown that this approximation is asymptotically true in the large system limit of AMP and turbo message passing for sparse signal recovery and low-rank matrix recovery problems
[
64
,
65
]
.
However, we find that in message passing for the CRPCA problem, the input errors of the low-rank denoiser do not behave as Gaussian distributed
1
1

1

The message passing algorithm for the low-rank matrix recovery problem involves only two modules, namely, a linear denoiser for handling the linear measurements, and a low-rank denoiser for handling the low-rank property of
\boldsymbol{L}
. Due to the linear mixing effect of the linear module, the input errors of the low-rank denoiser (i.e., the output errors of the linear module) follows a Gaussian distribution in the large-system limit. However, as aforementioned, the ITMP algorithm for CRPCA involves the iteration of three modules, where the input of the low-rank denoiser is a mixture of the outputs of the linear denoiser and the sparse denoiser. The output errors of the sparse denoiser are non-Gaussian, and so are the input errors of the low-rank denoiser.
. As such, the approaches in
[
66
,
67
,
68
,
69
]
, if directly applied to the CRPCA problem, suffer from performance degradation due to the Gaussian error approximation.

In this paper, we formulate the CRPCA problem as a probabilistic inference problem and derive an approximate message passing algorithm based on the sum-product rule. The proposed ITMP algorithm consists of three modules, where a linear denoiser is employed to linearly estimate
\boldsymbol{X}
based on
\boldsymbol{y}
in (
3
) and the messages from the other two modules, and two nonlinear denoisers that refine the coarse input estimates of
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
by exploiting the low-rankness of
\boldsymbol{L}
and the sparsity of
\boldsymbol{S}
. We assume that the input errors of the low-rank denoiser
(and the sparse denoiser) are i.i.d., but not necessarily Gaussian distributed.
We systematically derive the operations of the three denoisers by following the principle of extrinsic message exchange
[
54
]
.
With the new formulation, it is helpful to analyze the state evolution.
Moreover, based on the proposed algorithm design and starting from the i.i.d. assumptions for the current iteration input noise of non-linear denoisers, we prove that after each iteration, the input noise of each non-linear denoiser is uncorrelated with signal to be recovered. This uncorrelation is consistent with the two i.i.d. assumptions. This means that the i.i.d. assumptions are to some extent self-consistent under the proposed formulation during the iterative process.

I-B

Prior Work Related to Contribution 2

Under certain randomness assumptions on the measurement matrix, the dynamic processes of message-passing based compressed sensing algorithms
[
65
,
71
]
admit scalar functions to characterize the evolution of the mean-square errors (MSEs) (a.k.a. the state evolution). This work aims to establish the state evolution of ITMP for the CRPCA problem. We note that the CRPCA problem reduces to the sparse signal recovery problem by setting
\boldsymbol{L}
to zero, and to the low-rank matrix recovery problem by setting
\boldsymbol{S}
to zero. These two reduced problems can be solved by message passing algorithms; see, e.g.,
[
57
,
62
,
66
,
64
,
70
]
. However, the existing state evolution analysis cannot be straightforwardly extended to the TMP algorithm for the CRPCA problem. The main obstacle is that the empirical distribution of the input errors of the low-rank denoiser (as one of the three modules of the TMP algorithm) is generally non-Gaussian. As such, the main challenge of the state evolution is to establish the MSE transfer function of the low-rank denoiser with non-Gaussian input errors.

We now introduce the existing literature on the analysis of the low-rank denoiser. In
[
72
,
73
]
, the Stein’s unbiased risk estimation (SURE) can be employed to estimate the output MSE of the low-rank denoiser. These analyses cannot be used in our work since they are limited to case of Gaussian input noise. Another approach is to formulate low-rank denoising as a minimax risk problem that optimizes the denoiser function to obtain the minimal risk guarantee under a fixed input noise power. In the minimax risk formulation, the optimal hard threshold and the optimal shrinkage of singular values for fixed rank
r
are obtained in the large-system limit
[
74
]
. In
[
75
]
, the authors analyzed the optimal singular-value soft threshold in the large-system limit with a proportionally increasing rank. Yet, the asymptotic MSEs obtained in
[
74
]
,
[
75
]
are obtained by fixing the noise level and letting the signal power go to infinity, which only represents a point in our desired MSE transfer function.

In this paper, we derive an asymptotic MSE transfer function of the low-rank denoiser with i.i.d. non-Gaussian input noise under certain regularity conditions. Our results are applicable to commonly used denoisers, including the best-rank-r denoiser, the singular-value soft-threshold denoiser, and the singular-value hard-threshold denoiser.

I-C

Prior Work Related to Contribution 3

A phase transition is a rapid and significant transformation in the nature of a computational problem as its parameters undergo variations. This phenomenon emerges in a variety of compressed sensing problems; for example, see
[
57
,
76
,
42
,
77
,
62
]
. For the CRPCA problem, we consider a parameter space with axes quantifying sparsity level
k/n=\rho
, rank fraction
r/n_{1}=\gamma
and undersampling ratio
m/n=\alpha
. In the limit of large dimensions
n
, the parameter space splits in two phases: one presenting that the proposed algorithm is successful in accurately reconstructing
\boldsymbol{S}
and
\boldsymbol{L}
, and the other representing the unsuccessful case. For the message passing based algorithms, state evolution, which tracks the MSE between the real signals and the estimations, can be used to analyze the phase transition of the algorithms. When the MSE approaches zero, it means that the signal has been accurately recovered. Phase transitions have been extensively studied in the sparse signal recovery problem
[
57
,
76
]
and low-rank matrix recovery problem
[
62
]
, and tight bounds have been obtained by analysing the convergence of SE for the AMP based algorithms. Ref.
[
77
]
further provides an explanation of phase transitions by using high-dimensional geometric tools.

For the CRPCA problem, a preliminary analysis of the phase transition bound has been conducted in
[
42
]
under the framework of finite dimensional settings. This result, however, becomes trivial in the large system limit, since the global convergence bound of
\gamma
goes to zero as
n\to\infty
. To the best of our knowledge, the phase transition of the CRPCA problem in the large system limit remains a challenging open problem.

In this paper, based on the developed SE, we establish a sufficient condition for the global convergence of ITMP in the large system limit. The tightness of the sufficient condition is supported by the observation that the phase transition curve specified by the sufficient condition closely matches that of ITMP in various numerical simulations. We further establish a necessary condition for the global convergence, corresponding to a lower bound of the phase transition curve. These upper and lower bounds contribute to a more comprehensive understanding of the performance of ITMP in the considered asymptotic regime.

I-D

Organizations and Notations

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
In Section
II
, we present the CRPCA problem. We derive the overall message passing algorithm based on sum-product rule and approximate the message as a denoising problem. In Section
III
, we characterize the asymptotic behavior for the three generic denoisers and develop the state evolution of the algorithm. In Section
IV
, we present the general convergence analysis of the algorithm by tracking the dynamic system given by SE. In Section
V
, we present the SE and the global convergence for certain settings of the algorithm and provide numerical experiments.

Throughout this paper, we use bold lower-case and upper case letters to denote vectors and matrices respectively. The nomenclature illustrates the notations used in this paper.

II
Improved Turbo Message Passing for CRPCA

II-A

System Model

In this paper, we focus on special families of linear operators defined below.
It is well known that the generation model of the linear operator
\mathcal{A}
affects the reconstruction of the structured signals
[
71
,
54
,
67
,
65
,
58
]
. The linear operator
\mathcal{A}
is commonly assumed to sample the signal “uniformly” enough. For example, in
[
42
]
, the rows of
\boldsymbol{A}
(i.e., the matrix form of
\mathcal{A}
) are assumed to follow the Haar measure, and in
[
44
]
,
\boldsymbol{A}
is considered to satisfy the restricted isometry property (RIP) and rank-restricted isometry property (RRIP).
In this paper, we focus on special families of linear operators defined below.

Definition 1

(Rotationally invariant matrix)
.

A random matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
is rotationally invariant if the distribution of
\boldsymbol{M}
is equal to that of
\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{U}
and
\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{M}
for any orthogonal matrix
\boldsymbol{U}
, independent of
\boldsymbol{M}
.

Definition 2

(ROIL)
.

A linear operator
\mathcal{A}
is a rotationally invariant linear (ROIL) operator if the matrix form
\boldsymbol{A}
of
\mathcal{A}
is rotationally invariant, where
\boldsymbol{A}
is given by
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{X})=\boldsymbol{A}\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{X})
.

Definition 3

(Haar (distributed) matrix)
.

The set of
n\times n
orthogonal matrices over
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is denoted by
\mathcal{O}{n}
. We say an
n\times n
random matrix
\mathbf{V}\in\mathcal{O}{n}
is Haar distributed on
\mathcal{O}{n}
if for any fixed
\mathbf{U}\in\mathcal{O}{n}
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}\overset{p}{=}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{V}\overset{p}{=}\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{U}.

(4)

We also refer to such a matrix
\mathbf{V}
as the Haar matrix in this paper.

Consider the singular value decomposition (
\operatorname{SVD}
) of
\boldsymbol{M}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{M}=\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{M}}\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{M}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{M}}^{T}

(5)

where
\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{M}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
is a diagonal matrix,
\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{M}}\in\mathcal{O}{m}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{M}}\in\mathcal{O}{n}
.
\boldsymbol{M}
is a rotationally invariant matrix, if and only if
\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{M}}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{M}}
are Haar matrices independent of each other and independent of
\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{M}}
.

We say that a linear operator
\mathcal{A}
is a partial Haar operator if the matrix form
\boldsymbol{A}
is given by
\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{U}_{s}\boldsymbol{V}
, where
\boldsymbol{U}_{s}
consists of uniformly and randomly selected rows of the identity matrix and
\boldsymbol{V}
is a Haar matrix. (When constructing the matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{s}
, rows are chosen without repetition. The partial Haar operator requires selecting rows from the identity matrix while ensuring that each row vector are orthogonal.) We say that a linear operator
\mathcal{A}
is a Gaussian operator if the entries of the matrix form
\boldsymbol{A}
are
i.i.d.
Gaussian with zero mean and
\frac{1}{n}
variance. Clearly, both partial Haar operators and Gaussian operators belong to the family of ROIL operators. In the following, we always assume that
\mathcal{A}
is a ROIL operator, unless otherwise specified.

II-B

Improved Turbo Message Passing Algorithm

We take a Bayesian approach to tackle the CRPCA problem. We first introduce a probability model for the variables in (
3
). Assume that

\boldsymbol{L}
,
\boldsymbol{S}
, and
\boldsymbol{n}
are independent of each other. Denote by
p(\boldsymbol{L})
and
p(\boldsymbol{S})
the prior distributions of
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
, respectively. The joint distribution of
\boldsymbol{y}
,
\boldsymbol{X}
,
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{L})=p(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{X})p(\boldsymbol{X}|\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{S})p(\boldsymbol{S})p(\boldsymbol{L})

(6)

where
p(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{X})=\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{y}-\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{X});\boldsymbol{0},\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\right)
, and
p(\boldsymbol{X}|\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{S})=\delta(\boldsymbol{X}-\boldsymbol{S}-\boldsymbol{L})
with
\delta(\cdot)
being the Dirac delta function. Here,
\boldsymbol{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is a rank-
r
matrix with
r\ll n_{1},n_{2}
,
\boldsymbol{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is a sparse matrix with sparsity level
\rho
(i.e., a fraction
\rho
of the elements of
\boldsymbol{S}
are nonzero),
and
\boldsymbol{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times 1}
is an additive white Gaussian noise with mean zero and variance
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{I}}
.

Computing the maximum a posteriori probability (MAP) estimate or minimum mean square error (MMSE) estimate based on (
6
) is usually intractable for high dimensional problems. In this paper, we obtain a message passing algorithm following the sum-product rule together with necessary approximations.

We start with a factor graph representation of the considered probability model. Recall that
p(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{X})
is a Gaussian distribution with mean
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{X})
and covariance
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}\boldsymbol{I}
, and

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{X}|\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{L})=\displaystyle\prod_{i,j}\delta(X_{i,j}-S_{i,j}-L_{i,j}),

(7)

where
X_{i,j}
,
S_{i,j}
and
L_{i,j}
are respectively the
(i,j)
-th elements of
\boldsymbol{X}
,
\boldsymbol{S}
, and
\boldsymbol{L}
. Then, (2) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{L})=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{y};\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{X}),\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\right)p(\boldsymbol{S})p(\boldsymbol{L})\displaystyle\prod_{i,j}\delta(X_{i,j}-S_{i,j}-L_{i,j}).

(8)

Fig. 1:
The factor graph representation of (
8
)

The factor graph representation of (
8
) is shown in Fig.
1
, where each white circle represents a variable node, and each black square represents a factor node. For notational convenience, we abbreviate
\delta(X_{i,j}-L_{i,j}-S_{i,j})
to
\delta_{i,j}
and
p(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{X})
to
\boldsymbol{y}
in situations without causing ambiguity. In addition, we denote by
\delta
the collection of all
{\delta_{i,j}}
. Denote by
m_{a\to b}^{(t)}(x)
the message from node
a
to node
b
with respect to variable
x
in the
t
-th iteration, where
x_{a\to b}^{(t)}
and
v_{a\to b}^{(t)}
are the corresponding mean and variance respectively, and by
m_{a}^{(t)}(x)
the message of node
a
with respect to variable
x
in the
t
-th iteration. We now describe the proposed algorithm based on the sum-product rule.

We observe the symmetry of the nodes
p(\boldsymbol{S})
and
p(\boldsymbol{L})
in the factor graph. Below, we provide derivation of the messages associated with
\boldsymbol{S}
. By replacing
\boldsymbol{S}
in the derived messages related to
\boldsymbol{S}
, we obtain the corresponding messages associated with
p(\boldsymbol{L})
node.

II-B
1
Messages associated with
\boldsymbol{S}
and
\boldsymbol{L}

The message from variable node
S_{i,j}
to factor node
p(\boldsymbol{S})
and the message from variable node
S_{i,j}
to factor node
\delta_{i,j}
are respectively given by

\displaystyle m^{(t)}_{S_{i,j}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}

\displaystyle=m^{(t)}_{\delta_{i,j}\to S_{i,j}}

(9a)

\displaystyle m^{(t)}_{S_{i,j}\to\delta_{i,j}}

\displaystyle=m^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to S_{i,j}}.

(9b)

The messages
m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}
and
m^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}
are respectively defined as

\displaystyle m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\prod_{i,j}m^{(t)}_{S_{i,j}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}

(10a)

\displaystyle m^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\prod_{i,j}m^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to S_{i,j}},

(10b)

where

\displaystyle m^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to S_{i,j}}=\frac{\displaystyle\int m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}p(\boldsymbol{S})\,d\boldsymbol{S}_{/(i,j)}}{m_{S_{i,j}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}}

(11)

with

\displaystyle m^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})}=\displaystyle\int m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}p(\boldsymbol{S})\,d\boldsymbol{S}_{/(i,j)}.

(12)

Clearly,
m^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}
cannot be evaluated when
p(\boldsymbol{S})
is unavailable (which is usually true in practice). Even if
p(\boldsymbol{S})
is available, the evaluation of
m_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
is still difficult since it involves high-dimensional integrals. Considering the symmetry of the nodes
p(\boldsymbol{S})
and
p(\boldsymbol{L})
in the factor graph, we can obtain the messages associated with
\boldsymbol{L}
by just replacing
\boldsymbol{S}
with
\boldsymbol{L}
in (
9
)-(
12
).
Again,
m_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
is difficult to evaluate since it involves
p(\boldsymbol{L})
(which is unavailable in practice) and the high-dimensional integral.

To avoid the above difficulties, we use the mean and variance for message passing, following the idea of approximate message passing
[
57
]
, expectation propagation
[
78
]
and mean field
[
79
]
. Inspires by the construction of the
extrinsic message
, which have been developed in
[
66
,
67
,
68
,
69
,
54
]
, we approximately evaluate the mean and variance of the message.

The idea of exchanging extrinsic messages between components was first introduced in decoding turbo-codes
[
80
]
.
The output message by one component becomes the prior message for the other, and this process iterates multiple times. The output of each component decoder should be the so-called ’extrinsic message’, which is obtained by excluding the prior message of a component decoder from the corresponding posterior message.

Inspired by the exchange of extrinsic messages in turbo decoding, the idea of exchanging extrinsic messages has been applied to message-passing algorithms
[
66
,
81
]
that solve sparse linear inverse problems. For example, denoising-based Turbo-CS
[
68
]
was proposed to replace the MMSE denoiser in
[
66
]
with a generic denoiser, even without prior knowledge of the signal distribution. Inspired by Gaussian message passing, the authors in
[
66
]
extend the concept of independence to uncorrelatedness to accommodate a broader range of posterior denoisers. Additionally, the mean of the extrinsic message is constructed through a linear combination of the
a priori
mean and the
a posteriori
mean.
This formulation has also been applied to construct message passing algorithms in various signal reconstruction problems
[
69
,
54
]
.

Let
\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}
and
v_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}
(
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}
and
v_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}
) be the mean and variance of message
m_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}
(and
m_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}
), respectively. We next describe how to approximately evaluate the mean and variance of
m_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to S_{i,j}}^{(t)}
(denoted respectively by
\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to S_{i,j}}
and
v^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to S_{i,j}}
).
To start with, we make the following assumptions on
\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}
and
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}
.

Assumption 1
.

For each iteration
t
, the elements of the estimation error matrix
\boldsymbol{N}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}-\boldsymbol{S}
are independently drawn from a common distribution
\mathcal{Q}_{N_{\boldsymbol{S}}}^{(t)}(N_{\boldsymbol{S}})
with mean zero and variance
v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}
.

Assumption 2
.

For each iteration
t
, the elements of the estimation error matrix
\boldsymbol{N}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}-\boldsymbol{L}
are independently drawn from a common distribution
\mathcal{Q}^{(t)}_{N_{\boldsymbol{L}}}(N_{\boldsymbol{L}})
with zero mean and variance
v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}
.

The above assumption is supported by some empirical evidences presented in the next section. Similar assumptions have been previously used in the development of efficient iterative algorithms; see, e.g.,
[
66
]
and
[
69
]
.
This assumption is useful in approximate calculation of
\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}
and
\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}
.
In the following, we will only describe how to approximate the messages related to
\boldsymbol{S}
; a similar method can be applied to
\boldsymbol{L}
. Without necessarily knowing the exact expression of
p(\boldsymbol{S})
, we generally approximate the mean of
m_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}=\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})};v_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right)

(13)

where
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}
is a denoising function that suppresses the noise in the input by exploiting certain prior knowledge of
\boldsymbol{S}
(such as the sparsity of
\boldsymbol{S}
, but not necessarily the complete knowledge of
p(\boldsymbol{S})
).

Then, the mean of
m^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}
is constructed by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}=c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\left(\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}-a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right)

(14)

where
a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
are scalar parameters. Note that
\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
in (
14
) is also referred to as “extrinsic mean” since the linear combination of
\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}
and
\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}
in (
14
) can be interpreted as excluding the contribution of the “prior mean”
\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}
from the “posterior mean”
\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}

[
69
,
54
,
68
]
. The parameters
a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
are determined by solving the following optimization problem
[
54
]
:

\displaystyle\min_{a^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}},c^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}}\mathbb{E}\lVert\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}-\boldsymbol{S}\rVert^{2}_{F}

(15a)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r@{\quad}r@{}l@{\quad}l}{\rm s.t.}&\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}-\boldsymbol{S}\right\rangle=0\end{array}.

where the expectations are taken over the joint distribution of
\boldsymbol{S}
and
\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}
, i.e.,
p(\boldsymbol{S})\prod_{i,j}\mathcal{Q}_{N_{\boldsymbol{S}}}^{(t)}\left((\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})})_{i,j}-S_{i,j}\right)
from Assumption
1
. In the above, (
15
b) ensures that the output estimation error is statistically orthogonal to the input estimation error, and (
15
a) ensures that the output mean square error (MSE) is minimized. By solving (
15
), we obtain
a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
as

\displaystyle a^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}\right\rangle}{\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right\rangle}

(16a)

\displaystyle c^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left(\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}-a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right),\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right\rangle}{\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}-a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}}.

(16b)

Correspondingly, the variance of message
m^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}
is given by

\displaystyle v_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}-c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right\rangle+c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}+v_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right).

(17)

The detailed derivation of (
17
) can be found in Appendix
A-B
.
When
p(\boldsymbol{S})
and
\mathcal{Q}_{N_{\boldsymbol{S}}}^{(t)}(N_{\boldsymbol{S}})
are unavailable, the above expectations are computationally intractable. In Section
II-C
, we use statistical signal processing techniques to approximately calculate (
16
) and (
17
) without requiring the full knowledge of
p(\boldsymbol{S})
and
\mathcal{Q}_{N_{\boldsymbol{S}}}^{(t)}(N_{\boldsymbol{S}})
. Note that
v_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
can be obtained from (
17
) by replacing
\boldsymbol{S}
by
\boldsymbol{L}
. But for
the convenience of practical calculation, we express the variance
v^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}
equivalently as

\displaystyle v_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{L}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|_{F}^{2}=\frac{\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left\|\!\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}\!\right\|_{F}^{2}\!\left\|\!\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}\!\right\|_{F}^{2}\!\!-\!\left\langle\!\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})},\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}\!\right\rangle^{2}\!\right]}{n\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}-a_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]}-\!v_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}.

(18)

The detailed derivation of (
18
) is given in Appendix
A-C
. Similarly to (
16
) and (
17
), the expectations in (
18
) depend on
p(\boldsymbol{L})
and
\mathcal{Q}^{(t)}_{N_{\boldsymbol{L}}}(N_{\boldsymbol{L}})
which are usually unknown in practice. We describe how to approximately evaluate
a_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
,
c_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
and
v_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
without requiring exact knowledge of
p(\boldsymbol{L})
and
\mathcal{Q}^{(t)}_{N_{\boldsymbol{L}}}(N_{\boldsymbol{L}})
in Section
II-D
.

II-B
2
Messages associated with check node
\delta

Based on the sum-product rule, the message from
\delta
to
\boldsymbol{X}
is given by

\displaystyle m^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{X}}=\int\displaystyle\prod_{i,j}\delta(X_{i,j}-S_{i,j}-L_{i,j})m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to\delta}m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta}\,d\boldsymbol{L}\,d\boldsymbol{S}.

(19)

Then, the mean and variance of message
m^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{X}}
are respectively given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{X}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to\delta}+\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta},\quad v^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{X}}

\displaystyle=v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to\delta}+v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta}.

(20)

Based on the sum-product rule, similar to (
19
) and (
20
), the mean and variance of message
m^{(t+1)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}
are respectively given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}^{(t+1)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{X}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\delta}-\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta},\quad v^{(t+1)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}

\displaystyle=v^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\delta}+v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta}.

(21)

and
the mean and variance of message
m^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta}
are respectively given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t+1)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{X}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\delta}-\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to\delta},\quad v^{(t+1)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}

\displaystyle=v^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\delta}+v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to\delta}.

(22)

II-B
3
Messages associated with
\boldsymbol{X}

The message from variable node
X_{i,j}
to factor node
p(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{X})
and the message from factor node
p(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{X})
to variable node
X_{i,j}
are respectively given by

\displaystyle m^{(t)}_{\delta\to X_{i,j}}

\displaystyle=m^{(t)}_{X_{i,j}\to\boldsymbol{y}},\quad m^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to X_{i,j}}

\displaystyle=m^{(t+1)}_{X_{i,j}\to\delta}.

(23)

Recall
p(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{X})=\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{y};\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{X}),\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\right)
. Then, message
m^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to X_{i,j}}
is given by

\displaystyle m^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to X_{i,j}}=\frac{\displaystyle\int\displaystyle\prod_{k,l}m_{X_{k,l}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{y};\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{X}),\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\right)\,d\boldsymbol{X}_{/(i,j)}}{m_{X_{i,j}^{(t)}\to\boldsymbol{y}}}

(24)

with

\displaystyle m^{(t)}_{X_{i,j}}=\int\displaystyle\prod_{k,l}m_{X_{k,l}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{y};\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{X}),\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\right)\,d\boldsymbol{X}_{/(i,j)}.

(25)

For notational convenience, define the compound messages between factor node
p(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{X})
and
\boldsymbol{X}
as

\displaystyle m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\prod_{i,j}m^{(t)}_{X_{i,j}\to\boldsymbol{y}},\quad m^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\prod_{i,j}m^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to X_{i,j}}.

(26)

The compound message
m_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
is defined as

\displaystyle m_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}=\displaystyle\prod_{i,j}m^{(t)}_{X_{i,j}}.

(27)

From (
25
), (
26
) and (
27
), we obtain

\displaystyle m^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}=\frac{m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}}}{m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}}.

(28)

The high-dimensional integrals in (
24
) can be solved explicitly by assuming
m_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}=\mathcal{N}\left(vec(\boldsymbol{X});vec(\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{X}}),v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{I}\right)
. This is referred to as the LMMSE approach in
[
54
]
and the resultant message-update expressions can be found therein. Here we introduce a denoising-based approach similar to the treatment for approximating the messages associated with
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
. The denoising-based approach presented here is a generalization of the LMMSE approach. More importantly, we show that, with the denoising conditions satisfied, Assumptions
1
and
2
are self-consistent in the iterative process.

Denote by
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{X}}
the denoiser of matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
. Then, the mean of message
m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}}
is approximated by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}=\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}};v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\right)

(29)

where
\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}
and
v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}
are the mean and variance of message
m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}
. Then, the mean of
m^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}
is given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}=c_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}-a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\right)

(30)

where
a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
are scalar parameters satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}-\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}-\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle=0

(31a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}-\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle=0.

(31b)

The constraint in (
31
a) is similar to the conditions for sparse denoiser (see (
15
b)) and low-rank denoiser (see (
16
b)) to ensure the uncorrelation between the prior message and the extrinsic message. The constraint (
31
b), which is introduced in
[
67
]
, ensures the uncorrelation between the extrinsic message and the signal to be recovered.
Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, the expectations in (
31
) are taken over the joint distribution
p(\boldsymbol{L})p(\boldsymbol{S})p(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to\delta}^{(t)}|\boldsymbol{L})p(\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta}^{(t)}|\boldsymbol{S})\delta(\boldsymbol{X}-\boldsymbol{S}-\boldsymbol{L})p(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{X})
. We see that (
31
a), similar to (
15
b), requires that the output error is statistically orthogonal to the input error. More interestingly, (
31
b) requires that the output error is statistically orthogonal to
\boldsymbol{X}
. The conditions in (
31
) are important to ensure that Assumptions
1
and
2
are self-consistent in the iterative process. With (
31
) and (
32
), we obtain

\displaystyle a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}\!=\!\frac{\!\mathbb{E}\!\left\|\boldsymbol{X}\right\|_{F}^{2}\!\mathbb{E}\left\langle\!\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}\!,\!\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}\!\right\rangle\!-\!\mathbb{E}\left\langle\!\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}\!,\!\boldsymbol{X}\!\right\rangle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\!\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}\!,\!\boldsymbol{X}\!\right\rangle}{nv^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{X}\right\|_{F}^{2}\!-\!\left(\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{X}\right\|_{F}^{2}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}},\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle\right)^{2}}

(32a)

\displaystyle c_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}\!=\!\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle-a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}},\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle}{\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{X}\right\|_{F}^{2}}\right)^{-1}.

(32b)

Correspondingly, the variance of message
m^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}
is given by

\displaystyle v_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{X}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}-\boldsymbol{X}\right\|_{F}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|c_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}\!\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}\!-\!a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}\right)\right\|_{F}^{2}\!-\!\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{X}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

(33)

II-B
4
Message from
\delta
to
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}

We now present the main results of this subsection.

Lemma 1
.

Suppose that Assumptions 1 and 2 hold, and that
\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
and
\boldsymbol{L}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
are the solutions of (
15
) Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}-\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle=-v^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{X}},

(34)

where
v^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{X}}
is defined by (
20
b). Further assume that
\boldsymbol{X}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}
satisfies (
31
) and that
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}};v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\right)
is a linear estimator of
\boldsymbol{X}
given
\boldsymbol{y}
as

\displaystyle\operatorname{vec}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}};v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\right)\right)=\operatorname{vec}\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}\right)+\boldsymbol{M}^{(t)}\left(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{A}\operatorname{vec}\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}\right)\right)

(35)

where
\boldsymbol{M}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
is a deterministic combinating matrix usually depending on
v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}\right\rangle=0,\quad\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{L}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}-\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{L}\right\rangle=0.

(36)

The expectations in (
34
) and (
36
) are taken over

\displaystyle p\left(\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})},\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right)=p(\boldsymbol{L})p(\boldsymbol{S})\prod_{i,j}\mathcal{Q}^{(t)}_{N_{\boldsymbol{S}}}\left((\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})})_{i,j}-S_{i,j}\right)\prod_{i,j}\mathcal{Q}^{(t)}_{N_{\boldsymbol{L}}}\left((\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})})_{i,j}-L_{i,j}\right).

(37)

Proof.

See Appendix
A-D

∎

Remark 1
.

The linear estimator in the form of (
35
) is widely used in various iterative linear inversion algorithms (e.g. AMP
[
71
]
, TMP
[
66
,
68
,
54
]
, OAMP
[
67
]
and VAMP
[
70
]
). In the next subsection, we discuss how to calculate
a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
,
c_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
, and
v_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
in (
32
) and (
33
) for different choices of the combining matrix
\boldsymbol{M}^{(t)}
.

II-C

Details for Sparse Denoiser
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}

In this section, we discuss the choice of the denoiser for matrix
\boldsymbol{S}
.
With knowing the prior distribution of the
i.i.d.
signal
\boldsymbol{S}
, we can apply MMSE denoiser that is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(\left(S^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right)_{i,j}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(S_{i,j}\Big|\left(S^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right)_{i,j}\right)

\displaystyle\forall i,j

(38)

where the expectation is taken over the joint distribution
p\left(S_{i,j},\left(S^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right)_{i,j}\right)
. We have the following result.

Lemma 2

(MMSE denoiser for
\boldsymbol{S}
)
.

Assume that message
m_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}
satisfies Assumption
1
, and that
\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
in (
15
) is the solution of (
14
). Then,

\displaystyle a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{v_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}{v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}}

(39a)

\displaystyle c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}}{v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}-v_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}

(39b)

where
v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}
is the variance of
m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}
, and
v_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
is the variance of
m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}
. Then, the variance of
m^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}
is given by

\displaystyle v^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{v_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}-\frac{1}{v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}}\right)^{-1}.

(40)

Proof.

See Appendix
A-E
.
∎

Remark 2
.

From Lemma
2
and (
14
), the mean of message
m^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}
is given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}=v^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}}{v_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}-\frac{\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}}{v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}}\right).

(41)

The mean in (
41
) and variance in (
40
) are referred as the extrinsic message of the sparsity denoiser in
[
66
]
(that is derived in a different way). This imply that the design of the sparsity denoiser based on (
14
) and (
15
) is indeed a generalization of the previous approach in
[
66
]
.

We note that the soft-thresholding denoiser could also be applied to recover the sparse signal without requiring the exact prior distribution. The parameters
a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
in (
16
) can be obtained by invoking Stein’s lemma as in
[
54
]
.

II-D

Details for Low-rank Denoiser
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}

In this section, we discuss the choice of the denoiser for matrix
\boldsymbol{L}
.
Singular value soft-thresholding (SVST), singular value hard-thresholding (SVHT) and best Rank-
r
are commonly used spectral denoisers to recover low-rank matrix from noisy observation.

Let the SVD of
\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}
be
\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}=\boldsymbol{U}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{\left(V^{(t)}\right)}^{T}
, where
\boldsymbol{U}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{1}}
and
\boldsymbol{V}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}\times n_{2}}
are orthogonal matrices, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(t)}=\operatorname{diag}\left\{\sigma_{1}^{(t)},\sigma_{2}^{(t)},\cdots,\sigma^{(t)}_{\min\left(n_{1},n_{2}\right)}\right\}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is a non-negative diagonal matrix with the diagonal elements arranged in the descending order.

Let
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{(t)}=\frac{\sigma_{i}^{(t)}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}}}
. The SVST denoiser has a closed-form expression given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})};\omega\right)=\sqrt{n_{2}v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}}\sum_{i=1}^{\min\left(n_{1},n_{2}\right)}\!\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{(t)}-\!\omega\right)_{+}\!\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{(t)}\!\left(\!\boldsymbol{v}_{i}^{(t)}\!\right)^{T}

(42)

where
\omega
is a predetermined threshold,
\boldsymbol{u}^{(t)}_{i}
is the
i
-th column of
\boldsymbol{U}^{(t)}
, and
\boldsymbol{v}^{(t)}_{i}
is the
i
-th column of
\boldsymbol{V}^{(t)}
. The SVST denoiser applies soft-thresholding denoising to the singular values of the input matrix.

The best rank-
r
denoiser is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{L}\left(\!\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})};r\!\right)=\boldsymbol{U}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}^{(t)}\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{(t)}\right)^{T}

(43)

where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(t)}_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is a diagonal matrix with the first
r
diagonal elements being
\sigma^{(t)}_{1},\sigma^{(t)}_{2},\cdots,\sigma^{(t)}_{r}
and the others being 0. The parameter
r
of the best rank-
r
denoiser is assumed to be known by the algorithm. Otherwise, the rank-contraction method can be used to estimate the value of
r
.

We need to further compute the extrinsic message of module
\boldsymbol{L}
. To this end, we need to calculate the scalar
a_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
as defined in (
16
).

\displaystyle a_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}-\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}\right\rangle}{\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}-\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}-\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}\right\rangle}{nv^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}}

(44a)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{div}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}\right)\right)

(44b)

where (
44
a) follows from Assumption
2
and (
44
b) follows from the Stein’s lemma
[
82
,
54
]
under Gaussian noise. We will show that the approximation in (
44
b) is asymptotically accurate for the SVST and best rank-
r
denoisers even under i.i.d. noise in subsection
III-C
and Lemma
9
. Furthermore,
c_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
in (
16
b) and
v_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
in (
18
) are approximated by dropping the expectation and substituting the approximated
a_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
in (
44
b).
The divergence of singular value soft-thresholding (SVST) and best Rank-
r
are respectively given by
[
72
]
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{div}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\!\!\left(\!\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})};\omega\!\right)\right)=\frac{\sqrt{\!n_{2}v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}\!}}{n}\left(\!\left|n_{1}\!-\!n_{2}\right|\!\!\!\sum_{i=1}^{\min\left(n_{1}n_{2}\right)}\!\!\left(1-\frac{\omega}{\tilde{\sigma}^{(t)}_{i}}\right)_{+}\!\!+\!\!\sum_{i=1}^{\min\left(n_{1}n_{2}\right)}\!\!\!\!\mathbb{I}\left(\!\tilde{\sigma}^{(t)}_{i}\!>\!\omega\!\right)\!+\!2\!\!\!\!\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}\!\!\frac{\tilde{\sigma}^{(t)}_{i}\!\left(\!\tilde{\sigma}^{(t)}_{i}\!-\!\omega\right)_{+}}{\left(\!\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{(t)}\!\right)^{2}\!-\!\left(\!\tilde{\sigma}_{j}^{(t)}\!\right)^{2}}\right)

(45)

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{div}\left(\mathcal{D}_{L}\left(\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})};r\right)\right)=\frac{\sqrt{n_{2}v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}}}{n}\left(\left|n_{1}\!-\!n_{2}\right|r\!+r^{2}\!+\!2\sum_{i=1}^{r}\!\sum_{j=r+1}^{min(n_{1},n_{2})}\!\frac{\left(\tilde{\sigma}^{(t)}_{i}\right)^{2}}{\left(\tilde{\sigma}^{(t)}_{i}\right)^{2}-\left(\tilde{\sigma}^{(t)}_{j}\right)^{2}}\right),

(46)

provided that
\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}
has
min(n_{1},n_{2})
distinct, positive singular values with probability
1
.

II-E

Details for Linear Denoiser
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{X}}

In this section, we describe use linear denoiser to estimate matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
given the noisy observation
\boldsymbol{y}
in (
3
). We first rewrite
\boldsymbol{y}
as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{A}\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{X})+\boldsymbol{n}

(47)

where
\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{X})=\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{1}^{T},\boldsymbol{x}_{2}^{T},\cdots,\boldsymbol{x}_{n_{2}}^{T}\right]^{T}
with
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}
being the
i
-th column of
\boldsymbol{X}
, and
\boldsymbol{A}
is the matrix form of linear operator
\mathcal{A}
.

Combining the message
m^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}
and (
47
), we obtain the LMMSE estimator of
\boldsymbol{X}
given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{vec}\left(\!\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}\!\right)\!=

\displaystyle\operatorname{vec}\left(\!\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}\!\right)\!+\!v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}\left(\!v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}\!+\!\sigma^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}\boldsymbol{I}\!\right)^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{A}\operatorname{vec}\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}\right)\right).

(48)

We have the following results.

Lemma 3

(Parameters for LMMSE denoiser)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{A}
is a ROIL operator.
a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
for the LMMSE denoiser are respectively given by

\displaystyle a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{v_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}}{v_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}}

(49a)

\displaystyle c_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{v_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}}{v_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}-v_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}},

(49b)

where
v_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
is given by

\displaystyle v_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}=v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}-\frac{\left(v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\right)^{2}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{i,\boldsymbol{A}}}{v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}\sigma^{2}_{i,\boldsymbol{A}}+\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}}.

(50)

Here,
\sigma_{i,\boldsymbol{A}}
is the
i
-th singular value of
\boldsymbol{A}
.
And

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{X}}

\displaystyle=v^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{X}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}}{v_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}}-\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}}{v_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}}\right)

(51)

with

\displaystyle v_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{i,\boldsymbol{A}}}{v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}\sigma^{2}_{i,\boldsymbol{A}}+\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}}}-v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}

(52)

where
\sigma_{i,\boldsymbol{A}}
is the
i
-th singular value of
\boldsymbol{A}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
A-F

∎

Corollary 1
.

Assume that
\mathcal{A}
is a partial Haar operator. Then

\displaystyle v_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}=\frac{n-m}{m}v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}+\frac{n}{m}\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}

(53a)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{X}}=\operatorname{vec}\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}\right)+\frac{n}{m}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}\left(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{A}\operatorname{vec}\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}\right)\right).

(53b)

Proof.

See Appendix
A-G

∎

II-F

Overall Algorithm

Algorithm 1

Improved Turbo message passing (ITMP) algorithm

0:

\mathcal{A}
,
\boldsymbol{y}
,
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}
,
t=0
,
m_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow\delta}^{(0)}\!=\!\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{L};\mathbf{0},\sigma^{2}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\boldsymbol{I}})
,
m_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow\delta}^{(0)}\!=\!\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{S};\mathbf{0},\sigma^{2}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\boldsymbol{I}})

1:

while
the stopping criterion is not met
do

2:

\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\!=\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t)}+\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t)}
,
v_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\!=\!v_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t)}+v_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t)}

3:

\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}=\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}},v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\right)\%\emph{lineardenoiser}

4:

Update
a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
by (
32
)

5:

\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t+1)}=c_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}-a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\right)

6:

Update
v_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t+1)}
by (
33
)

7:

\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t+1)}=\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t+1)}-\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t)}
,
v_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t+1)}=v_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t+1)}+v_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t)}

8:

\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t+1)}=\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t+1)}-\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t)}
,
v_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t+1)}=v_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t+1)}+v_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t)}

9:

t=t+1

10:

\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}\!=\!\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})},v_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right)\%\emph{sparsedenoiser}

11:

Update
a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
by (
16
)

12:

\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t)}=c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\left(\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}-a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right)

13:

Update
v_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t)}
by (
17
)

14:

\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}\!=\!\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})},v_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}\right)\%\emph{low-rankdenoiser}

15:

Update
a_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
by (
16
)

16:

\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t)}=\!c_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\left(\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}-a_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}\right)

17:

Update
v_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow\delta}^{(t)}=v_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
by (
18
)

18:

end

while

18:

\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}
,
\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}

We summarize the overall improved turbo message passing algorithm in Algorithm
1
. The algorithm mainly consists of three parts, corresponding to the linear denoiser, the sparsity denoiser and the low-rank denoiser, as illustrated in Fig.
1
. We next briefly explain Algorithm
1
in a line-by-line manner.

We initialize
m_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow\delta}^{(0)}
and
m_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow\delta}^{(0)}
with
\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{L};\mathbf{0},v_{\boldsymbol{L}}\boldsymbol{I}})
and
\mathcal{N}({\boldsymbol{S};\mathbf{0},v_{\boldsymbol{S}}\boldsymbol{I}})
, where
v_{\boldsymbol{L}}
and
v_{\boldsymbol{S}}
are respectively the normalized powers of
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
. In lines 2, we calculate the message from
\boldsymbol{X}
to
\boldsymbol{y}
(which is equal to the message from
\delta
to
\boldsymbol{X}
). In line 3,
\boldsymbol{X}
is estimated by combining the updated message from
\delta
and the knowledge of
\boldsymbol{y}
. The linear denoiser
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{X}}
has a variety of choices, such as the LMMSE denoiser described in the preceding subsection. In lines 4 and 6, the calculations methods of
a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
,
c_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
and
v_{\boldsymbol{y}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}
are given in Lemma
3
and its corollaries.
In lines 7-8, the message from
\delta
to
\boldsymbol{L}
and the message from
\delta
to
\boldsymbol{S}
are updated based on the sum-product rule. In line 10-13, the messages associated with the sparsity denoiser are updated.
For the sparsity denoiser
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}
in line 13, we choose the point-wise MMSE denoiser when the elements of
\boldsymbol{S}
are independently and identically drawn from a known distribution; we choose the soft-thresholding denoiser when the prior distribution of
\boldsymbol{S}
is unknown. In lines 11 and 13, the updating methods of
a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
,
c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
, and
v_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
can be found in Subsection
II-C
. In lines 14-17, the messages associated with the low-rank denoiser are updated.
For the low-rank denoiser in line 14, we choose the best-rank-
r
denoiser when the rank of
\boldsymbol{L}
is known, and choose the singular-value soft-thresholding otherwise. In lines 15 and 17, the updating methods of
a_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
,
c_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
and
v_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
can be found in Subsection
II-D
.

III
Asymptotic Performance Analysis

III-A

Preliminaries

The dynamic processes for message-passing algorithms
[
71
,
65
]
can be tracked by scalar functions that delineate the progression of variances, commonly known as state evolution. The state evolution is proved to be exact asymptotically in the large system when the non-linear function is separable
[
71
]
. Moreover, the state evolution of AMP with non-separable functions is also successful in many experimental observations. While, the corresponding theoretical proof is a challenging. In
[
65
]
, the authors generalize state evolution to non-separable non-linear functions under
i.i.d.
Gaussian sensing matrix. In
[
70
]
, the authors extend the Gaussian sensing matrix to the right-orthogonally invariant matrix and provide theoretical proofs to the corresponding analysis. While, some technical conditions should be met in their theory
[
70
]
that is not satisfied in our case. For this, instead of providing rigorous analysis of the overall algorithm, we present some assumptions for the state evolution.

Assumptions
1
and
2
allow for decoupling the probability spaces from iteration to iteration. Similar assumptions are also used in the prior work such as AMP
[
57
]
, EP
[
78
]
, and other message-passing algorithms
[
66
,
69
,
54
]
. With Assumptions
1
and
2
, we aim to establish the mean square error (MSE) transfer functions of the denoisers and analyze the convergence and the fixed point of the algorithm based on the derived transfer functions as
n\to\infty
with
\frac{m}{n}=\alpha\in(0,1]
,
\frac{n_{1}}{n_{2}}=\beta\in(0,1]
,
\frac{r}{n_{1}}=\gamma\in(0,1]
and

\rho\in(0,1]
. For the sake of simplicity, we henceforth abbreviate this limit process as “
n\to\infty
” or “
n_{1}\to\infty
” by ommiting the other conditions.

For ease of analysis, we introduce some definitions and regularity conditions for the three denoisers involved in the ITMP algorithm.

Definition 4

(Pseudo-Lipschitz
[
64
]
)
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
and any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is called pseudo-Lipschitz of order
k
if there exists a constant
L
such that for any
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\|f(\boldsymbol{x})\!-\!f(\boldsymbol{y})\|_{2}}{\sqrt{m}}\!\leq\!L\!\left(\!1\!+\!\left(\!\frac{\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\!\right)^{k\!-\!1}\!\!\!\!+\!\left(\!\frac{\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\!\right)^{k\!-\!1}\!\right)\frac{\|\boldsymbol{x}\!-\!\boldsymbol{y}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n}}.

(54)

L
is then called the pseudo-Lipschitz constant of
f
. Furthermore, denote by
\{L_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}}
the Lipschitz constants of a sequence of pseudo-Lipschitz functions
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}}
of order
k
. If
L_{n}<\infty
for each
n
and
\lim sup_{n\to\infty}L_{n}<\infty
, the sequence
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}}
is called uniformly pseudo-Lipachitz of order
k
. We call any
L>\lim sup_{n\to\infty}L_{n}<\infty
a pseudo-Lipschitz constant of the sequence.

For
k=1
, the above definition reduces to the standard definition of a Lipschitz function.

Definition 5

(Weak Convergence)
.

Let
\{p_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of probability measures on
(\mathbb{S},\mathbf{\Omega})
, where
\mathbb{S}
is a metric space and
\mathbf{\Omega}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathbb{S}
.
We say that a sequence of probability measure
\{p_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to a probability measure
p_{\infty}
and write
p_{n}\overset{w}{=}p_{\infty}
if

\displaystyle\int f(z)dp_{n}(z)\to\int f(z)dp_{\infty}(z)

(55)

for all continuous and bounded function
f:\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{R}
.

This article focuses solely on random variables whose domains are restricted to the real number field. Consequently, the sequence
\{p_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to a probability measure
p_{\infty}
if and only if it converges to the same probability measure
p_{\infty}
in distribution.

We first present the assumptions we make for our state evolution analysis.

Assumption 3
.

(3.i)

The entries of
\boldsymbol{S}
follow an i.i.d. distribution
p_{\boldsymbol{S}}(S)
with finite
2k
-th moment for some
k\geq 1
;

(3.ii)

\mathcal{Q}^{(t)}_{N_{\boldsymbol{S}}}(N_{\boldsymbol{S}})
in Assumption
1
has finite
2k
-th moment for some
k\geq 1
for each iteration;

(3.iii)

\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a separable denoiser and a pseudo-Lipschitz function of order
k
for each components. Here “separable” means that we evaluate the elements of
\boldsymbol{S}
separately by

\hat{S}^{(t)}_{i,j}=\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(S_{i,j}+\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j}\right)\forall i,j.
;

(3.iv)

\boldsymbol{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is rotationally invariant;

(3.v)

\mathcal{Q}^{(t)}_{N_{\boldsymbol{L}}}(N_{\boldsymbol{L}})
in Assumption
2
has finite
4
-th moment for each iteration. Let
\sigma_{i,\boldsymbol{L}}
be the
i
-th singular value of
\boldsymbol{L}
, and
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}_{n_{1},n_{2}}}(\sigma)
be the empirical distribution of
\{\frac{\sigma_{i,\boldsymbol{L}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\}_{i}^{n_{1}}
. Let
\frac{\sigma_{i,\boldsymbol{L}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}
be bounded. The empirical singular value distribution
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}_{n_{1},n_{2}}}(\sigma)
converges weakly to a deterministic distribution
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)

, and the
2
-nd moment of
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)
is finite;

(3.vi)

\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}:\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is a non-separable denoiser acting on the singular values of the noisy observation (e.g.
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N})=\sqrt{n_{2}v}\boldsymbol{U}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}})\boldsymbol{V}^{T}
), where
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right)
is a diagonal matrix with the
(i,i)
-th entry
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}})
. And
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}:[0,+\infty)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
is a monotonically increasing and continuously differentiable function, where
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(0)=0
and
f^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(0)=0
.
