(3.vii)

The empirical eigenvalue distribution of
\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}
converges weakly to a deterministic distribution
p(\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}})
with compact support
[\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T},\min},\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T},\max}]
, where
0<\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T},\min}<\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T},\max}<\infty
.

Assumption (
3
.i)-(
3
.iii) for the sparsity denoiser was previously introduced in
[
71
]
to analyze the asymptotic property for separable Lipschitz functions in AMP. Here, we characterize the asymptotic behavior of the parameters
a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
,
c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
and the extrinsic mean
\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}
based on this assumption.

(a) input error of module
\boldsymbol{X}

(b) input error of module
\boldsymbol{L}

(c) input error of module
\boldsymbol{S}

(d) output error of module
\boldsymbol{X}

(e) output error of module
\boldsymbol{L}

(f) output error of module
\boldsymbol{S}

Fig. 2:
The QQplots of the input and output error in the second iteration of the proposed algorithm. The first row are the QQplots of the estimation errors
input
each modules. The second row are the QQplots of the estimation errors
output
each modules. The entries of sparse matrix
\boldsymbol{S}
are independent drawn from the Gaussian-Bernoulli distribution with
\rho=0.1
and the low-rank matrix
\boldsymbol{L}
with rank-
r
. We use the point-wise MMSE denoiser to estimate
\boldsymbol{S}
and best-rank-
r
denoiser to estimate
\boldsymbol{L}
. We use the LMMSE denoiser to estimate
\boldsymbol{X}
. Other parameter settings are
n=n_{1}\times n_{2}=1000,\beta=1,\alpha=0.4,\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}=10^{-5},\gamma=0.05.

For low-rank matrix recovery, the existing work mostly focused on the estimation of a low-rank matrix given its noisy observation under an additive white Gaussian noise; see, e.g.,
[
74
,
72
,
83
]
.
The results therein are, however, inapplicable here since numerical experiments reveal that empirical distribution of the input error
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L}
of low-rank denoiser is not Gaussian; see, e.g., Fig.
2
. Therefore, we need to consider the situation that the entries of
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L}
are
i.i.d.
non-Gaussian. To facilitate the analysis, we restrict
\boldsymbol{L}
to satisfy (iv) and (v) of Assumption
3
.

Part (vi) of Assumption
3
gives some regularity conditions on the spectral denoiser.
More concretely, instead of giving a mapping
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
related with the matrix size
n_{2}
and
v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
, we calibrate
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
to
\frac{\boldsymbol{L}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}}}=\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}}}+\frac{\boldsymbol{N}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}
similarly to the expression in
[
74
]
. In this paper, we consider the most general form of an invariant estimator
2
2

2

We say that an estimator
D_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})
is orthogonally invariant if

\boldsymbol{U}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})\boldsymbol{V}=\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{L}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{V})

where
\boldsymbol{U}\in\mathcal{O}_{n_{1}}
,
\boldsymbol{V}\in\mathcal{O}_{n_{2}}
.
, which is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{L}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\right)=\sqrt{n_{2}v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}}\boldsymbol{U}^{(t)}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(t)}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}}}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{(t)}\right)^{T}.

(56)

where
\boldsymbol{U}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(t)}\left(\boldsymbol{V}^{(t)}\right)^{T}
be the SVD of
\boldsymbol{L}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
,
\boldsymbol{U}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{1}}
and
\boldsymbol{V}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}\times n_{2}}
are orthogonal matrices, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(t)}=\text{diag}\{\sigma_{1}^{(t)},\sigma_{2}^{(t)},\cdots,\sigma_{n_{1}}^{(t)}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is a diagonal matrix with the diagnal elements arranged in the descending order. Let
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{(t)}=\frac{\sigma_{i}^{(t)}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}}}
.
Thus, the invariant estimator is equivalent to a vector map
\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}
acting on the normalized singular value
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{(t)}
. Each normalized singular values
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{(t)}
are mapped by an identical scaling function
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
. And
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{(t)})
is the
(i,i)
-th entry of the diagonal matrix
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(t)}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}}}\right)
.
With the smoothing technique, the
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
assumed in Assumption (
3
.vi) can be made arbitrarily close to commonly used spectral denoisers, such as the best-rank-
r
denoiser, the singular value soft-thresholding denoiser, and the singular value hard-thresholding denoiser.

Assumption (
3
.vii) for linear denoiser was introduced to analyze the asymptotic behavior of the ROIL operator
\mathcal{A}
in
[
67
]
and
[
84
]
. Under Assumptions 1-3, we derive the transfer functions of the denoisers in the following three subsections.

III-B

Transfer Function of the Sparsity Denoiser

Denote by
\hat{v}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}
the empirical MSE of
\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}
(as an estimate of
\boldsymbol{S}
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

(57)

Similarly,
\hat{v}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
the empirical MSE of
\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
respectively by

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

(58)

Furthermore, denote the empirical values of
a^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}
and
c^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}
respectively by

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}\right\rangle}{\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right\rangle}

(59a)

\displaystyle\hat{c}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{\left\langle\left(\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}-\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right),\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right\rangle}{\left\lVert\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}-\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}}.

(59b)

As the system size approaches infinity, the randomness observed in the empirical Mean Squared Errors (MSEs) vanishes, and these MSEs converge towards constant values. As a result, the output empirical MSE of a denoiser can be expressed as a deterministic function of the input empirical MSE in the large system limit. This deterministic function is called the transfer function of the corresponding denoiser.

From Assumption
1
, we can model
\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=\boldsymbol{S}+\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}\boldsymbol{N}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}

(60)

where the elements of
\boldsymbol{N}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}
are
i.i.d.
drawn from the distribution
\mathcal{Q}^{(t)}_{N_{\boldsymbol{S}}}(N_{\boldsymbol{S}})
with zero mean and unit variance.

III-B
1
Asymptotic MSE of a general separable denoiser

Denote
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}
a general separable denoiser without knowing the exact distribution of
\boldsymbol{S}
and
\boldsymbol{N}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
. The asymptotic behaviors of separable denoisers under Gaussian noise have been studied in prior works
[
71
]
. In the following lemma, we present the asymptotics of a general separable denoiser under
i.i.d.
noise, which is not necessarily Gaussian.

Lemma 4

(Asymptotics for sparse denoiser)
.

Under Assumptions 1 and 3, as
n\to\infty
, the following holds:

1)

The empirical posteriori variance asymptotically satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right)-\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}\overset{p}{=}\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{S}}(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})

(61)

where

\displaystyle\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{S}}(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}(S+\sqrt{v^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}}N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}})-S\right)^{2}\right].

(62)

Here,
S
follows the distribution of
p_{\boldsymbol{S}}(S)
, and
N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}
is independently drawn from
\mathcal{Q}_{N_{\boldsymbol{S}}}^{(t)}(N_{\boldsymbol{S}})
.

2)

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\overset{p}{=}a_{\boldsymbol{S},\infty}^{(t)}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{c}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\overset{p}{=}c_{\boldsymbol{S},\infty}^{(t)}

(63)

where
\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
and
\hat{c}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
are defined in (
59a
), and

\displaystyle a_{\boldsymbol{S},\infty}^{(t)}=\frac{\mathbb{E}[\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(S+\sqrt{v^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}}N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right)]}{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}

(64a)

\displaystyle c_{\boldsymbol{S},\infty}^{(t)}=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\!\left(\!S\!-\!\frac{\mathbb{E}[S^{2}]}{\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}}N_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\!\right)\!\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\!\left(\!S\!+\!\sqrt{v^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}}N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\!\!\right)\!\right]}{\mathbb{E}\!\left[\!\left|\!\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\!\!\left(\!S\!\!+\!\!\sqrt{\!v^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}\!}N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\!\right)\!\!\!-\!a_{\boldsymbol{S},\infty}^{(t)}\!\!\left(\!\!S\!\!+\!\!\sqrt{\!v^{(\!t\!)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}\!}N^{(\!t\!)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\!\right)\!\right|^{2}\!\right]}.

(64b)

3)

The transfer function for sparsity denoiser is obtained by

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\overset{p}{=}\varphi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})

(65)

where

\displaystyle\varphi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})=\mathbb{(}1+c_{\boldsymbol{S},\infty}^{(t)}a_{\boldsymbol{S},\infty}^{(t)})\mathbb{E}[S^{2}]-c_{\boldsymbol{S},\infty}^{(t)}\mathbb{E}\left[S\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(S+\sqrt{v^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}}N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\right].

(66)

4)

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}-\boldsymbol{S}\right\rangle\overset{p}{=}0

(67a)

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}\right\rangle\overset{p}{=}-\varphi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})

(67b)

where the expectation
\mathbb{E}
is taken over the joint distribution
p_{\boldsymbol{S}}(S)\mathcal{Q}_{N_{\boldsymbol{S}}}(\boldsymbol{N}_{\boldsymbol{S}})
,
a_{\boldsymbol{S},\infty}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{S},\infty}^{(t)}
are respectively the large system limits of
a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
in (
16
).

Proof.

Proof see Appendix
B-A
.
∎

III-C

Transfer Function of the Low-Rank Denoiser

Following the discussions on to the sparse denoiser, the empirical MSEs of in the low-rank denoiser are given by

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|_{F}^{2},

(68a)

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{L}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

(68b)

The empirical values of
a_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
are given by

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{\left\langle\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}-\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}\right\rangle}{\left\langle\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}-\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}\right\rangle}

(69a)

\displaystyle\hat{c}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{\left\langle\left(\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}-\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}\right),\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}\right\rangle}{\left\lVert\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}-\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}}.

(69b)

From Assumption
2
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}}\boldsymbol{N}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}

(70)

where the entries of
\boldsymbol{N}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
are i.i.d. with zero mean and unit variance and
\boldsymbol{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is a matrix with rank
r
.
v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
is a finite positive real number.
In the following, we give the asymptotic analysis of the low-rank denoisers, as
n_{1},n_{2}\to\infty
with
\frac{n1}{n_{2}}=\beta
.

Lemma 5

(Asymptotics for low-rank denoiser)
.

Under Assumptions
2
and
3
, as
n_{1}\to\infty
, the following holds:

1)

The empirical posteriori variance asymptotically satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}\right)-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|_{F}^{2}\overset{p}{=}\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})

(71)

where
\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})
is given in the following

\displaystyle\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})=v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left(f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\!\sigma\!)-\!\sigma\!\right)^{2}\right]+2(1-\beta)\mathbb{E}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)\right]+2\beta\mathbb{E}_{\sigma,\hat{\sigma}}\left[H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma,\hat{\sigma})\right]-1\right).

(72)

Here,
g_{\boldsymbol{L}}
:
[0,+\infty)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
with
g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)=\frac{f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)}{x}
for
x>0
and
g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(0)=0
.
H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x,y)
is defined as

\displaystyle H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x,y)\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&(x,y)=(0,0)\\
\frac{xf_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{\prime}(x)+f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)}{2x}&x=y\neq 0\\
\frac{xf_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)-yf_{\boldsymbol{L}}(y)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}.&x\neq y\\
\end{array}\right.

(73)

2)

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\overset{p}{=}a_{\boldsymbol{L},\infty}^{(t)}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{c}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\overset{p}{=}c_{\boldsymbol{L},\infty}^{(t)}

(74)

where
\hat{a}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
and
\hat{c}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
are defined in (
69
), and

\displaystyle a_{\boldsymbol{L},\infty}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=(1-\beta)\mathbb{E}g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)+\beta\mathbb{E}\left[H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma,\hat{\sigma})\right]

(75a)

\displaystyle c_{\boldsymbol{L},\infty}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)-a_{\boldsymbol{L},\infty}^{(t)}\sigma\right)\sigma\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)-a_{\boldsymbol{L},\infty}^{(t)}\sigma\right)^{2}\right]}.

(75b)

3)

The transfer function of the low-rank denosier is given by

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\overset{p}{=}\phi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})

(76)

where

\displaystyle\phi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})

\displaystyle\overset{p}{=}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\sigma^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[f_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{2}(\sigma)\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)-a_{\boldsymbol{L},\infty}^{(t)}\sigma\right)^{2}\right]}\!v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{\!(t)\!}-\frac{\!\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\sigma f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)\right]\right)^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)-a_{\boldsymbol{L},\infty}^{(t)}\sigma\right)^{2}\right]\!}\!v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{\!(t)\!}-v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}.

(77)

4)

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}-\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}-\boldsymbol{L}\right\rangle\overset{p}{=}0

(78a)

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}-\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{L}\right\rangle\overset{p}{=}-\phi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})

(78b)

where the expectation is take over two independent and identical distributed variables,
\sigma
and
\hat{\sigma}
.
Here
\sigma\sim\mu_{\boldsymbol{L},v_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}}
with
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L},v_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}}(\sigma)=2v_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}\sigma\mu_{\boldsymbol{M}}(v_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}\sigma^{2})
. And
\mu_{\boldsymbol{M}}
is specified by the following Stieljes transform

\displaystyle m(z):=\int\frac{1}{\lambda-z}d\mu_{\boldsymbol{M}}(\lambda)

where
m(z)
is given by

\displaystyle m(z)=\int\frac{d\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}}(t)}{\frac{t}{1+v_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}\beta m(z)}-(1+v_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}\beta m(z))z+v_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}(1-\beta)}.

(79)

Here,
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}}
is the limiting empirical eigenvalue distribution of
\frac{1}{n_{2}}\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}
.

Proof.

Proof see Appendix
C-B
.
∎

Note that SURE is also an unbiased estimator for the MSE of the singular value soft-thresholding denoiser, as demonstrated in
[
72
]
, even though the corresponding function
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
is Lipschitz but not continuously differentiable. Furthermore, Lemmas
18
and
19
also hold for SVST. Consequently, the proof of Lemma
20
also holds for SVST, and the aforementioned results remain valid.

To prove Lemma
5
, we first obtain the asymptotic MSE transfer function of the low-rank denoiser with i.i.d. Gaussian input errors in the large-system limit based on the random matrix theory
[
85
]
in Lemma
20
.a. For the non-Gaussian i.i.d. noise, we assume that the singular matrices of the low-rank matrix
\boldsymbol{L}
follow the Haar distribution
3
3

3

Without imposing this assumption on the singular matrices, decomposing
\boldsymbol{S}
and
\boldsymbol{L}
from compressed measurements
\boldsymbol{y}
is fundamentally ill-posed. In fact, there are various scenarios where the existence of a unique splitting of
\boldsymbol{X}
into “low-rank” and “sparse” parts is not guaranteed. For instance, the low-rank matrix itself may exhibit significant sparsity, giving rise to challenges in identifiability.
, and impose some mild conditions on the empirical eigenvalue distribution of
\boldsymbol{L}
. We further show the uniformly Lipschitz property of the normalized MSE with respect to the singular values of the noise matrix in Lemma
18
. Then, in Lemma
20
.c, we show that the asymptotic MSE under i.i.d. non-Gaussian noise is equal to that under i.i.d. Gaussian noise, by using the Marchenko–Pastur law
[
86
]
from the random matrix theory and Lemma
19
. The detailed proof of Lemma 5 can be found in Appendix
C-B
.

III-D

Transfer Function of the Linear Denoiser

Before describing the dynamics of the linear denoiser in the large system, we give the definitions of the empirical MSEs:

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{X}\right\|_{F}^{2}

(80a)

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}-\boldsymbol{X}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

(80b)

The empirical values
a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
are given by

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}\!=\!\frac{\!\!\left\|\boldsymbol{X}\right\|_{F}^{2}\!\left\langle\!\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}\!,\!\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}\!\right\rangle\!-\!\left\langle\!\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}\!,\!\boldsymbol{X}\!\right\rangle\left\langle\!\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}\!,\!\boldsymbol{X}\!\right\rangle}{nv_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\left\|\boldsymbol{X}\right\|_{F}^{2}\!-\!\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{X}\right\|_{F}^{2}-\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}},\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle\right)^{2}}

(81a)

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}\!=\!\left(\frac{\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle-\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}},\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle}{\left\|\boldsymbol{X}\right\|_{F}^{2}}\right)^{-1}.

(81b)

Note that
\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
and
\hat{c}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
are intractable in practice since both of them involve
\boldsymbol{X}
. However,
\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
and
\hat{c}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
are proved almost surely convergence to a finite number as
n\to\infty
. Hence, we can approximately evaluate the parameters by the asymptotic results in the iterations.

Lemma 6

(Asymptotics for linear denoiser)
.

Under Assumptions
1
-5, the following relations hold for each iteration t:

1)

For the LMMSE denoiser,

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}

\displaystyle\overset{p}{=}1\!-v^{(t)}_{\!\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}\!}\mathbb{E}\left[\!\frac{\!\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}}\!}{\!\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}}\!v^{(t)}_{\!\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}\!}\!+\!\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}\!}\right]

(82a)

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}

\displaystyle\overset{p}{=}\frac{1}{v^{(t)}_{\!\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}\!}\mathbb{E}\left[\!\frac{\!\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}}\!}{\!\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}}\!v^{(t)}_{\!\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}\!}\!+\!\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}\!}\right]}

(82b)

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle\overset{p}{=}\psi(v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}})

(82c)

where

\displaystyle\psi(v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}})=\frac{1}{\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[\!\frac{\!\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}}\!}{\!\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}}\!v^{(t)}_{\!\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}\!}\!+\!\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}\!}\right]}-v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}.

(83)

Here, the expectation is taken over the limiting empirical eigenvalue distribution
p(\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}})
.

2)

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}-\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}-\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle\overset{p}{=}0

(84a)

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle\overset{p}{=}0.

(84b)

Proof.

Proof see Appendix
E
.
∎

Corollary 2
.

Under Assumptions
1
-5, suppose
\mathcal{A}
is a partial Haar operator. Then

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}\overset{p}{=}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}.

(85)

In practice, the discrete Fourier transform (DFT) and the discrete cosine transform (DCT) are commonly used in the construction of the compressing matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
. Despite the fact that the DFT and DCT matrices are subsamples of special orthogonal matrices, the transfer function derived above for the partial Haar operator is also empirically applicable to the random compressing matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
generated by uniformly and randomly selecting rows from a DCT or DFT matrix in the large system limits.

III-E

SE for the Overall Algorithm

In the last three subsections, we have analyzed the state evolution for each parts separately. In the following, we combine the SE of the three parts and analysis the SE of the overall algorithm based on Assumptions 1-5. We will replace
v
with
\tau
to represent the variance of the messages in the following.

Theorem 1

(State evolution)
.

Under Assumptions 1-5, as
n\to\infty
, the asymptotic MSE evolution of the ITMP satisfies the following equations:

\displaystyle\tau^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}\

\displaystyle=\ \psi(\tau^{(t-1)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}+\tau^{(t-1)}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}})

(86a)

\displaystyle\tau^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}\

\displaystyle=\ \varphi(\tau^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}+\tau^{(t-1)}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}})

(86b)

\displaystyle\tau^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}\

\displaystyle=\ \phi(\tau^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}+\tau^{(t-1)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}).

(86c)

where the general form of
\varphi
and
\phi
are respectively defined in (
66
) and (
77
) and
\psi
is specific to the form (
83
) for LMMSE denosier .

Proof.

Under Assumptions 1 and 3, as
n\to\infty
, we obtain (
65
) by Lemma
4
. Under Assumptions 2 and 4, as
n\to\infty
, we obtain (
76
) by Lemma
5
. We rewrite them as

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\overset{p}{=}\varphi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})

(87a)

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\overset{p}{=}\phi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})

(87b)

Recall that the message from
\delta
to node
\boldsymbol{X}
is constructed as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{X}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to\delta}+\hat{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta},\quad\hat{\tau}^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{X}}=\hat{\tau}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to\delta}+\hat{\tau}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta}.

(88)

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}-\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle=\left\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{L}+\boldsymbol{S}\right\rangle+\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}+\boldsymbol{L}\right\rangle\overset{p}{=}-\phi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})-\varphi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}),

(89)

by (
67
b) of Lemma
4
and (
78
b) Lemma
5
, where
\boldsymbol{L}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S}
are separately independent with
\boldsymbol{S}
and
\boldsymbol{L}
.
Then

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\overset{p}{=}\phi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})+\varphi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}).

(90)

Furthermore, by Lemma
6
, we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}\overset{p}{=}\psi(\phi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})+\varphi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})).

(91)

Note that the messages from
\delta
to node
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
are respectively constructed as

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t+1)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\delta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to\delta},\quad\hat{\tau}^{(t+1)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}=\hat{\tau}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\delta}+\hat{\tau}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to\delta}

(92a)

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t+1)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\delta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta},\quad\hat{\tau}^{(t+1)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}=\hat{\tau}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\delta}+\hat{\tau}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta}.

(92b)

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}^{(t+1)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}\overset{p}{=}\psi(\phi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})+\varphi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}))+\varphi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})

(93)

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}^{(t+1)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}\overset{p}{=}\psi(\phi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})+\varphi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}))+\varphi(\tau_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}).

So far, we complete the proof.
∎

Note that the proposed SE of the algorithm is developed based on a semi-closed loop proof with Assumption
1
and
2
, comparing to the analysis in
[
71
,
65
]
.
Nevertheless, the SE precisely forecasts the evolution of MSE as shown in Fig.
5
. The analysis of MSE provides meaningful insights, particularly for non-separable spectral functions in the presence of non-Gaussian noise. Moreover, the convergence analysis will be developed in the next section based on the proposed SE iteration.

As
t\to\infty
, the fixed point
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*})
is given by

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}=\varphi\left(\psi(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*})+\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*}\right)

(94a)

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*}=\phi\left(\psi(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*})+\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}\right).

(94b)

Clearly, the fixed point is a function of
\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\rho,p_{\boldsymbol{S}}(S),\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma),p(\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}}),\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2},\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}},\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\}
. For given
\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\rho,p_{\boldsymbol{S}}(S)
,
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma),p(\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}})
,
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}
,
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}},\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\}
, we say that the algorithm successfully recover
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
, when
\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(\psi(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*})+\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*}\right)
and
\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\psi(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*})+\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}\right)
are smaller than a predetermined value.
For some fixed parameters of
\{p_{\boldsymbol{S}}(s)
,
p_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\theta)
,
p(\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}})
,
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}
,
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}
,
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
,
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\}
, if we choose some proper value of
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
and
\rho
, the algorithm will stably recover
\boldsymbol{S}
and
\boldsymbol{L}
. This is verified in the next section.

IV
Convergence Analysis of State Evolution

Recall that in Theorem
1
, we obtain the SE for the proposed algorithm. In this section, We follow the analysis in
[
87
]
and characterize the convergence the SE.
To this end, we formally define the variance evolution sequence as follows.

Definition 6
.

Let
\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=v^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}
,
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=v^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}
and
\tau_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}=v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}
. The recursion starts from
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{L}}}^{(0)},\tau_{\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{S}}}^{(0)})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and we get the sequences
\left\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq 1}
and
\left\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq 1}
by:

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=\varphi\left(\psi(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t-1)}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t-1)})+\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t-1)}\right)

(95a)

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=\phi\left(\psi(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t-1)}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t-1)})+\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t-1)}\right),

(95b)

where
\psi
,
\varphi
and
\phi
follow the definitions in Theorem
1
.

For the purpose of convergence analysis, we focus on situation that the linear operator
\mathcal{A}
is a partial Haar operator. Then, the transfer function
\psi
for LMMSE denoiser is given by

\displaystyle\psi(v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)})=v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)+\frac{1}{\alpha}\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}

(96)

where
0<\alpha\leq 1
is the undersampling ratio.

Moreover, we make a few assumptions about the two scalar functions
\varphi(x)
and
\phi(x)
. Our numerical results in Section
V
suggest these assumptions are mild and are satisfied for commonly used denoisers.

Assumption 4
.

\varphi(x)
and
\phi(x)
are continuous and strictly monotonically increasing on
[0,\infty)
with
\varphi(0)=0
and
\phi(0)=0
.
\varphi^{\prime}(x)
and
\phi^{\prime}(x)
are continuous on
[0,\infty).

Our goal is to understand the conditions under which the sequences
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq 1}
and
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq 1}
in (
95
) converge to zero, namely, the matrices
\boldsymbol{S}
and
\boldsymbol{L}
are accurately recovered. We first give a necessary condition for perfect reconstruction.

IV-A

Necessary Condition for Global Convergence

Lemma 7

(Necessary condition for global convergence)
.

Suppose that Assumption
4
holds. Let
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq 1}
and
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq 1}
be generated as in (
95
). If

\lim_{t\to 0}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{t\to 0}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=0,

for any
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(0)},\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(0)})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, then necessarily

\alpha>\alpha_{nec}:=\sup_{x>0}\ \frac{\varphi(x)+\phi(x)}{\varphi(x)+\phi(x)+x}.

(97)

Proof.

See Appendix
F-A
.
∎

Note that both
\varphi(x)
and
\phi(x)
are nonnegative. Hence,
\alpha_{nec}\leq 1
and the necessary condition in (
97
) is not vacuous.
Furthermore,
\alpha_{nec}
can be lower bounded by

\displaystyle\alpha_{nec}\geq\max\{\alpha_{\boldsymbol{S}},\alpha_{\boldsymbol{L}}\}

(98)

where

\displaystyle\alpha_{\boldsymbol{S}}:=\sup_{x>0}\ \frac{\varphi(x)}{\varphi(x)+x},

(99a)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\boldsymbol{L}}:=\sup_{x>0}\ \frac{\phi(x)}{\phi(x)+x}.

(99b)

It is straightforward to show that
\alpha_{\boldsymbol{S}}
is exactly the threshold of
\alpha
for the global convergence of the iteration
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t-1)}\right)
, and
\alpha_{\boldsymbol{L}}
is the threshold for the global convergence of the iteration
\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t-1)}\right)
. Hence, (
98
) means that the number of measurements needed for exactly recovering both
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
is larger than that needed for recovering
\boldsymbol{L}
or
\boldsymbol{S}
individually, which is consistent with our intuition.

IV-B

Sufficient Condition for Global Convergence

Before we analyze the convergence of SE, we first define three thresholds of
\alpha
which are useful to understand the fixed points of (
95
):

\begin{split}\alpha_{1}&:=\sup_{x>0}\ \frac{\varphi^{\prime}(x)}{\varphi^{\prime}(x)+1},\\[3.0pt]
\alpha_{2}&:=\sup_{x>0}\ \frac{\phi^{\prime}(x)}{\phi^{\prime}(x)+1},\\[3.0pt]
\alpha_{3}&:=\inf\{\alpha\in(0,1);x\geq\Psi_{1}(\Psi_{2}(x,\alpha),\alpha),\forall 0<x<\sup\varphi\},\end{split}

(100)

where
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
are defined in (
104
). By Assumption
4
, there exist a
0<\hat{x}<x
such that
\phi^{\prime}(\hat{x})x=\phi(x)
. Then, we obtain
\alpha_{1}\geq\alpha_{\boldsymbol{S}}
by definition. Similarly, we obtain
\alpha_{2}\geq\alpha_{\boldsymbol{L}}
. As it will be clear in our later discussions, these thresholds are related to the uniqueness of the fixed point for the SE equations (
95
); see Lemma
22
-Lemma
23
.

We next present a sufficient condition for the global convergence of
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq 1}
and
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq 1}
based on
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
and
\alpha_{3}
.

Theorem 2

(Sufficient condition for global convergence)
.

Suppose that Assumption
4
holds. Let
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq 1}
and
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq 1}
be generated as in (
95
). Consider the noiseless model where
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}=0
.
If
\alpha>\max\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}
, then the following holds for any
\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(0)}\geq 0
and
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(0)}\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=0.

(101)

Proof.

Proof see Appendix
F-C
.
∎

IV-C

Proof of Theorem
2

Our main goal is to study the dynamics of the iterations

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t-1)}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t-1)}\right)

(102a)

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t-1)}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t-1)}\right).

(102b)

First of all, we hope the dynamic system will converge to the solutions of the following equations:

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}=\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*}\right)

(103a)

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*}=\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}\right).

(103b)

Before studying the dynamics of the SE, we first analyze the fixed point of (
103
a) and (
103
b).

When
\alpha
is small, there may exist multiple fixed points. As
\alpha
is large enough, both (
103
a) and (
103
b) only have one globally attractive fixed point. This will be shown in Lemmas
22
.

Note that based on Assumption
4
,
\varphi
and
\phi
are strictly monotonically increasing functions on
(0,+\infty)
. Hence, there inverse functions exist. For convenience, we define the inverse of function
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
as

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}},\alpha)\triangleq\alpha\varphi^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})-(1-\alpha)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}

(104)

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}_{2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}},\alpha)\triangleq\alpha\phi^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})-(1-\alpha)\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}.

where
\varphi^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})
and
\phi^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})
are defined in a certain domain. Specifically,

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}\varphi^{-1}\quad=\quad[0,\sup\varphi]

(105)

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}\phi^{-1}\quad=\quad[0,\sup\phi].

We need to restrict to

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\triangleq\left\{\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right):0<\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}\leq\sup\varphi,0<\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\leq\sup\phi\right\}.

(106)

After a single iteration, for any initialization,

\displaystyle\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\right)\in\mathcal{R},\quad\forall t\geq 1

(107)

With these definitions, we can equivalently write (
103
) as

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*},\alpha),

(108a)

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*},\alpha).

(108b)

We will sometimes write
\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}},\alpha)
and
\Psi_{2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}},\alpha)
as
\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})
and
\Psi_{2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})
when
\alpha
is fixed.

Fig. 3:
Illustration of the three regions in (
115
) with
\alpha>\max\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}
and the generated
(\hat{\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}}^{(t_{0})},\hat{\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}}^{(t_{0})})
from
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})})
in the three regions
\mathcal{R}_{0}
,
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
. The
arrows
represent the mapping defined in (
109
). This function maps any initialization
\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}\right)
in
\mathcal{R}
to a new initialization
\left(\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})},\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}\right)
in
\mathcal{R}
.
Here, we choose MMSE estimator for the sparse denoiser and best-rank-
r
estimator for the low rank denoiser. The entries of
\boldsymbol{S}
are independently generated by following a Gaussian-Bernoulli distribution with unit variance and zero mean with
\rho=0.35
. The matrix
\boldsymbol{L}
is generated by the multiplication of two independent zero mean Gaussian matrix of sizes
n_{1}\times r
and
r\times n_{2}
with
\gamma=0.1
. Then,
\boldsymbol{L}
is normalized by
\frac{1}{n}\|\boldsymbol{L}\|^{2}_{F}=1
.
\alpha=0.8
.

The importance of the condition
\alpha>\max\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}
in Theorem
2
can be explained as follows. First, we prove in Lemma
22
that both (
103
a) and (
103
d)
only exist one globally attractive fixed point when
\alpha>\max\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}
; see Lemma
22
for definitions of attractive fixed point. Furthermore, (
108
a) and (
108
b) are all strictly increasing functions by Lemma
22
. We further show in Lemma
23
that
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})=(0,0)
is the only fixed points of (
103
) when
\alpha>\max\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}
.

It turns out that, under the same condition, the SE dynamics converges to this fixed point from any starting point, which we shall prove in the rest of this section.

Define, for given
t_{0}\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})}\triangleq\max\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})},\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})})\right)

(109)

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}\triangleq\max\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})},\Psi_{2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})})\right).

We generated
\left\{\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq t_{0}}
and
\left\{\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq t_{0}}
by (
95
) from initialization
(\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})},\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})})
. Our goal is to prove

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})}\geq\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\geq\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+2)}\cdots

(110)

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}\geq\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\geq\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0}+2)}\cdots.

Namely,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}

(111)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*}

Note that by Assumption
4
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)}{\partial\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}}>0,\frac{\partial\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)}{\partial\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}}>0

(112a)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)}{\partial\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}}>0,\frac{\partial\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)}{\partial\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}}>0.

(112b)

Then, for any
t\geq t_{0}

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\geq\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}

(113)

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\geq\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}.

So far, we only need to prove
\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}=\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{*}=0
, which implies

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=0

(114)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=0.

For this, we divide
\mathcal{R}
into the following three sub-regions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{0}\triangleq\left\{\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)|0<\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}<\sup\phi,0<\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}<\Psi_{2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})\right\},

(115)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}\triangleq\left\{\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)|0<\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}<\sup\phi,\Psi_{2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})\leq\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\leq\min(\Psi^{-1}_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}),\sup\varphi)\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}\triangleq\left\{\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)|0<\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}<\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}),\Psi_{1}^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})<\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}<\sup\phi\right\}.

Note that for any
\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}\right)\in\mathcal{R}
, the corresponding
(\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})},\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})})
is in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
; see illustration in Fig.
3
.
Therefore, to conclude Theorem
2
, it remains to show that for any
(\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})},\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})})\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
, the sequences
\left\{\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq t_{0}}
and
\left\{\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq t_{0}}
converge to
(0,0)
, as guaranteed by Lemma
8
below.

Lemma 8
.

Suppose that Assumption
4
holds. Let
\left\{\hat{\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq t_{0}}
and
\left\{\hat{\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq t_{0}}
be generated as in (
95
). Let
\alpha>\max\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}
. Then

(i)

(\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)},\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})
remains in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
for all
t>t_{0}

(ii)

(\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)},\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})
converges:

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\hat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=0.

(116)

The proof of Lemma
8
can be found in Appendix
F-B
.

V
Numerical Results

In this section, we present numerical experiments corroborating our main results.
The rank-
r
matrix
\boldsymbol{L}
is generated by the multiplication of two zero mean random Gaussian matrices of size
n_{1}\times r
and
r\times n_{2}
, with
n=n_{1}\times n_{2}
. Each entry of the sparse matrix
\boldsymbol{S}
is independently generated by following a Gaussian-Bernoulli distribution with zero mean and unit variance, where
1-\rho
is the probability of being non-zero in the distribution. We normalize
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
such that
\|\boldsymbol{S}\|_{F}^{2}=n
and
\|\boldsymbol{L}\|_{F}^{2}=n
.
The parameter
\alpha
is defined as
\alpha=\frac{m}{n}
, where
m
represents the number of observations, i.e., the length of
\boldsymbol{y}
.
\alpha
is the measurement rate of the compression matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
, which is an
m\times n
matrix.
The matrix form
\boldsymbol{A}
of linear operator
\mathcal{A}
is generated by
\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{W}
, where
\boldsymbol{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
is a random selection matrix that selects rows
randomly from
\boldsymbol{W}
and
\boldsymbol{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a discrete cosine transform (DCT) matrix.
For the linear denoiser, we choose the LMMSE denoisr. Recall that the transfer function
\Psi(\cdot)
is given by

\displaystyle\psi(v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}})=\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}.

(117)

For the sparse denoiser, we choose the MMSE denoiser. The transfer function
\psi(\cdot)
is given by

\displaystyle\varphi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})=\left(\frac{1}{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}-\frac{1}{\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{S}}(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})}\right)^{-1}

(118)

with

\displaystyle\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{S}}(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathbb{E}_{S}[S|S+\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}N_{\boldsymbol{S}}]-S\right)^{2}\right].

(119)

For the low-rank denoiser, we choose best-rank-
r
denoiser. Note that for the best-rank-
r
denoiser,
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
can be regard as the singular value hard thresholding truncated at the normalized
r
-th singular value
\tilde{\sigma}_{r}^{(t)}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)=\operatorname{h}(x;\tilde{\sigma}_{r}^{(t)})=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }0\leq x<\tilde{\sigma}_{r}^{(t)}\\
x&\text{ if }\tilde{\sigma}_{r}^{(t)}\leq x\end{cases}

(120)

by identical functions
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
acting on all singular values
\sigma_{i}
. Let the cumulative distribution function of the empirical distribution of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}v_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}}}\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}
be denoted by
F_{\sigma,n_{1}}(x)

\displaystyle F_{\sigma,n_{1}}(x)=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\tilde{\sigma}_{r}^{(t)}\leq x}.

(121)

As
n_{1}\to\infty
,
F_{\sigma,n_{1}}(x)
convergence weakly to a deterministic function
F_{\sigma}(x)
whenever
F_{\sigma}(x)
is continuous at
x
. Then, let
\tilde{\sigma}^{*}
be the largest value such that
F_{\sigma}(\tilde{\sigma}^{*})=1-\gamma
.
Note that the best-rank-r denoiser is not Lipschitz continuous and has discontinuities. We employ the smoothing technique to construct a smooth denoiser satisfies the requirements in Assumption
3
, which is proved closely enough to the original one in Lemma
9
. Concretely, we smooth
\operatorname{h}(x;\tilde{\sigma}^{*})
by convolving it with a bump function.
For arbitrary radii
\epsilon>0
, define

\displaystyle B_{\epsilon}(x)=\frac{B(\frac{x}{\epsilon})}{\int B(\frac{x}{\epsilon})dx}.

(122)

Let

\displaystyle\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x,\tilde{\sigma}^{*})=\operatorname{h}(x,\tilde{\sigma}^{*})\star B_{\epsilon}(x).

(123)

Similar technique have been adapted in
[
88
]
[
89
]
with Gaussian kernel to smooth the point-wise denoisers in the message passing algorithms. In Lemma
21
, we show that
\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x,\tilde{\sigma}^{*})
satisfies the requirements in Assumption (
3
.vi). We show that the corresponding low-rank denoiser
D_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon}
is closed to the best-rank-
r
denoiser in Lemma
9
. Its proof is shown in Appendix
D
.

Lemma 9
.

Let
x,\sigma^{*},\epsilon,v>0
and
\epsilon<\sigma^{*}
. Let
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}=\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}
.
Suppose Assumption
2
hold.
As
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}\overset{p}{=}\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon}(v)

(124)

where
\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon}(v)
is given in (
72
) with
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)=\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x,\tilde{\sigma}^{*})
. Here,
\tilde{\sigma}^{*}\triangleq\sup\{x:F_{\sigma}(x)=1-\gamma\}
and
F_{\sigma}(x)
is cumulative dsitribution function of the limiting empirical singular value distribution of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{vn_{2}}}\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
. Denote the best-rank-
r
denoiser by
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}
. Let
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\cdots
denote a decreasing sequence of numbers such that
\epsilon_{i}>0
and
\epsilon_{i}\to\infty
. Then,
\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}
weakly converge and

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}\overset{p}{=}\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}.

(125)

Moreover, the empirical parameters
\hat{a}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
and
\hat{c}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
defined in (
69
) and
\frac{1}{n}\left\|\boldsymbol{L}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|_{F}^{2}
for best-rank-
r
denoiser are all converge to their limits, which can be arbitrary approximated with the smooth denoiser sequence
\{\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}\}
as in (
125
).

Note that the analytical form obtain in (
72
) still requires the continuously differentiable property of
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
. Consequently, (
72
) is not applicable to the best-rank-
r
denoiser or the hard thresholding denoiser, despite their empirical MSE proven convergence. However, we can still employ Monte Carlo method to approximate the limits of
\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}
by SURE, which is an unbiased estimator proven in
[
73
]
.

V-
1
State evolution simulation

In this subsection, we verify the proposed state evolution of our algorithm. For this, we track
\frac{1}{n}\|\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to\delta}-\boldsymbol{L}\|^{2}_{F}
and
\frac{1}{n}\|\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S}\|^{2}_{F}
(referred to as “simulation”) and the predicted MSEs by (
17
) and (
18
) (referred to as “evolution”).

We choose the LMMSE denoiser given in Corollary
1
for the linear module. For the sparse module, We observe that the empirical distribution of the error
\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S}
shown in Fig.
2
is close to Gaussian distribution. Then,
we further assume that
N_{\boldsymbol{S}}
follows Gaussian distribution in (
119
).
In our experiments,
\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
for MMSE denoiser is approximated by
\frac{\hat{v}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}}{v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}}
, with

\displaystyle\hat{v}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i,j}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{S_{i,j}}\left\|S_{i,j}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{S_{i,j}}\left\{S_{i,j}|\left(S_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right)_{i,j}\right\}\right\|^{2}_{F}.

(126)

According to Lemma
9
,
\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}
for the best-rank-
r
denoiser is approximated by the Monte Carlo method in
[
90
]
, where

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}}=\frac{1}{n\boldsymbol{v}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}}\left\langle\!\frac{\mathcal{D}\left(\!\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}\!+\!\epsilon\boldsymbol{N}\!\right)\!-\!\mathcal{D}\left(\!\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}\!\right)}{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{N}\right\rangle

(127)

Here,
\boldsymbol{N}
is a random Gaussian matrix with zero
mean and unit variance entries.
\epsilon
is a small positive real number, which is take as
10^{-4}
in our experiments.

For the state evolution iteration,
\Psi(\cdot)
is given by

\displaystyle\psi(v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}})=\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}.

(128)

The transfer function
\psi(\cdot)
is given by

\displaystyle\varphi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})=\left(\frac{1}{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}-\frac{1}{\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{S}}(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})}\right)^{-1}

(129)

with

\displaystyle\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{S}}(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathbb{E}_{S}[S|S+\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}N_{\boldsymbol{S}}]-S\right)^{2}\right].

(130)

The transfer function
\phi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})
for best-rank-r denoiser is approximated by using Monte Carlo method.
Consider the following model

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}=\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}}\boldsymbol{N}

(131)

where
\boldsymbol{N}
is a random Gaussian matrix with zero
mean and unit variance entries, and
v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}
is a positive real number.

Fig. 4:
The curve
\phi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}})
obtained by averaging 50 runs with a precision of
0.01
for the variance. The other settings are
n_{1}=n_{2}=2000
.

We obtain the curve of
\phi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}})
in the region
[0,5]
with
\frac{1}{n}\left\|\hat{c}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\mathcal{D}\left(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}\right)-\hat{\alpha}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}\right)-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}
by using the Monte Carlo method, which is shown in Fig.
4
.
Then, we are able to approximate
\phi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}})
by linear interpolation of the obtained data with arbitrary
v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}
.

Fig. 5:
The left figure shows the dynamic changes of the estimation error of message
m_{\boldsymbol{L}\to\delta}^{(t)}
. The right figure shows the dynamic changes of the estimation error of message
m_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta}^{(t)}
. The settings are
n_{1}=n_{2}=1000,\gamma=0.05,\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}=0,\rho=0.05
and different
\alpha
.

From Fig.
5
, we see that
the state evolution iteration accurately predicts the variance in practical algorithm iteration.

V-
2
Phase transition

Recall that Theorem
2
proposes a sufficient condition for the global convergence of
\alpha
. The following experiments verify the accuracy of the boundary presented in Theorem
2
. Fig.
6
illustrates the phase transition phenomenon generated by varying
\rho
and
\gamma
under a given measurement rate
\alpha
, representing the boundaries between the
success
and
failure
of the algorithm. Both the grid intervals of the horizontal and vertical axes in the figure are set to
0.01
.
To numerically verify Theorem
2
, we obtain the corresponding transfer functions (
\varphi(x)
and
\phi(x)
) and the corresponding first-order derivative curves (
\varphi^{\prime}(x)
and
\phi^{\prime}(x)
) for each pair
(\rho,\gamma)
through numerical simulations:

\begin{split}\alpha_{1}&:=\sup_{x>0}\ \frac{\varphi^{\prime}(x)}{\varphi^{\prime}(x)+1},\\[3.0pt]
\alpha_{2}&:=\sup_{x>0}\ \frac{\phi^{\prime}(x)}{\phi^{\prime}(x)+1},\\[3.0pt]
\alpha_{3}&:=\inf\{\alpha\in(0,1);x\geq\Psi_{1}(\Psi_{2}(x,\alpha),\alpha),\forall 0<x<\sup\varphi\}.\end{split}

(132)

Furthermore, for each pair
(\rho,\gamma)
, we calculate
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
and
\alpha_{3}
based on the numerical values of the transfer functions. If
\max\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}
is lower than the sampling rate
\alpha
, Theorem
2
shows that the recovery can be achieved. For example, in the upper-left plot of Fig.
6
, if for a given pair
(\rho,\gamma)
the corresponding
\max\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}
obtained through numerical simulations is less than
0.4
, then Theorem
2
asserts that the algorithm will succeed.
The
blue
region in the figure represents the theoretically predicted successful recovery area, while the
yellow
region represents the area represents the area where recovery may or may not be possible. To empirically verify whether the theory is correct, we run experiments with a set of
(\rho,\gamma)
and check for successful recovery. To obtain the boundary points of the empirical phase transition, for a given
\rho
, we conduct experiments to explore the maximum value of
\gamma
that allows the algorithm to successfully recover. The maximum parameters
(\rho,\gamma)
that can enable successful experiments are indicated by the
*
in the figure.

The settings of Monte Carlo experiments are given as follows. The rank-
r
matrix
\boldsymbol{L}
is generated by the multiplication of two zero mean random Gaussian matrices of size
n_{1}\times r
and
r\times n_{2}
. Each entry of the sparse matrix
\boldsymbol{S}
is independently generated by following a Gaussian-Bernoulli distribution with zero mean and unit variance, where
1-\rho
is the probability of being non-zero in the distribution. We normalize
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
such that
\|\boldsymbol{S}\|_{F}^{2}=n
and
\|\boldsymbol{L}\|_{F}^{2}=n
.
The matrix form
\boldsymbol{A}
of linear operator
\mathcal{A}
is generated by
\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{W}
, where
\boldsymbol{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a random permutation matrix and
\boldsymbol{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a discrete cosine transform (DCT) matrix. We consider the noiseless case, where
\sigma^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}=0
.
We consider the algorithm to have successfully recovered
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
when the following conditions are met:
1) the normalized mean square error (NMSE) for
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
is defined as
\frac{\left\|\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}}\leq 10^{-3}
and
\frac{\left\|\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S}\right\|^{2}_{F}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}}\leq 10^{-3}
, respectively;
2) the iteration count
t
is less than 100. For each grid point, the numerical values were tested in ten Monte Carlo experiments. If all the ten trials are successful, we consider that the values at this grid point can successfully recover the desired signal.

We illustrate the phase transition of the algorithm in Fig.
6
.

Fig. 6:
The phase transition curves of CRPCA algorithms with different
\alpha
. The blue region corresponds to the situation that the algorithm successfully recovers
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
predicted by Theorem
2
. The 2D red coordinates
*
represent the limit situation where the algorithm successfully recovers
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
through practical experiments.
At the 2D red coordinates
*
, increasing
\rho
or
\gamma
will lead to the failure of the algorithm.

We observe that the sufficient boundary condition proposed in Theorem
2
closely approximates the empirical phase transition observed in the experiments.

Fig. 7:
The empirical phase transition curves of various CRPCA algorithm.
n_{1}=n_{2}=500,\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}=0
. The region upon each phase transition curve corresponds to the situation that the corresponding algorithm
successfully recovers
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
.

Furthermore, we compare the phase transition of our algorithm with that of SPCP
[
43
]
and SpaRCS
[
44
]
, as illustrated in Fig.
7
. According to discussions on the degrees of freedom of
(\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{S})
in
[
42
]
, the minimal sampling ratio
\alpha
required for successful recovery must satisfy
\alpha\geq\rho+(2-\gamma)\gamma
.
We see that the phase transition curve of our algorithm is the closest to
the upper bound specified by the degrees of freedom and considerably higher than those of
SpaRCS and SPCP.

Indeed, since the recovery of
\boldsymbol{S}
necessitates the simultaneous reconstruction of its support set and the values on this support, a higher sampling rate is required than merely the sparse level
\rho
. Consequently, it is acknowledged that there is a discernible gap between the theoretical degrees of freedom and the empirical phase transition curve of the CRPCA problem.

VI
Conclusions

In this paper, we presented a new formulation for turbo message passing in the context of the CRPCA problem. We derived an asymptotic MSE transfer function of the low-rank denoiser with i.i.d. non-Gaussian input noise under certain regularity conditions. Leveraging these results, we established the SE of the algorithm under some mild assumptions. By analyzing the SE, we investigated the global convergence properties and identified the boundary of measurements required for reliable signal recovery. Numerical results demonstrated that the SE accurately tracks the MSEs of the denoisers in each iteration. Additionally, the predicted boundary based on the SE closely matches the empirical phase transition curve.

In conclusion, our proposed algorithm provides an effective approach for the CRPCA problem, leveraging turbo message passing and the SE framework. The SE analysis offers valuable insights into the algorithm’s behavior and convergence properties. The phase transition curve comparison further highlights the efficacy of our algorithm.

Appendix A
Proofs Related to the Algorithm Design

A-A

Useful Lemma

We first present the following useful lemma applied in the proofs.

Lemma 10
.

Let
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
denote a random vector satisfies
\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{a}\right\|_{F}^{2}=c_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
denote a random vector satisfies
\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{a}^{T}\boldsymbol{b}\right]=c_{2}
. Suppose
\textbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a deterministic matrix. Let
\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol{M})}{n}=d
. Let
\boldsymbol{V}\in\mathcal{O}{n}
be a Haar matrix independent of
\boldsymbol{a}
,
\boldsymbol{b}
and
\boldsymbol{M}
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{a}^{T}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{a}\right]=c_{1}d;\quad\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{a}^{T}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{b}\right]=c_{2}d

(133)

Proof.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{a}^{T}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{a}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i,j}M_{i,j}\left(\sum_{k}a_{k}V_{i,k}\right)\left(\sum_{l}a_{l}V_{j,l}\right)\right)\right]

(134a)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=j}M_{i,j}\left(\sum_{k}a^{2}_{k}V^{2}_{i,k}\right)\right]=\frac{c_{1}}{n}\sum_{i=j}M_{i,j}=c_{1}d.

(134b)

Similarly,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{b}^{T}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{a}\right]=c_{2}d

(135)

∎

In the rest of this appendix, we provide the proofs needed in Section
II
.

A-B

Proof of (
17
)

To start with, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\langle c^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}-a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right),\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right\rangle

(136a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\lVert c^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}-a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right)\right\rVert^{2}_{F}

(136b)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}

(136c)

where (
136
a) follows from the definition of
\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
in (
14
), (
136
b) follows from the definition of
c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
in (
16
b) and (
136

\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
in (
14
). From (
15
b) and Assumption
1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right\rangle+\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{S}\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{S}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}

(137a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{S}\right\rangle.

(137b)

Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}+\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S}\right\rangle

(138a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{S}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}

(138b)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}+\!2\mathbb{E}\left\langle\!\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{S}\!\right\rangle\!-\!\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{S}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}

(138c)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}

(138d)

where (
138
a) is from (
15
b), (
138
b) is from Assumption
1
, (
138
c) is from (
15
b) and Assumption 1, (
138
d) is from (
136
) and (
137
).
We are now ready to prove (
17
). To this end, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}\right\rangle

(139a)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}-c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}\right\rangle+c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}+v_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}\right)

(139b)

where (

) and (

), and (

\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{S}}
in (

) and Assumption 1.

)

To start with, we obtain the following results for the low rank denoiser similarly to the derivation in

.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}\right\rangle=\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{L}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{L}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{L}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}\right\|_{F}^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|_{F}^{2}

(141a)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}\right\|_{F}^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{L}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\right\|_{F}^{2}-v_{\boldsymbol{L}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}

(141b)

(141c)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left\|\!\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}\!\right\|_{F}^{2}\!\left\|\!\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}\!\right\|_{F}^{2}\!\!-\!\left\langle\!\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})},\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}\!\right\rangle^{2}\!\right]}{n\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}-a_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\boldsymbol{L}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]}-\!v_{\boldsymbol{L}\to p(\boldsymbol{L})}^{(t)}

(141d)

where (

, (

).

(143b)

\displaystyle=-v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}

(143c)

where (

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t+1)}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}\right\rangle+\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{M}\left(\boldsymbol{A}\operatorname{vec}\left(\boldsymbol{n}\right)\right),\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{S})\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{M}\left(\boldsymbol{A}\operatorname{vec}\left(\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to\delta}-\boldsymbol{S}\right)\right),\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{S})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=0

A-E

Proof of Lemma
2

We first present some useful relations in MMSE denoiser.
The correlations between
\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)}
,
\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}
and
\boldsymbol{S}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}\right\rangle=\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}

(145a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}\right\rangle=\sum_{i,j}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{S_{i,j}}\left[S_{i,j}|(S_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)})_{i,j}\right]^{2}\right].

(145b)

And, the MSE of the MMSE denoiser is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\boldsymbol{S}-\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}\right\|^{2}\right]=\sum_{i,j}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(S_{i,j}\right)^{2}\right]\!-\!\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{S_{i,j}}\left[S_{i,j}|(S_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}^{(t)})_{i,j}\right]^{2}\right]\right]

(146)

where (
146
) follows from (
145b
).
Combining the above relations, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}\right\rangle=\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

(147)

What remains is to prove (
39
).

\displaystyle a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}\right\rangle}{nv_{\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}}^{(t)}}=\frac{v_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}{v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}}

(148)

where (
148
) follows from Assumption 1 and (
147
).
Then, (
39
b) is given by

\displaystyle\quad c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=\frac{\left(1-a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\right)\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}-nv_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}{\left(1-a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{S}\right\|_{F}^{2}-nv_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\left(1-a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}

(149a)

\displaystyle=\frac{v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}}{v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}-v_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}

(149b)

where (
149
a) follows from (
145a
) and Assumption 1, (
149
b) follows from (
148
).
The rest is to prove (
40
). By substituting
a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
in (
139
) with the reduced form in (
148
) and (
149
), we obtain

\displaystyle\quad v_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=\frac{v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}v_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}{v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\rightarrow p(\boldsymbol{S})}-v_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}.

(150)

This completes the proof of Lemma
2
.

A-F

Proof of Lemma 3

Recall that the SVD form of the matrix form
\boldsymbol{A}
of ROIL operator
\mathcal{A}
is
\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{A}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{A}}^{T}
, where
\boldsymbol{U}_{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
are orthogonal matrices and
\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
is a diagonal matrix. Moreover,
\boldsymbol{V}_{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a Haar random matrix. Based on Assumptions
1
and
2
, for each iteration,
\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}
is independent of the linear operator
\mathcal{A}
, specifically, is independent of the haar matrix
\boldsymbol{V}_{A}
.
To start with, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{A}vec\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{X}\right)\right\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[vec\left(\!\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\!-\!\boldsymbol{X}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{A}}^{T}\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{A}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{A}}^{T}vec\left(\!\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\!-\!\boldsymbol{X}\!\right)\right]

(151a)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\|\boldsymbol{A}\right\|_{F}^{2}}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{X}\right\|_{F}^{2}

(151b)

where (
151
b) follows from Lemma
10
.

Similarly, we first present the following relations before determining the parameters
a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
. Denote
\boldsymbol{X}_{L}^{(t)}
the estimation under LMMSE denoiser, which is given in (
48
). Then,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{2}^{F}-v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}

(152a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}_{L}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{2}^{F}-v_{\boldsymbol{X}_{L}}^{(t)}

(152b)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{X}_{L}^{(t)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{2}^{F}-v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}.

(152c)

where (
152
a) follows from Lemma
1
, (
152
b) and (
152
c) follows from Lemma
10
and (
152
a), and
v_{\boldsymbol{X}_{L}}^{(t)}
is given by

\displaystyle v_{\boldsymbol{X}_{L}}^{(t)}=v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}\!-\!\frac{(v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}})^{2}}{n_{1}n_{2}}\operatorname{tr}\left(\!\boldsymbol{A}^{T}\!\left(v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}\!+\!\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\!\boldsymbol{A}\!\right).

(153)

Then, we take (
152
) into (
32
) and obtain

\displaystyle a_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\!\frac{\!\frac{\!\mathbb{E}\!\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{2}^{F}}{n}\!\!\left(\!\frac{\mathbb{E}\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{2}^{F}}{n}\!-\!\!v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\!\right)\!-\!\left(\!\frac{\mathbb{E}\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{2}^{F}}{n}\!\!-\!\!v_{\boldsymbol{X}_{L}}^{(t)}\!\right)\!\left(\!\frac{\mathbb{E}\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{2}^{F}}{n}\!\!-\!\!v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\!\!\right)}{\frac{\mathbb{E}\|\!\boldsymbol{X}\!\|_{2}^{F}}{n}v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\!-\!\left(\!v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\!\right)^{2}}=\frac{v_{\boldsymbol{X}_{L}}^{(t)}}{v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}}

(154a)

\displaystyle c_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{2}^{F}}{n}-v_{\boldsymbol{X}_{L}}^{(t)}\right)-\frac{v_{\boldsymbol{X}_{L}}^{(t)}}{v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}}(\frac{\mathbb{E}\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{2}^{F}}{n}-v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)})}{\frac{\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{F}^{2}}{n}}\right)^{-1}=\frac{v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}}{v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}-v_{\boldsymbol{X}_{L}}^{(t)}}.

(154b)

Then, we obtain
