\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\!\rightarrow\boldsymbol{X}}\!=\!\operatorname{vec}\!\left(\!\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}\!\right)\!+\!\!\frac{(v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)})^{2}}{v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\!\!-\!v_{\boldsymbol{X}_{L}}^{(t)}}\!\boldsymbol{A}^{T}\!\!\left(\!\boldsymbol{y}\!-\!\!\boldsymbol{A}\!\operatorname{vec}\left(\!\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}\right)\!\right).

(155)

And the corresponding variance
v_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}
for LMMSE denoiser is given by

\displaystyle v_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}=\frac{v_{\boldsymbol{X}_{L}}^{(t)}v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}}{v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}-v_{\boldsymbol{X}_{L}}^{(t)}}=\frac{n}{\operatorname{tr}\left(\!\boldsymbol{A}^{T}\!\left(v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}\!+\!\sigma^{2}_{\boldsymbol{n}}\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\!\boldsymbol{A}\!\right)}-v_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}.

(156)

A-G

Proof of Corollary
1

If
\mathcal{A}
is a partial Haar operator, we have
\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}=\boldsymbol{I}_{m}
. Substituting this into (
156
), we obtain

\displaystyle v_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}=v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}\!-\!\frac{(v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}})^{2}}{n_{1}n_{2}}\frac{m}{(v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}})^{2}+\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}}

(157a)

\displaystyle v_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t+1)}=\frac{n-m}{m}v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}+\frac{n}{m}\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}.

(157b)

Appendix B
Proofs Related to The State Evolution in Sparsity Model

B-A

Proof of Lemma
4

In this lemma, we first prove the convergence of a general pseudo-Lipschitz function
f(S_{i,j},N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j})
with proper moments assumptions. Then, we show that
S_{i,j}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(S_{i,j}+\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j}\right)
and
N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(S_{i,j}+\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j}\right)
are all pseudo-Lipschitz functions, which complete the proof.

By the pseudo-Lipschitz property of
f
, we obtain

\displaystyle f(S_{i,j},N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j})

\displaystyle\leq L\left(\left\|S_{i,j}\right\|^{k}+\left\|N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j}\right\|^{k}+1\right).

(158)

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|f(S_{i,j},N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j})|\leq L\mathbb{E}|\left\|S_{i,j}\right\|^{k}+\left\|N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j}\right\|^{k}+1|.

(159a)

By the bounded
k
-th assumption of
S
and
N_{S}
,
\mathbb{E}|f(S_{i,j},N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j})|
is bounded. By the Kolmogorov’s strongly law of large number, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}f(S_{i},N_{\boldsymbol{S},i})\overset{\text{a.s.}}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[f(S,N)\right].

(160)

with the
i.i.d.
assumption of
S_{i,j}
and
N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j}
in Assumption
3
.
Note that almost sure convergence implies convergence in probability. Then,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}f(S_{i},N_{\boldsymbol{S},i})\overset{p}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[f(S,N)\right].

(161)

By the
k
-th pseudo-Lipschitz assumption of a general pointwise denoiser
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}
, we conclude that
S_{i,j}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(S_{i,j}+\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j}\right)
and
N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(S_{i,j}+\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j}\right)
are pseudo-Lipschitz functions of
k+1
and
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{2}\left(S_{i,j}+\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j}\right)
is pseudo-Lipschitz functions of
2k
. Then, we conclude
(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(S_{i,j}+\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}N_{\boldsymbol{S},i,j}\right)-S_{i,j})^{2}
is pseudo-Lipschitz functions of
2k
. Then, (
61
) holds. Similarly, we conclude that
\frac{1}{n}\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}\to p(\boldsymbol{S})}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}^{(t)}\right\rangle
converge almost surely to
\mathbb{E}[\sqrt{v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}}N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(S+\sqrt{v^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}}N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right)]
. And the denominator part of
\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
almost surely converge to
v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
by i.i.d. Assumption. Then,
\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
converges.
As for
\hat{c}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
, the denominator part is decomposed by three parts

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(S+\sqrt{v^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}}N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\right\|^{2}_{F}

(162a)

\displaystyle\frac{2}{n}\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\left\langle\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(S+\sqrt{v^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}}N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right),S+\sqrt{v^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}}N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right\rangle

(162b)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}(\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)})^{2}\left\|S+\sqrt{v^{(t)}_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}}N^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right\|^{2}_{F}

(162c)

where (
162
a) conveges due to the pseudo-Lipschitz property.
Note that we have prove the almost sure convergence of
\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
. Then, (
162
b) and (
162
c) also converge. Then, (
63
) holds. Similarly, we conclude that (
66
) holds. Recall that
a_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
and
c_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
are designed by (
15
). With above asymptotic results for
\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
and
\hat{c}_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}
, (
67
) is obtained with some straightforward manipulations.

Appendix C
Proofs Related to the State Evolution in Low-Rank Model

C-A

Useful Lemmas

For the convenience of the following proofs, we consider a general model with arbitrary finite noise level
\sqrt{v}
by dropping the subscript
t
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}=\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N},

(163)

where
\boldsymbol{L}
follows the requirements in Assumption (
3
.v). The entries of
\boldsymbol{N}
are
i.i.d. zero-mean and unit variance random variables.
Let
\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}
be the SVD of
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
, where
\boldsymbol{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{1}}
and
\boldsymbol{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}\times n_{2}}
are orthogonal matrices, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\text{diag}\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\cdots,\sigma_{n_{1}}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is a diagonal matrix with the diagonal elements arranged in the descending order. Denote by
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{i}
respectively the
i
-th columns of
\boldsymbol{U}
and
\boldsymbol{V}
. Let
\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{T}
be the SVD of
\boldsymbol{L}
, where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{L}}=\text{diag}\{\sigma_{1,\boldsymbol{L}},\sigma_{2,\boldsymbol{L}},\cdots,\sigma_{n_{1},\boldsymbol{L}}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is a diagonal matrix with the diagonal elements arranged in the descending order. Let
\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}}^{T}
be the SVD of
\boldsymbol{N}
, where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{N}}=\text{diag}\{\sigma_{1,\boldsymbol{N}},\sigma_{2,\boldsymbol{N}},\cdots,\sigma_{n_{1},\boldsymbol{N}}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is a diagonal matrix with the diagonal elements arranged in the descending order. Let
\tilde{\sigma}_{i,\boldsymbol{L}}
be the
i
-th singular value of
\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{i,\boldsymbol{N}}
be the
i
-th singular value of
\frac{\boldsymbol{N}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}
. Let
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}}=[\tilde{\sigma}_{1,\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\sigma}_{2,\boldsymbol{L}},\cdots,\tilde{\sigma}_{n_{1},\boldsymbol{L}}]
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}}=[\tilde{\sigma}_{1,\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\sigma}_{2,\boldsymbol{N}},\cdots,\tilde{\sigma}_{n_{1},\boldsymbol{N}}]
.

First of all, we present the following lemma concerning the differentiability of denoiser
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
. We restrict our analysis to the scenario in which
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
is both simple and full-rank. This condition is ensured with probability 1 when
\boldsymbol{N}
is an i.i.d. matrix, as explained in
[
91
]
.
The first order differential form of spectral denoiser
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})
has been derived in
[
72
]
, where
f_{i}
is continuous. Here
f_{i}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is the scaling function acting on
\sigma_{i}
. The derived form also can be applied to SVST
[
72
]
. For the completeness of our proof, we give a briefly proof.

Lemma 11

(First order differential for spectral function)
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
is simple
4
4

4

We say that a matrix is simple if it has no repeated singular values besides zero.
and full-rank.
The first order differential form of spectral denoiser
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})
is given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}\!\left(\![\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}]\!\circ(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})+[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}\circ\left[\!(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})_{1:n_{1},1:n_{1}}^{T}\right],\boldsymbol{0}]\!\right)\!\boldsymbol{V}^{T}

(164)

where
\boldsymbol{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
is an arbitrary perturbation matrix.

Proof.

Based on the standard rules of calculus, we obtain the differentiation

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]=\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{U}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\boldsymbol{V}^{T}+\boldsymbol{U}\!\mathrm{d}(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\!)[\!\boldsymbol{\Delta}]\boldsymbol{V}^{T}\!+\!\boldsymbol{U}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\mathrm{d}\!\boldsymbol{V}\![\!\boldsymbol{\Delta}\!]^{T}

(165)

where
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{U}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]
,
\mathrm{d}(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\circ\boldsymbol{\Sigma})[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]
and
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{V}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]^{T}
are respectively the differentiation of
\boldsymbol{U}
,
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
and
\boldsymbol{V}^{T}
caused by the perturbation
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
.
The perturbation matrix
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
can be represented by:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Delta}=\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{U}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}+\boldsymbol{U}\mathrm{d}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]\boldsymbol{V}^{T}+\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{V}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]^{T}.

(166)

We multiply
\boldsymbol{U}^{T}
on the left side of (
166
) and
\boldsymbol{V}
on the left side.

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V}=\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{U}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]\boldsymbol{\Sigma}+\mathrm{d}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{V}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]^{T}\boldsymbol{V}

(167)

We denote
\Omega_{\boldsymbol{U}}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]=\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{U}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]\text{ and }\Omega_{\boldsymbol{V}}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]=\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{V}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]^{T}V
.
\Omega_{\boldsymbol{U}}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]
and
\Omega_{\boldsymbol{V}}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]
are anti-symmetric.

\displaystyle\Omega_{\boldsymbol{U},ii}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\Omega_{\boldsymbol{V},ii}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]=0.

(168)

Then, it’s not hard to see that the diagonal entries of
\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V}
are respectively equal to those of
\mathrm{d}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]
, i.e.,
\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V}\right)_{i,i}=\left(\mathrm{d}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]\right)_{i,i}
for
1\leq i\leq n_{1}
.
For the off-diagonal entries (
i\neq j
) of
\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V}
, we obtain the following the relations

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V}\right)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\boldsymbol{U},ij}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]\sigma_{j}+\sigma_{i}\Omega_{\boldsymbol{V},ij}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]

(169a)

\displaystyle-\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V}\right)_{ji}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\boldsymbol{U},ij}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]\sigma_{i}+\sigma_{j}\Omega_{\boldsymbol{V},ij}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}].

(169b)

Based on the above facts, we next represent the differentiation (
\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]
) of
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
at
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
as a function of
\boldsymbol{U}
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
,
\boldsymbol{V}
and arbitrary perturbation
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
in a closed form. Recall that the differentiation of the SVD form of
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
is given in (
165
). We will replace the differentiation of
\boldsymbol{U}
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
and
\boldsymbol{V}
with the linear combination of
\boldsymbol{U}
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
,
\boldsymbol{V}
and
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
.
From (
169
), for
i\neq j
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Omega_{\boldsymbol{U},ij}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma_{j}(\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{v}_{j})+\sigma_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{v}_{i})}{\sigma_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{j}^{2}}\quad 1\leq i,j\leq n_{1},

(170a)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\boldsymbol{V},ij}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{v}_{j})+\sigma_{j}(\boldsymbol{u}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{v}_{i})}{\sigma_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{j}^{2}}\quad 1\leq i,j\leq n_{1},

(170b)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\boldsymbol{V},ij}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}}{\sigma_{i}},\quad i\leq n_{1},n_{1}<j\leq n_{2}.

(170c)

In addition, the differentiation of the singular value
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is given by
\left(\mathrm{d}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\right)_{i,i}=\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V}\right)_{ii}
.

For
1\leq i=j\leq n_{1}
,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]\boldsymbol{V})_{i,i}=f_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}(\sigma_{i})\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}.

(171)

For
1\leq i,j\leq n_{1}
and
i\neq j
,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]\boldsymbol{V})_{i,j}

(172)

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{i}(\sigma_{i})\sigma_{i}\!-\!f_{j}(\sigma_{j})\sigma_{j}}{\sigma^{2}_{i}-\sigma^{2}_{j}}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}\!+\!\frac{f_{i}(\sigma_{i})\sigma_{j}\!-\!f_{j}(\sigma_{j})\sigma_{i}}{\sigma^{2}_{i}-\sigma^{2}_{j}}\boldsymbol{u}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}.

For
1\leq i\leq n_{1}
and
n_{1}<i\leq n_{2}
,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\mathrm{d}f[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]\boldsymbol{V})_{i,j}=\frac{f_{i}(\sigma_{i})}{\sigma_{i}}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}.

(173)

Denote
\frac{f_{i}(\sigma_{i})\sigma_{i}-f_{j}(\sigma_{j})\sigma_{j}}{\sigma^{2}_{i}-\sigma^{2}_{j}}
by the
(i,j)
-th entry of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}
for
1\leq i,j\leq n_{1}
,
i\neq j
and
f_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}(\sigma_{i})
by the
(i,i)
-th entry of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}
for
1\leq i\leq n_{1}
. Denote
\frac{f_{i}(\sigma_{i})\sigma_{j}-f_{j}(\sigma_{j})\sigma_{i}}{\sigma^{2}_{i}-\sigma^{2}_{j}}
by the
(i,j)
-th entry of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}
for
1\leq i,j\leq n_{1}
and
i\neq j
. Denote the
(i,i)
-th entry of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}
by
0
for
1\leq i,j\leq n_{1}
. Denote the
(i,j)
-th entry of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}
by
\frac{f_{i}(\sigma_{i})}{\sigma_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq n_{1}
and
n_{1}<j\leq n_{2}
. In summary, we represent
\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}[\boldsymbol{\Delta}]
as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}\!\left(\![\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}]\!\circ(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})\!+\![\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}\circ\left[\!(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\!\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})_{1:n_{1},1:n_{1}}^{T}\!\right]\!,\!\boldsymbol{0}]\!\right)\!\boldsymbol{V}^{T}

(174)

where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}
are only related to the singular value
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
and spectral function
f_{i}
.
∎

With the the first order differential for spectral denoiser
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
, the corresponding divergence can be derived.

Lemma 12
.

[
72
, Theorem IV.3]

Let
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
be a low-rank denoiser and
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be differentiable. Suppose that
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
is simple and full-rank. Then
\operatorname{div}(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}))
is given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}))=

\displaystyle(n_{2}-n_{1})\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\frac{f_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\sigma_{i}\right)}{\sigma_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}f_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{\prime}\left(\sigma_{i}\right)+\sum_{i\neq j,i,j=1}^{n_{1}}\frac{\sigma_{i}f_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\sigma_{i}\right)-\sigma_{j}f_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\sigma_{j}\right)}{\sigma_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{j}^{2}}

(175)

where
\sigma_{i}
is the
i
-th singular value of
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
.

Remark 3
.

Note that
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
is simple and has full-rank with probability 1 as shown in
[
91
]
. For the other case,
[
72
, Theorem IV.6]
also provides an extended version of
\operatorname{div}(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}))
where
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
has repeated singular values and has singular value
0
. Define
g_{\boldsymbol{L}}
:
[0,+\infty)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
with
g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)=\frac{f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)}{x}
for
x>0
and
g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(0)=0
.
Define
H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x,y)
by

\displaystyle H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x,y)\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&(x,y)=(0,0)\\
\frac{xf_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{\prime}(x)+f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)}{2x}&x=y\neq 0\\
\frac{xf_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)-yf_{\boldsymbol{L}}(y)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}&x\neq y.\\
\end{array}\right.

(176)

Then, a general expression for
\operatorname{div}(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}))
applicable to all matrices is given by:

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}))=

\displaystyle(n_{2}-n_{1})\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}f_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{\prime}\left(\sigma_{i}\right)+\sum_{i\neq j,i,j=1}^{n_{1}}H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j}).

(177)

In the following lemma, we analyze the property of function
H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x,y)
.

Lemma 13
.

Suppose
f:[0,+\infty)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
is a monotonic increasing and continuously differentiable function defined on
[0,+\infty)
and also satisfies the property of being locally Lipschitz over
[0,b]
for any
b>0
. Let
M_{b}
denote the Lipschitz constant on the interval
[0,b]
. Further assume that
f
with
f(0)=0
and
f^{\prime}(0)=0
. Denote
H(x,y)
and
G(x,y)
by

\displaystyle H(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&(x,y)=(0,0)\\
\frac{xf^{\prime}(x)+f(x)}{2x}&x=y\neq 0\\
\frac{xf(x)-yf(y)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}&x\neq y\\
\end{array}\right.

(178)

and

\displaystyle G(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&(x,y)=(0,0)\\
\frac{xf^{\prime}(x)-f(x)}{2x}&x=y\neq 0\\
\frac{yf(x)-xf(y)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}&x\neq y.\\
\end{array}\right.

(179)

Then, for any compact domain
D\subset[0,b]\times[0,b]
, where
b\geq 0
,
H(x,y)
and
G(x,y)
are both continuous functions, where
H(x,y)
is bounded by
\frac{3}{2}M_{b}
and
G(x,y)
is bounded by
M_{b}
.

Proof.

We first proof the boundedness.
Without loss of generally, we first consider the situation
x>y
in
[0,b]\times[0,b]
.
H(x,y)
and
G(x,y)
are both continuous by assumption. We only need to show the boundedness. With the monotonic increasing property of
f
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{yf(y)-yf(x)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\leq\frac{xf(y)-yf(x)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\leq\frac{xf(y)-yf(y)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}.

(180)

Note that
\frac{yf(y)-yf(x)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}<0
. Then

\displaystyle\left|\frac{xf(y)-yf(x)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right|\leq\operatorname{max}\left(\frac{f(y)}{x+y},\frac{y}{x+y}\frac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}\right).

(181)

With the Lipschitz property of
f
and
f(0)=0
, we conclude
\frac{f(y)}{x+y}<\frac{M_{b}}{2}
and
\frac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}<M_{b}
. Then

\displaystyle|G(x,y)|=\left|\frac{xf(y)-yf(x)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right|<\frac{M_{b}}{2}.

(182)

Note that

\displaystyle\frac{xf(x)-yf(y)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}-\frac{xf(y)-yf(x)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}=\frac{(x+y)\left(f(x)-f(y)\right)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}=\frac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}<M_{b}.

(183)

Then, by the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle|H(x,y)|=\left|\frac{xf(x)-yf(y)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle<\left|\frac{xf(y)-yf(x)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\right|+\left|\frac{(x+y)\left(f(x)-f(y)\right)}{(x+y)(x-y)}\right|<\frac{3M_{b}}{2}.

(184)

Next, we establish the case for
x=y\neq 0
.
By the definition of
H(x,y)
and
G(x,y)
at
x=y\neq 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle|H(x,y)|=\left|\frac{yf^{\prime}(y)+f(y)}{2y}\right|\leq\left|\frac{f^{\prime}(y)}{2}\right|+\left|\frac{f(y)}{2y}\right|<M_{b}

(185)

\displaystyle|G(x,y)|=\left|\frac{yf^{\prime}(y)-f(y)}{2y}\right|\leq\left|\frac{f^{\prime}(y)}{2}\right|+\left|\frac{f(y)}{2y}\right|<M_{b}.

We next prove the continuity at
x=y\neq 0
. We need to show that for every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\delta_{\epsilon}
such that for any
(x,y)
satisfying
0<(x-z)^{2}+(y-z)^{2}<\delta_{\epsilon}
, the following condition holds:

\displaystyle|H(x,y)-H(z,z)|<\epsilon.

(186)

Note that

\displaystyle|H(x,y)-H(z,z)|=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}|\frac{xf^{\prime}(x)+f(x)}{2x}-\frac{zf^{\prime}(z)+f(z)}{2z}|&x=y\neq 0\\
|\frac{xf(x)-yf(y)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}-\frac{zf^{\prime}(z)+f(z)}{2z}|&x\neq y\\
\end{array}\right.

(187)

By assumption,
\frac{xf^{\prime}(x)+f(x)}{2x}
is continuous on
(0,+\infty)
. For the case
x=y\neq 0
, there exists
\delta_{\epsilon,1}>0
, such that for any
0<(x-z)^{2}<\delta_{\epsilon,1}
,
|\frac{xf^{\prime}(x)+f(x)}{2x}-\frac{zf^{\prime}(z)+f(z)}{2z}|<\epsilon
.
For the case
x\neq y
, let
w=x^{2}
and
k=y^{2}
. There exists
q
such that

\displaystyle\frac{xf(x)-yf(y)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}=\frac{\sqrt{w}f(\sqrt{w})-\sqrt{k}f(\sqrt{k})}{w-k}=(\sqrt{q}f(\sqrt{q}))^{\prime}

(188)

where
q\in(x^{2},y^{2})
for
x<y
or
q\in(y^{2},x^{2})
for
y<x
by the mean value theorem.
​
Note that
\min\{|x-z|,|y-z|\}<|\sqrt{q}-z|<\max\{|x-z|,|y-z|\}
. By the continuity of
\frac{xf^{\prime}(x)+f(x)}{2x}
, there exists
\delta_{\epsilon,2}>0
, such that for any
0<(x-z)^{2}+(y-z)^{2}<\delta_{\epsilon,2}
,

\displaystyle|\frac{\sqrt{q}f^{\prime}(\sqrt{q})+f(\sqrt{q})}{2\sqrt{q}}-\frac{zf^{\prime}(z)+f(z)}{2z}|<\epsilon

(189)

where
\sqrt{q}
is bounded by
|\sqrt{q}-z|<\delta_{\epsilon,2}
. Denotes
\delta_{\epsilon}=\min\{\delta_{\epsilon,1},\delta_{\epsilon,2}\}
. Then, the obtained
\delta_{\epsilon}
satisfies the requirement that
0<(x-z)^{2}+(y-z)^{2}<\delta_{\epsilon}
, ensuring that
|H(x,y)-H(z,z)|<\epsilon
. Note that

\displaystyle G(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&(x,y)=(0,0)\\
f^{\prime}(x)-H(x,y)&x=y\neq 0\\
\frac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}-H(x,y)&x\neq y.\\
\end{array}\right.

(190)

Denotes

\displaystyle G_{H}(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&(x,y)=(0,0)\\
f^{\prime}(x)&x=y\neq 0\\
\frac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}&x\neq y.\\
\end{array}\right.

(191)

Then,
G(x,y)=G_{H}(x,y)-H(x,y)
.
To show the continuity of
G_{(}x,y)
at
x=y\neq 0
, we only need to show
G_{H}(x,y)
is continuous at
x=y\neq 0
. Similar to the method used to prove the continuity of
H(x,y)
at
x=y\neq 0
, we can similarly demonstrate the continuity of
G_{H}(x,y)
.
Then,
G(x,y)
is also continuous at
x=y\neq 0
.
​
For the case
(x,y)=(0,0)
,
we first consider
H(x,y)
. We need to show
{\forall}\epsilon>0
,
{\exists\delta_{\epsilon}}
, such that for any
(x,y)
satisfy
0<x^{2}+y^{2}<\delta_{\epsilon}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle|H(x,y)-0|<\epsilon.

(192)

​By assumption,
\frac{xf^{\prime}(x)+f(x)}{2x}
is continuous on
(0,+\infty)
and
\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}\frac{xf^{\prime}(x)+f(x)}{2x}=0
. For the case
x=y\neq 0
, there exists
\delta_{\epsilon,1}>0
, such that for any
0<x^{2}<\delta_{\epsilon,1}
,
|\frac{xf^{\prime}(x)+f(x)}{2x}|<\epsilon
.
For the case
x\neq y
, let
w=x^{2}
and
k=y^{2}
. There exists
q
such that

\displaystyle\frac{xf(x)-yf(y)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}=\frac{\sqrt{w}f(\sqrt{w})-\sqrt{k}f(\sqrt{k})}{w-k}=(\sqrt{q}f(\sqrt{q}))^{\prime}

(193)

where
q\in(x^{2},y^{2})
for
x<y
or
q\in(y^{2},x^{2})
for
y<x
by the mean value theorem. Note that
\min\{x,y\}<\sqrt{q}<\max\{x,y\}
. By the property of
\frac{xf^{\prime}(x)+f(x)}{2x}
, there exists
\delta_{\epsilon,2}>0
, such that for any
0<x^{2}+y^{2}<\delta_{\epsilon,2}
,

\displaystyle|\frac{\sqrt{q}f^{\prime}(\sqrt{q})+f(\sqrt{q})}{2\sqrt{q}}-0|<\epsilon

(194)

where
\sqrt{q}
is bounded by
|\sqrt{q}-z|<\delta_{\epsilon,2}
. Let
\delta_{\epsilon}=\min\{\delta_{\epsilon,1},\delta_{\epsilon,2}\}
. Then, the obtained
\delta_{\epsilon}

satisfies the requirement.
​ A similar analysis demonstrates the continuity of
G_{H}(x,y)
at
(0,0)
, which in turn implies the continuity of
G(x,y)
at
(0,0)
. With this, we complete the proof.
∎

Note that
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
, required in Assumption (
3
.vi), is continuously differentiable. Therefore, its derivative is bounded in any closed and bounded domain, which implies the local Lipschitz property. Let
H_{\boldsymbol{L}}
be defined by replacing
f
with
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
in the expression for
H(x,y)
. Consequently,
H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x,y)
is also continuous and bounded in any closed and bounded domain.

Lemma 14
.

[
85
, Theorem 1.1]
Denote
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T},n_{1}}
the empirical eigenvalue distribution of
\frac{1}{n_{2}}\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}
. Assume that the empirical distribution converges weakly to a deterministic distribution
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}}(\sigma)
. And the entries of
\boldsymbol{N}
are i.i.d. zero-mean and unit variance random variables. Let
\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}=\frac{1}{n_{2}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})^{T}
. Then the corresponding empirical eigenvalue distribution of
\mu_{\boldsymbol{M},n_{1}}
converges weakly to a limiting distribution
\mu_{\boldsymbol{M}}
, whose Stieljes transform
m(z):=\int\frac{1}{\lambda-z}d\mu_{\boldsymbol{M}}(\lambda)
satisfied the integral equation

\displaystyle m(z)=\int\frac{d\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}}(t)}{\frac{t}{1+v\beta m}-(1+v\beta m)z+v(1-\beta)}.

(195)

Remark 4
.

Note that the singular value of
\frac{\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}}{\sqrt{vn_{2}}}
is exactly the positive square root of eigenvalue of
\frac{1}{v}\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}
. Thus, the empirical singular values distribution of
\frac{\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}
weakly converges to a limiting distribution, denoted by
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L},v}
.
Then, the two limiting empirical distributions follow

\displaystyle\mu_{\boldsymbol{L},v}(\sigma)=2v\sigma\mu_{\boldsymbol{M}}(v\sigma^{2}).

(196)

Lemma 15
.

Let
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
be a sequence of elements, where
\sigma_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
is bounded.
Write
\delta_{\sigma_{i}}
for the unit mass at
\sigma_{i}
and zero elsewhere, the empirical measure and the distribution function are respectively defined by

\displaystyle\mu_{\sigma,n_{1}}(x)=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\delta_{\sigma_{i}}(x=\sigma_{i})

(197)

\displaystyle F^{\sigma}_{n_{1}}(x)=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\sigma_{i}\leq x}.

Assume that the empirical measure of
\mu_{\sigma,n_{1}}
converges weakly to a limiting measure
\mu_{\sigma}
as
n_{1}\to\infty
.
Let
\{(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})\}=\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\times\{\sigma_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n_{1}}
.
Write
\delta_{(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})}
for the unit mass at
(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})
and zero elsewhere. The empirical measure and the distribution function are denoted by

\displaystyle\mu_{(\sigma,\sigma),n_{1}}=\frac{1}{n_{1}\times n_{1}}\sum_{i=1,j=1}^{n_{1},n_{1}}\delta_{(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})}

(198)

\displaystyle F^{\sigma,\sigma}_{n_{1}}(x,y)=\frac{1}{n_{1}\times n_{1}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n_{1},n_{1}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\sigma_{i}\leq x,\sigma_{j}\leq y}.

Then

\displaystyle\mu_{(\sigma,\sigma),n_{1}}\overset{p}{=}\mu_{\sigma}\times\mu_{\sigma}.

(199)

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle F^{\sigma,\sigma}_{n_{1}}(x,y)=\frac{1}{n_{1}\times n_{1}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n_{1},n_{1}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\sigma_{i}\leq x,\sigma_{j}\leq y}

(200)

\displaystyle=\frac{\#\left\{1\leq i\leq n_{1},1\leq j\leq n_{1}\mid\sigma_{i}\leq x,\sigma_{j}\leq y\right\}}{n_{1}\times n_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\#\left\{1\leq i\leq n_{1}\mid\sigma_{i}\leq x\right\}\times\#\left\{1\leq j\leq n_{1}\mid\sigma_{j}\leq y\right\}}{n_{1}\times n_{1}}

\displaystyle=F^{\sigma}_{n_{1}}(x)\times F^{\sigma}_{n_{1}}(y).

We have

\displaystyle\lim_{n_{1}\to\infty}F^{\sigma}_{n_{1}}(x)\to F^{\sigma}(x),

(201)

for every number
x\in\mathbb{R}
at where
F^{\sigma}(x)
is continuous. And, for every continuous point
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
of
F^{\sigma}(x)\times F^{\sigma}(y)
,
x
and
y
are also the continuous points of function
F^{\sigma}
. Then, we obtain that

\displaystyle\lim_{n_{1}\to\infty}F^{\sigma}_{n_{1}}(x)\to F^{\sigma}(x),

(202a)

\displaystyle\lim_{n_{1}\to\infty}F^{\sigma}_{n_{1}}(y)\to F^{\sigma}(y),

(202b)

where
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
is the continuous point of
F^{\sigma}(x)\times F^{\sigma}(y)
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{n_{1}\to\infty}F^{\sigma,\sigma}_{n_{1}}(x,y)\to F^{\sigma}(x)\times F^{\sigma}(y),

(203)

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 16
.

Let
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
be a sequence of elements, where
\sigma_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
is bounded. Write
\delta_{\sigma_{i}}
for the unit mass at
\sigma_{i}
and zero elsewhere, the empirical measure and the cumulative distribution function are respectively defined by

\displaystyle\mu_{\sigma,n_{1}}=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\delta_{\sigma_{i}}

(204)

\displaystyle F^{\sigma}_{n_{1}}(x)=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\sigma_{i}\leq x}.

Assume that the empirical measure of
\mu_{\sigma,n_{1}}
converges weakly to a limiting measure
\mu_{\sigma}
as
n_{1}\to\infty
.
Then, as
n_{1}\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}\sum^{n_{1}}_{i\neq j,i,j=1}H(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})\overset{p}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[H(\sigma,\hat{\sigma})\right]

(205)

where the expectation is taken over two independent and identical variables
\sigma\sim\mu_{\sigma}
and
\hat{\sigma}\sim\mu_{\sigma}
.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}\sum^{n_{1}}_{i\neq j,i,j=1}H(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})=\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}\sum^{n_{1}}_{i,j=1}H(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})-\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}\sum^{n_{1}}_{i}H(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i}).

(206)

From Lemma
13
,
H(x,y)
is continuous and bounded on any compact domain. And
\sigma_{i}
is bounded. Then, by Lemma
15
and Definition
5
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}\sum^{n_{1}}_{i,j=1}H(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})\overset{p}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[H(\sigma,\hat{\sigma})\right],

(207)

where the expectation is taken over the two independent and identical variables
\sigma\sim\mu_{\sigma}
and
\hat{\sigma}\sim\mu_{\sigma}
.
Moreover,
H(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i})
is bounded on bounded domain from Lemma
13
. Then, as
n_{1}\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}\sum^{n_{1}}_{i}H(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i})\rightarrow 0.

(208)

Then, as
n_{1}\to\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n_{1}}_{i\neq j,i,j=1}H(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})\overset{p}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[H(\sigma,\hat{\sigma})\right].

(209)

∎

The following lemma presents a sufficient condition for the spectral denoiser
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
, whose MSE can be unbiasedly estimated using SURE.

Lemma 17
.

[
73
, Theorem 1]

Consider a spectral function estimator

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}f_{i}(\sigma_{i})\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\boldsymbol{v}^{T}_{i}

(210)

with finite second moment and with spectral functions fulfilling that
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n_{1}-1}
are continuously differentiable on
(0,\infty)
,
f_{n_{1}}
is continuously differentiable on
[0,\infty)
with
f_{n_{1}}(0)=f^{\prime}_{n_{1}}(0)=0
,
f_{i}\geq f_{j}
for
i\leq j
and
f^{\prime}_{i}\geq 0
. Then, SURE (
211
) is an unbiased estimator of the MSE.

\displaystyle\operatorname{SURE}=\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\|^{2}_{F}+2v\operatorname{div}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})\right)-n_{1}n_{2}v.

(211)

Lemma 18

(Lipschitz property of
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
)
.

Let Assumption (
3
.v) and (
3
.vi) hold. Let
\tilde{{\sigma}}_{i,\boldsymbol{L}}
be the
i
-th singular value of
\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{i,\boldsymbol{N}}
be the
i
-th singular of
\frac{\boldsymbol{N}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}
.
Assume that the largest singular value of
\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}
is bounded by
C_{1}
.
Denote
F_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle F_{1}(\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N})\triangleq\sqrt{n_{2}v}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right).

(212)

Then,
F_{1}(\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N})
is a uniformly pseudo-Lipschitz function of order 1 (namely, Lipschitz) with pseudo-Lipschitz constant
\frac{\sqrt{26}}{2}M_{\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta}}
,
where
M_{\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta}}
is the Lipschitz constant of
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
in
[0,M_{\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta}}]
. Denote
F_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle F_{2}(\tilde{\sigma}_{1,\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\sigma}_{2,\boldsymbol{N}},\cdots,\tilde{\sigma}_{n_{1},\boldsymbol{N}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}+\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{N}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\right)-\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right\|^{2}_{F},

(213)

where
\operatorname{diag}(\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{N}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}})=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}}=[\tilde{\sigma}_{1,\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\sigma}_{2,\boldsymbol{N}},\cdots,\tilde{\sigma}_{n_{1},\boldsymbol{N}}]
. Then,
F_{2}(\tilde{\sigma}_{1,\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\sigma}_{2,\boldsymbol{N}},\cdots,\tilde{\sigma}_{n_{1},\boldsymbol{N}})
is a uniformly pseudo-Lipschitz function of order 1 with pseudo-Lipschitz constant
\sqrt{26}M_{\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta}}\left(\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{v}}+M_{\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta}}(\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta})\right)
.

Proof.

To prove the Lipschitz property, we bound the operator norm of the corresponding Jacobi matrix . To do this, we present the first order differential form of
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
and show the boundedness of the operator norm of the Jacobi matrix
\boldsymbol{J}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}n_{2}\times n_{1}n_{2}}
of
\operatorname{vec}(F_{1}(\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N})):\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}n_{2}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}n_{2}}
. We prove the existence of a constant independent of the dimensions
(n_{1},n_{2})
. Let
\boldsymbol{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
with
\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}\|^{2}_{F}=1
.
Consider the differential form of
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
at
\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}
under perturbation
\epsilon\boldsymbol{\Delta}
, where
\epsilon
is small enough.
Then,

\displaystyle F_{1}(\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}+\epsilon\boldsymbol{\Delta})\!-\!F_{1}(\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N})=

\displaystyle\sqrt{n_{2}v}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}+\epsilon\boldsymbol{\Delta}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}})-\sqrt{n_{2}v}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}})

(214)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\!\boldsymbol{U}\!\left(\!(\![\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\!\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}\!]\!)\!\circ(\!\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\!\boldsymbol{\Delta}\!\boldsymbol{V}\!)\!+\![\!\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}\circ\left[\!(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\!\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})_{1:n_{1},1:n_{1}}^{T}\!\right]\!,\boldsymbol{0}]\!\right)\!\boldsymbol{V}^{T}\!\right)\epsilon\!+o(\epsilon),

where
(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})_{1:n_{1},1:n_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{1}}
represents the matrix formed by taking the first
n_{1}
rows and columns of
(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})
and the matrix
\boldsymbol{0}
is an
n_{1}\times(n_{2}-n_{1})
matrix with all elements equal to zero.

Note that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{J}\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})=\boldsymbol{U}\!\left((\![\!\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\!\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}\!]\!)\circ(\!\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\!\boldsymbol{\Delta}\!\boldsymbol{V}\!)\!\!+\![\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}\circ\left[\!(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\!\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})_{1:n_{1},1:n_{1}}^{T}\!\right]\!,\!\boldsymbol{0}]\!\right)\!\boldsymbol{V}^{T}.

We only need to show the boundedness of

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{J}\|_{op}=\underset{\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}\|^{2}_{F}=1}{\operatorname{sup}}\|\boldsymbol{J}\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\|.

(215)

Note that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{J}\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\right\|^{2}_{F}

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{U}\left(([\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}])\circ(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})+[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}\circ\left[(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})_{1:n_{1},1:n_{1}}^{T}\right],\boldsymbol{0}]\right)\boldsymbol{V}^{T}\right\|^{2}_{F}

(216a)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|([\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}])\circ(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})\right\|^{2}_{F}+2\left\|[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}\circ\left[(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})_{1:n_{1},1:n_{1}}^{T}\right],\boldsymbol{0}]\right\|^{2}_{F}

(216b)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i,j}[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}]_{i,j}^{2}(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})_{i,j}^{2}+2\left\|[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}\circ\left[(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})_{1:n_{1},1:n_{1}}^{T}\right],\boldsymbol{0}]\right\|^{2}_{F}

(216c)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\max_{i,j}|[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}]|\right)^{2}\sum_{i,j}(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})_{i,j}^{2}+2\left(\max_{i,j}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}|\right)^{2}\sum_{i,j}(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\boldsymbol{V})_{i,j}^{2}

(216d)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\max_{i,j}|[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}]|\right)^{2}+2\left(\max_{i,j}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}|\right)^{2}\leq\frac{13}{2}M_{\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}^{2}<\infty

(216e)

where
M_{\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}
is the Lipschitz constant of
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
in
[0,\tilde{\sigma}_{1}]
,
\max_{i,j}|[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}]|
denotes the largest absolute value of the entries of
[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}]
, and
\max_{i,j}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}|
denotes the largest absolute value of the entries of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}
.
Recall that the
(i,j)
-th entry of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}
is
H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i},\tilde{\sigma}_{j})

for
1\leq i,j\leq n_{1}
,
i\neq j
and the
(i,i)
-th entry of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}
is
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq n_{1}
. The
(i,j)
-th entry of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}
is
G_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i},\tilde{\sigma}_{j})
for
1\leq i,j\leq n_{1}
and
i\neq j
. The
(i,i)
-th entry of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}
is
0
for
1\leq i,j\leq n_{1}
. The
(i,j)
-th entry of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}
is
\frac{f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i})}{\tilde{\sigma}_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq n_{1}
and
n_{1}<j\leq n_{2}
.
Then

\displaystyle\max_{i,j}|[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}]|\leq\frac{3}{2}M_{\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \max_{i,j}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}|\leq M_{\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}.

(217)

Note that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n_{1}\to\infty}\tilde{\sigma}_{1}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta}\ \ a.s..

(218)

Then, we obtain the pseudo-Lipschitz constant
L_{F_{1}}
of the uniformly Lipschitz function as
n_{1}\to\infty

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{J}\|_{op}\leq\frac{\sqrt{26}}{2}M_{\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta}}\triangleq L_{F_{1}}.

(219)

Consider the perturbation
\epsilon\boldsymbol{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}
with
\|\boldsymbol{d}\|^{2}=1
. Let
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1}=\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\operatorname{diag}(\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
.
Here,
\operatorname{diag}(\boldsymbol{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
, where
(\operatorname{diag}(\boldsymbol{d}))_{i,i}=\boldsymbol{d}_{i}
denotes that the elements on the diagonal are given by the elements of vector
\boldsymbol{d}
, with all other entries being zero.
We show that
the differential form of
F_{2}(\tilde{\sigma}_{1,\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\sigma}_{2,\boldsymbol{N}},\cdots,\tilde{\sigma}_{n_{1},\boldsymbol{N}})
is given by

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{F_{2}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}}+\epsilon\boldsymbol{d})-F_{2}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}})}{\epsilon}

(220)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}+\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{N}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{N}}+\epsilon\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\operatorname{diag}(\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\right)\right\|^{2}_{F}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}+\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{N}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\right)\right\|^{2}_{F}}{\epsilon}

\displaystyle-\!\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{\!\frac{2}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\!\left\langle\!\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\!\!\left(\!\frac{\!\boldsymbol{L}\!}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\!+\!\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\!\frac{\!\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\!}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\!\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\!+\!\epsilon\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\operatorname{diag}(\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\!\right)\!,\!\frac{\!\boldsymbol{L}\!}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\!\right\rangle\!-\!\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\!\left\langle\!\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\!\left(\!\frac{\!\boldsymbol{L}\!}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\!+\!\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\frac{\!\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\!}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\!\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\!\right)\!,\!\frac{\!\boldsymbol{L}\!}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\!\right\rangle}{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\left\langle\left(\!\boldsymbol{U}\!\left([\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\!\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}]\!\circ(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\!\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1}\!\boldsymbol{V})\!+\![\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}\circ\left[\!(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\!\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1}\boldsymbol{V})_{1:n_{1},1:n_{1}}^{T}\!\right]\!,\!\boldsymbol{0}]\right)\!\boldsymbol{V}^{T}\!\right),\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}+\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{N}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\left\langle\left(\!\boldsymbol{U}\!\left(\ [\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{I}\!\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{DI}]\!\circ(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\!\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1}\!\boldsymbol{V})+[\!\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{IJ}\circ\left[\!(\boldsymbol{U}^{T}\!\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1}\boldsymbol{V})_{1:n_{1},1:n_{1}}^{T}\!\right]\!,\!\boldsymbol{0}]\!\right)\!\boldsymbol{V}^{T}\right),\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\operatorname{vec}(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right))^{T}\boldsymbol{J}\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1})-\frac{2}{\sqrt{vn_{1}n_{2}}}\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{L})^{T}\boldsymbol{J}\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1})

Then, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{F_{2}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}}+\epsilon\boldsymbol{d})-F_{2}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}})}{\epsilon}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\|\boldsymbol{L}\|_{F}}{\sqrt{vn_{1}n_{2}}}\|\boldsymbol{J}\|_{op}+\frac{2\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right)\|_{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\|\boldsymbol{J}\|_{op}

(221)

\displaystyle\leq\left(2\tilde{\sigma}_{1,\boldsymbol{L}}+2f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{1})\right)\|\boldsymbol{J}\|_{op}

Similar to
L_{F_{1}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\limsup_{n_{1}\to\infty}\left(2\tilde{\sigma}_{1,\boldsymbol{L}}+2f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{1})\right)\|\boldsymbol{J}\|_{op}\leq\sqrt{26}M_{\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta}}\left(\!\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{v}}+M_{\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta}}(\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta})\!\right)\triangleq L_{F_{2}},

(222)

which completes the proof.
∎

Definition 7

(Wasserstein distances)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}(r)
be the set of all Borel probability measures
P
on
\mathcal{R}
with
\int_{\mathcal{R}}\|x\|^{r}\ dP(x)<\infty
, where
r\in[1,\infty)
. For
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}(r)
, the r-Wasserstein distance between
P
and
Q
is defined by

\displaystyle d_{r}(P,Q):=\inf_{(X,Y)}\mathbb{E}\left(\|X-Y\|^{r}\right)^{1/r}

(223)

where the infimum is taken over all pairs of random vectors
(X,Y)
defined on a common probability space with
X\sim P
and
Y\sim Q
.

Lemma 19

(Weak Convergence with uniformly Lipschitz function
F_{2}
)
.

Let
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
and
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
be two sets, where
\sigma_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
are bounded.
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
and
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
separately have no repeated elements. Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=[\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\cdots,\sigma_{n_{1}}]
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{n_{1}}]
. Assume that the empirical distribution of
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
and
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
converge weakly to the same limiting distribution
\mu_{\boldsymbol{N}}
as
n_{1}\to\infty
.
F_{2}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\cdots,\sigma_{n_{1}})
is defined as in Lemma
18
. Then, there exits a permutation
\{\pi_{i}\}
of
\{1,2,\cdots,n_{1}\}
such that, as
n_{1}\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}F_{2}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\cdots,\sigma_{n_{1}})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}F_{2}(\lambda_{\pi_{1}},\lambda_{\pi_{2}},\cdots,\lambda_{\pi_{n_{1}}})\to 0.

(224)

Proof.

Let
\mu_{\sigma}(n_{1})
denote the empirical distribution of
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
and
\mu_{\lambda}(n_{1})
denote the empirical distribution of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
.
Note that by assumption,
\mu_{\sigma}(n_{1})
and
\mu_{\lambda}(n_{1})
converge weakly to the same limiting distribution
\mu_{\boldsymbol{N}}
.
\sigma_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
are bounded. From Theorem 7.12 in
[
92
]
, we obtain

\displaystyle d_{2}(\mu_{\sigma}(n_{1}),\mu_{\boldsymbol{N}})\to 0\quad\text{and}\quad d_{2}(\mu_{\lambda}(n_{1}),\mu_{\boldsymbol{N}})\to 0,

(225)

where
d_{2}
is the
2
-Wasserstein distance defined in Definition
7
.
Then, we obtain

\displaystyle d_{2}(\mu_{\lambda}(n_{1}),\mu_{\sigma}(n_{1}))\to 0.

(226)

Recall that the 2-Wasserstein distance for empirical measure is defined

\displaystyle d_{2}(\mu_{\lambda}(n_{1}),\mu_{\sigma}(n_{1}))=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n_{1}}\min_{\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\|\sigma_{i}-\lambda_{\pi_{i}}\|^{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n_{1}}\min_{\boldsymbol{P}}\left\|\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{P}\right\|^{2}}

(227)

where the minimize is taken over all permutations
\pi
.
This is a linear assignment problem, and can be solved by the Hungarian algorithm in cubic time
[
93
,
94
]
. Denote the optimal permutation by
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\pi^{*}}
and the corresponding permutation matrix
\boldsymbol{P}^{*}
such that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\pi^{*}}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{P}^{*}
. Recall that
F_{2}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\cdots,\sigma_{n_{1}})
is a uniformly pseudo-Lipschitz function of order
1
, with pseudo-Lipschitz constant
L_{F_{2}}
.
Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\|F_{2}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-F_{2}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{P}^{*})\|_{2}\leq\frac{L_{F_{2},n_{1}}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\|\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{P}^{*}\|_{2}=L_{F_{2},n_{1}}d_{2}(\mu_{\lambda}(n_{1}),\mu_{\sigma}(n_{1}))

(228)

Note that as
n_{1}\to\infty
,
\limsup_{n_{1}\to\infty}L_{F_{2},n_{1}}<L_{F_{2}}
. Together with (
226
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\|f(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-f(\boldsymbol{P}^{*}\boldsymbol{\lambda})\|_{2}\to 0,

(229)

which completes the proof.
∎

In the following, we mainly concentrate the MSE function of the Low-rank denoiser. In this lemma, we first prove the convergence of the Stein’s unbiased risk estimate for
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
defined in Assumption (
3
.vi). Then, we show that SURE is an unbiased estimator for the MSE of
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
when
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
is continuously differentiable. Next, we obtain analytical form of the asymptotic MSE under Gaussian noise. By the property of the low-rank denoiser and the random matrix theorem, we finally conclude that the asymptotic MSE under Gaussian is identical to that under i.i.d. non Gaussian.

Lemma 20
.

Denote by
\boldsymbol{L}=\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{T}
the SVD of the low-rank matrix
\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{N}=\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}}^{T}
the SVD of the noise matrix
\boldsymbol{N}
. Let
\tilde{\sigma}_{i,\boldsymbol{L}}
be the
i
-th singular value of
\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{i,\boldsymbol{N}}
be the
i
-th singular value of
\frac{\boldsymbol{N}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}
. Let
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}}=[\tilde{\sigma}_{1,\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\sigma}_{2,\boldsymbol{L}},\cdots,\tilde{\sigma}_{n_{1},\boldsymbol{L}}]
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}}=[\tilde{\sigma}_{1,\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\sigma}_{2,\boldsymbol{N}},\cdots,\tilde{\sigma}_{n_{1},\boldsymbol{N}}]
. Specifically, in this lemma, we use
\boldsymbol{G}
to denote an i.i.d. random Gaussian matrix with zero mean and unit variance, and
\boldsymbol{N}
to denote a general i.i.d. random matrix. Define

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}\right)-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}

(230)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{v}{n_{1}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{L}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{L}}+\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{N}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\right)-\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{L}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right\|^{2}_{F}.

where
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}}=\operatorname{diag}(\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{L}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}})
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}}=\operatorname{diag}(\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{N}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}})
. The following holds:

(
20
.a)

Let Assumption
(
3
.v) and (
3
.vi) hold. The SVD form of an i.i.d. random Gaussian
\boldsymbol{G}
is given by
\boldsymbol{G}=\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{G}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{G}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{G}}^{T}
. Let
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}=\operatorname{diag}(\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{G}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}})
. As
n\to\infty

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}})\overset{p}{=}\operatorname{AMSE}(v)

(231)

where
\operatorname{AMSE}(v)=v\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left(f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\!\sigma\!)-\!\sigma\!\right)^{2}\right]+2(1-\beta)\mathbb{E}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)\right]+2\beta\mathbb{E}_{\sigma,\hat{\sigma}}\left[H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma,\hat{\sigma})\right]-1\right)
Here,
g_{\boldsymbol{L}}
:
[0,+\infty)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
with
g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)=\frac{f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)}{x}
for
x>0
and
g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(0)=0
.
H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x,y)
is defined as

\displaystyle H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x,y)\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&(x,y)=(0,0)\\
\frac{xf_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{\prime}(x)+f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)}{2x}&x=y\neq 0\\
\frac{xf_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)-yf_{\boldsymbol{L}}(y)}{x^{2}-y^{2}}.&x\neq y\\
\end{array}\right.

(232)

The expectation is taken over two independent and identical distributed variables,
\sigma
and
\hat{\sigma}
. Let
\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}=\frac{1}{n_{2}}(\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N})(\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N})^{T}
. Define the corresponding limiting empirical eigenvalue distribution by
\mu_{\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}}
.
Here
\sigma,\hat{\sigma}\sim\mu_{\boldsymbol{L},v}
with
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L},v}(\sigma)=2v\sigma\mu_{\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}}(v\sigma^{2})
. And
\mu_{\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}}
is specified by the following Stieljes transform

\displaystyle m(z):=\int\frac{1}{\lambda-z}d\mu_{\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}}(\lambda)

where
m(z)
is given by

\displaystyle m(z)=\int\frac{d\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}}(t)}{\frac{t}{1+v\beta m(z)}-(1+v\beta m(z))z+v(1-\beta)}.

(233)

Here,
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}}(t)
is the limiting empirical eigenvalue distribution of
\frac{1}{n_{2}}\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}
, with
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}}(t)=\frac{\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sqrt{t})}{2\sqrt{t}}
.

(
20
.b)

Let Assumption
(
3
.v) and (
3
.vi) hold. Let
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\in\mathcal{O}{n}
be independent haar matrices, which are also independent with
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}
. Then

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}})\overset{p}{=}F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}})

(234)

(
20
.c)

Let Assumption
(
3
.iv-vi) hold. Then

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}})\overset{p}{=}F(\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}),

(235)

namely,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}\right)-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}\overset{p}{=}\operatorname{AMSE}(v).

(236)

Proof.

1.

Proof of (Conclusion:(
20
.a))
With Assumption
1
, the spectral denoiser
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
satisfies the requirements in
[
85
, Theorem 1.1]
.
\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}
is simple and full rank with probably
1
. Then, under Gaussian noise, we conclude that SURE is an unbiased estimator for the MSE of
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
with continuously differentiable
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
, where

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{v}{n_{1}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}\!+\!\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right)\!-\!\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{v}{n_{1}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}})\!-\!\frac{\boldsymbol{L}\!+\!\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right\|^{2}_{F}\!+\!\frac{v}{n_{1}}\frac{2}{n_{2}}\operatorname{div}\!\left(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\!\frac{\boldsymbol{L}\!+\!\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{vn_{2}}}\!\right)\right)\!-\!v\right].

(237)

In the above,
\operatorname{div}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}})\right)
is defined by

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right)\right)=(n_{2}-n_{1})\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}f^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i})+\sum_{i,j,i\neq j}^{n_{1}}H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i},\tilde{\sigma}_{j}),

(238)

where
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}
is the
i
-th singular value of
\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}
.

Step (1) :

To show the convergence of
\frac{v}{n_{1}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right)-\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right\|_{F}^{2}
, we first show the convergence of the
\operatorname{SURE}
in the large system even under i.i.d. noise
\boldsymbol{N}
. We reformulate
\operatorname{SURE}
by replacing the
\operatorname{div}
in Lemma
12

\displaystyle\quad\frac{v}{n_{1}}\!\left\|\!\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\!\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}{\sqrt{vn_{2}}}\!\right)\!-\!\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}{\sqrt{vn_{2}}}\!\right\|_{F}^{2}\!+\!\frac{2v}{n_{1}n_{2}}\operatorname{div}\!\left(\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\!\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}{\sqrt{vn_{2}}}\!\right)\right)-v

(239a)

\displaystyle=v\left(\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i}^{n_{1}}(f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i})-\tilde{\sigma}_{i})^{2}-1\right)+\frac{2v}{n_{1}n_{2}}\sum_{i,j,i\neq j}^{n_{1}}H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i},\tilde{\sigma}_{j})+2v\!\left(\!\frac{n_{2}\!-\!n_{1}}{n}\!\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\!g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i})\!+\!\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\!f^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i})\!\right)

(239b)

\displaystyle\overset{p}{=}v\mathbb{E}\left[\left(f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\!\sigma\!)-\!\sigma\!\right)^{2}\right]+2v(1-\beta)\mathbb{E}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)\right]+2v\beta\mathbb{E}_{\sigma,\hat{\sigma}}\left[H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma,\hat{\sigma})\right]-v

(239c)

where
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}=\frac{\sigma_{i}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}
and
\sigma_{i}
is the
i
-th singular value of
\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}
.
g_{\boldsymbol{L}}
and
H_{\boldsymbol{L}}
are defined in (
73
). The expectation is taken over the limiting empirical singular distribution of
\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}{\sqrt{vn_{2}}}
, denoted by
\sigma\sim\mu_{\boldsymbol{L},v}
;
\hat{\sigma}
is an i.i.d. copy of
\sigma
; (
239
b) follows the divergence of spectral estimators obtained in
[
72
, Theorem IV.3]
.

To prove (
239
c), we first show the boundedness of the singular values
\{\tilde{\sigma}_{i}\}_{1}^{n_{1}}
.
By
[
95
]
, the largest singular value
\sigma_{1,\boldsymbol{N}}
of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\boldsymbol{N}

\displaystyle\lim\sup_{n_{1}\rightarrow\infty}\sigma_{1,\boldsymbol{N}}\leqslant 1+\sqrt{\beta}\quad a.s..

(240)

Then, the largest singular value
\tilde{\sigma}_{1}
is bounded by
\frac{\sigma_{1,\boldsymbol{L}}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta}
, where
\sigma_{1,\boldsymbol{L}}
is the largest singular value of
\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}
. By Lemma
14
, we conclude that the empirical singular value distribution of
\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}{\sqrt{vn_{2}}}
converges weakly to a deterministic distribution, denoted by
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L},v}(\sigma)
.
Moreover,
\mu_{\boldsymbol{L},v}(\sigma)=2v\sigma\mu_{\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}}(v\sigma^{2})
. And
\mu_{\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}}
is specified by the following Stieljes transform

\displaystyle m(z):=\int\frac{1}{\lambda-z}d\mu_{\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}}}(\lambda)

where
m(z)
is given by

\displaystyle m(z)=\int\!\frac{d\mu_{\boldsymbol{L}\boldsymbol{L}^{T}}(t)}{\frac{t}{1\!+v\beta m(z)}\!-\!(\!1+v\beta m(z)\!)z\!\!+\!\!v(\!1\!-\!\beta\!)}.

(241)

Furthermore, we prove the weak convergence of the product empirical measure of
\{(\tilde{\sigma}_{i},\tilde{\sigma}_{j})\}=\{\tilde{\sigma}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\times\{\tilde{\sigma}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n_{1}}
in Lemma
16
.
For the continuously differentiable
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
,
f^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)
is continuous and
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}
is local Lipschitz continious in
[0,\frac{\sigma_{1,\boldsymbol{L}}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta}]
. Then,
\left(f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\!\sigma\!)-\!\sigma\!\right)^{2}
and
g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)
are bounded and continuous in bounded domain.
We conclude that
H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x,y)
is bounded and continuous in compact domain
[0,\frac{\sigma_{1,\boldsymbol{L}}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta}]\times[0,\frac{\sigma_{1,\boldsymbol{L}}}{\sqrt{v}}+1+\sqrt{\beta}]
in Lemma
13
. At last, with Definition
5
of weak convergence and Lemma
16
, we conclude the proof.

Step (2) :

We next show that
\frac{v}{n_{1}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right)-\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right\|_{F}^{2}
converges to a deterministic limit. We show the analytical form of the asymptotic MSE of the denoisers under Gaussian noise.
Denote

\displaystyle F_{3}(\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{G}))\triangleq\frac{v}{n_{1}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right)-\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

(242)

To show the Gaussian concentration of
F_{3}(\boldsymbol{G})
, we can bound
\operatorname{Var}[F_{3}(\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{G}))]
by Gaussian Poincaré inequality
[
96
]
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}[F_{3}(\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{G}))]\leq c\mathbb{E}\left[\|\nabla F_{3}(\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{G}))\|_{2}^{2}\right]

(243)

Note that
F_{1}(\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G})=\sqrt{n_{2}v}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right)
is a uniformly pseudo-Lipschitz function of order
k=1
as shown in Lemma
18
. By
[
65
, Lemma C.5 ]
,
\frac{1}{n}\left\|\sqrt{n_{2}v}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right)-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}
is a uniformly pseudo-Lipschitz function of order
k=2
. Then, by
[
65
, Lemma C.8]
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\|\nabla F_{3}(\operatorname{vec}(\boldsymbol{G}))\|_{2}^{2}\right]
is bounded and asymptotically converge to
0
. And we conclude

\displaystyle\frac{v}{n_{1}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right)-\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right\|_{F}^{2}\overset{p}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{v}{n_{1}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right)-\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right\|_{F}^{2}\right].

(244)

With (
237
) and the analytical form obtained in (
239
) , we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{v}{n_{1}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{G}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right)-\frac{\boldsymbol{L}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\right\|_{F}^{2}\overset{p}{=}v\mathbb{E}\left[\left(f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)-\sigma\right)^{2}\right]-v+2v(1-\beta)\mathbb{E}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)\right]+2\beta v\mathbb{E}\left[H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma,\hat{\sigma})\right].

(245)

2.

Proof of (Conclusion: (
20
.b))

We have shown the asymptotic convergence of
F(\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}})
.
Recall that, for an i.i.d. random Gaussian matrix
\boldsymbol{G}
,
\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{G}}
,
\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{G}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}
are independent with each other and
\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{G}}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{G}}
are haar matrix.
For any two independent
\boldsymbol{U}_{p}\in\mathcal{O}{n}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{p}\in\mathcal{O}{n}
,
\boldsymbol{U}_{p}\boldsymbol{G}\boldsymbol{V}_{p}^{T}
is still an i.i.d. random Gaussian matrix, where
\boldsymbol{U}_{p}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{G}}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{p}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{G}}
are haar matrix and independent with each other.

For four independent haar matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{V}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{2}
,

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{2}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{2}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}})

(246a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{2}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}})

(246b)

\displaystyle\overset{p}{=}

\displaystyle\operatorname{AMSE}(v),

(246c)

where (
246
b) is easy to check with the definition in (
230
) and (
246
c) follows from the Conclusion: (
20
.a).
Note that
\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
are independent with
\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{G}}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{G}}
. Then,
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{2}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{G}}
are four independent haar matrices. We replace the four matrices by
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
and reformulate the Gaussian concentration as

\displaystyle F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}})\overset{p}{=}\operatorname{AMSE}(v).

(247)

Then, we obtain

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{G}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}})\overset{p}{=}F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}})

(248)

3.

Proof of (Conclusion: (
20
.c))

Consider the MSE under i.i.d. random matrix
\boldsymbol{N}

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}})=\frac{v}{n_{1}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{L}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{L}}+\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{N}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\right)-\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{L}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}v}}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right\|^{2}_{F}.

(249)

By the rotationally invariant assumption for
\boldsymbol{L}
,
\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
are two independent haar matrices.

For two independent haar matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{1}
,

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}})=F(\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}})

(250)

where
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
are four independent haar matrices. Then, by (Conclusion: (
20
.b)), we obtain

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}})\overset{p}{=}\operatorname{AMSE}(v).

(251)

Let

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}F_{2}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}})

(252)

where
F_{2}
is defined in Lemma
18
. Note that
the empirical distribution of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}
both converge weakly to a same deterministic distribution due to the Marchenko–Pastur law
[
86
]
. By Lemma
19
, for any
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}
, there always exists a permutation
\boldsymbol{P^{*}}
matrix, such that, as
n_{1}\to 0
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}F_{2}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}F_{2}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}\boldsymbol{P^{*}})\to 0.

(253)

Note that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}F_{2}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}\boldsymbol{P^{*}})=

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}\boldsymbol{P^{*}})

(254)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\boldsymbol{P^{*}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\boldsymbol{P}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{V}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}).

where

\boldsymbol{P}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{V}}=\left(\begin{matrix}\boldsymbol{P}^{*}&\boldsymbol{0}_{n_{1},n_{2}-n_{1}}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{n_{2}-n_{1},n_{1}}&\boldsymbol{I}_{n_{2}-n_{1},n_{2}-n_{1}}\\
\end{matrix}\right).

(255)

and
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\boldsymbol{P^{*}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\boldsymbol{P}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{V}}
are four independent haar matrix. Then, with (Conclusion: (
20
.b)), we conclude

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}F_{2}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}\boldsymbol{P^{*}})\overset{p}{=}\operatorname{AMSE}(v).

(256)

Combining (
252
), (
253
) and (
256
), we obtain

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{N}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{L}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{N}})\overset{p}{=}\operatorname{AMSE}(v),

(257)

which completes the proof.

∎

C-B

Proof of Lemma
5

Assume Assumption
2
and Assumption (
3
.iv-vi) hold. Lemma (
5
.1) is straight forward a application of Lemma
20
. Note that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\left\langle\boldsymbol{L},\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}\right)\right\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}\right)-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}-\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}\right)\right\|^{2}_{F}-\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\left\|\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}\right),

(258)

where
\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}\right)-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}\overset{p}{=}AMSE(v)
and
\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}\right)\right\|^{2}_{F}\overset{p}{=}\mathbb{E}f^{2}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)
. Then,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\left\langle\boldsymbol{L},\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\left(\boldsymbol{L}+\sqrt{v}\boldsymbol{N}\right)\right\rangle\overset{p}{=}(1-\beta)\mathbb{E}g_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma)+\beta\mathbb{E}\left[H_{\boldsymbol{L}}(\sigma,\hat{\sigma})\right].

(259)

Applying the above conclusion to
\hat{a}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
, we derive the asymptotic results presented in Lemma (
5
.2).
With similar straightforward calculations, it is easy to demonstrate the convergence of the parameters
\hat{c}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
and
v^{(t)}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}
in Lemma (
5
.3) and the asymptotic relations in Lemma (
5
.4). This completes the proof.

Lemma 21
.

Let
x,\sigma^{*},\epsilon>0
and
\epsilon<\sigma^{*}
. Then,

\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})
defined in (
123
) is continuous differentiable, where
\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(0)=0
and
\operatorname{h}^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(0)=0
. It’s also a Lipschitz function with Lipschitz constant
L_{\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}}\triangleq\max(1,2+\frac{1.2\sigma^{*}}{e\epsilon})
.
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})=\operatorname{h}(x;\sigma^{*})
whenever
\operatorname{h}(x;\sigma^{*})
is continuous at x.

Proof.

Note that
\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})
is defined as

\displaystyle\operatorname{h}(x;\sigma^{*})=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }0\leq x<\sigma^{*}\\
x&\text{ if }\sigma^{*}\leq x.\end{cases}

(260)

Consider the bump function
B(x):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle B(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}e^{\frac{1}{x^{2}-1}}&|x|<1\\
0.&otherwise\\
\end{array}\right.

(261)

\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})
is given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})=\int\operatorname{h}(t;\sigma^{*})B_{\epsilon}(x-t)dt=\frac{\int_{\sigma^{*}}^{+\infty}te^{\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{(x-t)^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}\boldsymbol{1}_{|x-t|\leq\epsilon}}{\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}e^{\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{x^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}\boldsymbol{1}_{|x|\leq\epsilon}dx}dt=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }0\leq x\leq\sigma^{*}-\epsilon\\
\frac{\int_{\sigma^{*}}^{x+\epsilon}te^{\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{(x-t)^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}dt}{\int_{-\epsilon}^{+\epsilon}e^{\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{x^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}dx}&\text{ if }\sigma^{*}-\epsilon<x<\sigma^{*}+\epsilon\\
x&\text{ if }\sigma^{*}+\epsilon\leq x\end{cases}

(262)

As shown in (
262
), for a
\epsilon_{1}
with
\sigma^{*}-\epsilon>\epsilon_{1}>0
, we only need to prove the continuous differentiable property in a bounded domain
x\in[\sigma^{*}-\epsilon-\epsilon_{1},\sigma^{*}-\epsilon+\epsilon_{1}]
. In the above domain, the integral only related to
\operatorname{h}(t;\sigma^{*})
, where
t\in[\sigma^{*}-\epsilon_{1},\sigma^{*}+2\epsilon+\epsilon_{1}]
. Define

\displaystyle\operatorname{h}_{p}(x;\sigma^{*})=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }0\leq x<\sigma^{*}\\
x&\text{ if }\sigma^{*}\leq x\leq\sigma^{*}+2\epsilon+\epsilon_{1}\\
0&\text{ if }\sigma^{*}+2\epsilon+\epsilon_{1}<x.\end{cases}

(263)

Then, we only need to prove the the continuous differentiable property for
\operatorname{h}_{p}(x,\sigma^{*})\star B_{\epsilon}(x)
in
[\sigma^{*}-\epsilon-\epsilon_{1},\sigma^{*}-\epsilon+\epsilon_{1}]
, where

\displaystyle\operatorname{h}_{p}(x,\sigma^{*})\star B_{\epsilon}(x)=\operatorname{h}(x,\sigma^{*})\star B_{\epsilon}(x).

(264)

Note that
\operatorname{h}_{p}(x,\sigma^{*})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
is of compact support. The bump function
B_{\epsilon}(x)
is infinitely differentiable and its all derivations are bounded. Then, by the Dominated Convergence Theorem,
x\in(\sigma^{*}-\epsilon-\epsilon_{1},\sigma^{*}-\epsilon+\epsilon_{1})
, we obtain for any
k\geq 1

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{k}\operatorname{h}(x,\sigma^{*})\star B_{\epsilon}(x)}{\partial x^{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{k}\operatorname{h}_{p}(x,\sigma^{*})\star B_{\epsilon}(x)}{\partial x^{k}}=\operatorname{h}_{p}(x,\sigma^{*})\star\frac{\partial^{k}B_{\epsilon}(x)}{\partial x^{k}}.

(265)

Note that
\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})
is infinitely differentiable in
0\geq x<\sigma^{*}
and
x>\sigma^{*}+\epsilon
. Combing the two parts, we conclude that
\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})
is infinitely differentiable in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with bounded
k
-th derivations. Concretely, the first-order derivative is given in (
266
).

\displaystyle\operatorname{h}^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }0\leq x\leq\sigma^{*}-\epsilon\\
\frac{\int_{\sigma^{*}}^{x+\epsilon}\frac{-2t\epsilon^{2}(x-t)}{\left((x-t)^{2}-\epsilon^{2}\right)^{2}}e^{\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{(x-t)^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}dt}{\int_{-\epsilon}^{+\epsilon}e^{\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{x^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}dx}&\text{ if }\sigma^{*}-\epsilon<x<\sigma^{*}+\epsilon\\
1&\text{ if }\sigma^{*}+\epsilon\leq x\end{cases}

(266)

Consider
\operatorname{h}^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})
in
(\sigma^{*}-\epsilon,\sigma^{*}+\epsilon)
. Let
y=\frac{t-x}{\epsilon}
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{h}^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})=\frac{\epsilon\int^{1}_{\frac{\sigma^{*}-x}{\epsilon}}\frac{y(y+\frac{x}{\epsilon})}{\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{2}}e^{\frac{1}{y^{2}-1}}dy}{\int_{-\epsilon}^{+\epsilon}e^{\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{x^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}dx}.

(267)

where
