0.44\epsilon<\int_{-\epsilon}^{+\epsilon}e^{\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{x^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}dx<0.45\epsilon
.
Note that for any
x\in(\sigma^{*}-\epsilon,\sigma^{*}+\epsilon)

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{\frac{\sigma^{*}-x}{\epsilon}}\frac{y(y+\frac{x}{\epsilon})}{\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{2}}e^{\frac{1}{y^{2}-1}}dy.

(268)

Expand the exponential

\displaystyle\frac{\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{2}}{y^{2}}e^{\frac{1}{1-y^{2}}}=\sum_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\frac{(1-y^{2})^{2}}{y^{2}}\frac{1}{(1-y^{2})^{n}}.

(269)

It’s easy to see that as
|y|\to 1
,
\frac{y(y+\frac{x}{\epsilon})}{\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{2}}e^{\frac{1}{y^{2}-1}}\to 0
. Then

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{\frac{\sigma^{*}-x}{\epsilon}}\frac{y(y+\frac{x}{\epsilon})}{\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{2}}e^{\frac{1}{y^{2}-1}}dy

\displaystyle=\int^{1}_{\frac{\sigma^{*}-x}{\epsilon}}\frac{y^{2}}{\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{2}}e^{\frac{1}{y^{2}-1}}dy+\frac{x}{\epsilon}\int^{1}_{\frac{\sigma^{*}-x}{\epsilon}}\frac{y}{\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{2}}e^{\frac{1}{y^{2}-1}}dy

(270)

\displaystyle<2\int^{1}_{0}\frac{1}{\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{2}}e^{\frac{1}{y^{2}-1}}dy+\frac{x}{2\epsilon}e^{\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{(\sigma^{*}-x)^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}

\displaystyle<0.8+\frac{x}{2\epsilon}e^{\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{(\sigma^{*}-x)^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}<0.875+\frac{\sigma^{*}}{2e\epsilon}

and

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{\frac{\sigma^{*}-x}{\epsilon}}\frac{y(y+\frac{x}{\epsilon})}{\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{2}}e^{\frac{1}{y^{2}-1}}dy>0+\frac{x}{\epsilon}\int^{1}_{\frac{\sigma^{*}-x}{\epsilon}}\frac{y}{\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{2}}e^{\frac{1}{y^{2}-1}}=\frac{x}{2\epsilon}e^{\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{(\sigma^{*}-x)^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}>0.

(271)

Then,
\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})
is monotonically increasing and continuously differentiable function. It’s also a Lipschitz function with Lipschitz constant
L_{\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}}\triangleq\max(1,2+\frac{1.2\sigma^{*}}{e\epsilon})
.

Recall that
\operatorname{h}(x;\sigma^{*})
is defined as (
260
) and is continuous at
(0,\sigma^{*})
and
(\sigma^{*},+\infty)
. The remaining task is to prove
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})
for any
x\in(0,\sigma^{*})\cup(\sigma^{*},+\infty)
. Recall that
\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})
is given in
(
262
). Then,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&x<\sigma^{*}\\
x&x>\sigma^{*}.\\
\end{array}\right.

(272)

This imply
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})
continuous at
(0,\sigma^{*})
and
(\sigma^{*},+\infty)
, which completes the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Proof of Lemma
9

In Lemma
21
, we prove
\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x;\sigma^{*})
satisfies the requirements in Assumption (
3
.vi). By Lemma
5
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}\overset{p}{=}\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon}(v)

(273)

where
\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{L}}(v,\epsilon)
is given in (
72
) with
f_{\boldsymbol{L}}(x)=\operatorname{h}_{\epsilon}(x,\tilde{\sigma}^{*})
.

Then, we prove the convergence of
\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(v)
. Note that, for
k,l>0
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{k}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}-\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{l}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{k}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{l}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})\right\|^{2}_{F}

(274)

\displaystyle\overset{p}{=}\frac{1}{v}\mathbb{E}[(f_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{k}}(\sigma)-f_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{l}}(\sigma))^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{v}\max(\epsilon_{l}^{2}+\epsilon_{l}\tilde{\sigma}^{*},\epsilon_{k}^{2}+\epsilon_{k}\tilde{\sigma}^{*}).

For any
\xi_{1}>0
, by
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
, there exists a
N
such that for any
i>N
, such that
\epsilon_{i}<\frac{v\xi_{1}}{4\sigma^{*}}
. Then
for any
k,l>N
, the following holds

\displaystyle|\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{k}}(v)-\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{l}}(v)|\leq\xi_{1}.

(275)

Then, by Cauchy principle of convergence, we conclude that
\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{MSE}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(v)
converges weakly to a deterministic value.
For the best-rank-
r
denoiser, by the triangle inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}-\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})\right\|^{2}_{F}\leq\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}

(276)

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|\leq\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\boldsymbol{L}\right\|^{2}_{F}+\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})\right\|^{2}_{F}.

(277)

If we can show

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})\right\|^{2}_{F}\overset{p}{=}0,

(278)

then (
125
) holds. Note that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})-\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})\right\|^{2}_{F}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}v}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}}{\sqrt{vn_{2}}})-\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\frac{\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}}{\sqrt{vn_{2}}})\right\|^{2}_{F}

(279)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{v}\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum^{r}_{i=1}(f_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i})-\tilde{\sigma}_{i})^{2}+\frac{1}{v}\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum^{n_{1}}_{i>r}f^{2}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i})\overset{p}{=}\frac{1}{v}\mathbb{E}[F_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i},\gamma}(\sigma)]

with

\displaystyle F_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i},\gamma}(\sigma)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\left(f_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\sigma)-\sigma\right)^{2}&\tilde{\sigma}^{*}\leq\sigma\\
f^{2}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\sigma)&0\geq\sigma<\tilde{\sigma}^{*},\\
\end{array}\right.

(280)

where the expectation is taken over the limiting empirical singular value distribution of
\frac{\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}}{\sqrt{vn_{1}}}
.

Since
F_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i},\gamma}(\sigma)<f^{2}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\sigma)+\sigma^{2}
and
\mathbb{E}[f^{2}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\sigma)+\sigma^{2}]<\infty
, if we can show

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon_{i}\to 0+}F_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i},\gamma}(\sigma)=0,\quad\text{a.s.}

(281)

then by the dominated convergence theorem, we can conclude (
125
). Combine with (
262
), it is straightforward to conclude

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon_{i}\to 0+}f^{2}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\sigma)=0,

\displaystyle\text{for}\quad\sigma\in(0,\tilde{\sigma}^{*})

(282)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon_{i}\to 0+}\left(f_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\sigma)-\sigma\right)^{2}=0,

\displaystyle\text{for}\quad\sigma\in(\tilde{\sigma}^{*},+\infty).

Then, we conclude the proof for (
125
).
Similar to the above arguments, we can conclude

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})\right\|^{2}_{F}\overset{p}{=}\frac{1}{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}})\right\|^{2}_{F};\quad\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left\langle\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}),\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\right\rangle\overset{p}{=}\frac{1}{n}\left\langle\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}),\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\right\rangle.

(283)

Then, combining (
125
), it is straightforward to obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left\langle\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}),\boldsymbol{L}\right\rangle\overset{p}{=}\frac{1}{n}\left\langle\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}),\boldsymbol{L}\right\rangle;\quad\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left\langle\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},\epsilon_{i}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}),\boldsymbol{N}\right\rangle\overset{p}{=}\frac{1}{n}\left\langle\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{L},b}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{N}}),\boldsymbol{N}\right\rangle.

(284)

Then, with above results, it’s easy to check our claims.

Appendix E
Proof of Lemma
6

Under Assumptions 1-5, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}-\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{X}\right\rangle=\left\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L},\boldsymbol{L}+\boldsymbol{S}\right\rangle+\left\langle\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{S}+\boldsymbol{L}\right\rangle\overset{p}{=}-\phi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})-\varphi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}),

(285)

by (
67
b) of Lemma
4
and (
78
b) Lemma
5
, where
\boldsymbol{L}_{p(\boldsymbol{L})\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{L}
and
\boldsymbol{S}_{p(\boldsymbol{S})\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{S}
are separately independent with
\boldsymbol{S}
and
\boldsymbol{L}
.
Then

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}\overset{p}{=}\phi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)})+\varphi(v_{\delta\to\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}).

(286)

Note that
\frac{x}{x\hat{v}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}+\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}}
is continuous and bounded with respect to
x
on
[0,+\infty)
for any given
\hat{v}_{\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{y}}^{(t)}
and
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}
. Recall
v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}}
in (
50
) and define empirical value of
v_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
by
\hat{v}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}}
.
For the LMMSE denoiser,

\displaystyle\quad\hat{v}^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\overset{p}{=}v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}}\!-\!\alpha(v^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}})^{2}\int\!\!\frac{\!\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}}\!}{\!\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}}\!v^{(t)}_{\!\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{y}\!}\!+\!\sigma_{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}\!}dp(\theta_{\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{T}})

(287)

by using Assumption
3
and definition of weak convergence.
Similarly, we prove that
\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(t)}
,

and
v^{(t+1)}_{\boldsymbol{y}\to\boldsymbol{X}}
converge weakly and obtain their asymptotic forms.

Appendix F
Proofs Related to the Convergence Analysis of the State Evolution

F-A

Proof of Lemma
7

Consider the following SE:

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t-1)}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t-1)}\right),\quad\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t-1)}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t-1)}\right).

(288)

Note that by Assumption
4
,
\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t-1)}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t-1)}\right)
and
\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t-1)}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t-1)}\right)
are monotonically increasing functions. It follows that

\displaystyle\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)\geq\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\right)

(289)

\displaystyle\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\geq\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\right).

Consider the sequences
\left\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{L},nec}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq 1}
and
\left\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{S},nec}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq 1}
generated by the following iteration (under the same initial values as the original SE):

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S},nec}^{(t)}=\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S},nec}^{(t-1)}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{L},nec}^{(t-1)}\right)\right)

(290a)

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{L},nec}^{(t)}=\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L},nec}^{(t-1)}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{S},nec}^{(t-1)}\right)\right).

(290b)

From (
289
) and a simple induction argument, we obtain

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\geq\tau_{\boldsymbol{S},nec}^{(t)}\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\geq\tau_{\boldsymbol{L},nec}^{(t)}.

(291)

Hence, a necessary condition for

\lim_{t\to\infty}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=0

is

\lim_{t\to\infty}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L},nec}^{(t)}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S},nec}^{(t)}=0.

The rest of the proof is to analyze the condition under which
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{L},nec}^{(t)}\}
and
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{L},nec}^{(t)}\}
converge to zero. To this end, we sum up (
289
a) and (
289
b) and get

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S},nec}^{(t)}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{L},nec}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S},nec}^{(t-1)}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{L},nec}^{(t-1)}\right)\right)+\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L},nec}^{(t-1)}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{S},nec}^{(t-1)}\right)\right).

(292)

We define
\tau_{\boldsymbol{X},nec}^{(t)}:=\tau_{\boldsymbol{S},nec}^{(t)}+\tau_{\boldsymbol{L},nec}^{(t)}
and rewrite (
292
) as

\tau_{\boldsymbol{X},nec}^{(t)}=\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{X},nec}^{(t-1)}\right)+\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{X},nec}^{(t-1)}\right).

It is easy to see that
\left\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{X},nec}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq 1}
converges to
0
from any initial value if and only if the following condition holds

\displaystyle x>\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)x\right)+\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)x\right),\quad\forall x>0.

(293)

Let
y=(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1)x
, Then, the above equation is rewritten as

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}y>\varphi\left(y\right)+\phi\left(y\right),\quad\forall y>0.

(294)

Thus, we obtain the necessary condition that
\alpha
satisfies

\displaystyle\alpha>\alpha_{nec}:=\sup_{x>0}\ \frac{\varphi(x)+\phi(x)}{\varphi(x)+\phi(x)+x}\quad\forall x>0.

(295)

On the other hand, the sequences
\left\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{L},nec}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq 1}
and
\left\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{S},nec}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq 1}
converge to
0
if and only if
the sequence
\left\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{X},nec}^{(t)}\right\}_{t\geq 1}
converges to
0
.

F-B

Proof of Lemma
8

Our proof strategy is as follows. We will show that if
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})})\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
but not at the origin, then both
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}
and
\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}
improve in the next iteration, i.e.,

\begin{split}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\leq\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})},\quad\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\leq\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}.\end{split}

(296)

Further, we show that if
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})})\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
, then
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+1)},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0}+1)})\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
. We then use an induction argument to conclude that the sequences
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq t_{0}}
and
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq t_{0}}
monotonically converge, as both sequences are bounded. Finally, we show the limit points must be
(0,0)
when
\alpha>\max\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}
.

F-B
1
Step 1

Our first goal is to prove (
296
). From (
115
),
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})})\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
implies

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})})\geq\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})}\geq\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}),

(297a)

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})})\geq\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}\geq\Psi_{2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})}).

(297b)

Since
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
are monotonically increasing (from Lemma
22
), the second inequalities of (
297
) yield

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{-1}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})}\right)\geq\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})},\quad\Psi_{2}^{-1}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}\right)\geq\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})}.

(298)

Recall the definitions of
\Psi_{1}^{-1}
and
\Psi_{2}^{-1}
:

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}},\alpha)\triangleq\alpha\varphi^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})-(1-\alpha)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}

(299)

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}_{2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}},\alpha)\triangleq\alpha\phi^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})-(1-\alpha)\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}.

Using (
299
) and noting that
\varphi
and
\phi
are increasing (see Assumption
4
), we can rewrite (
298
) as

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})}\geq\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}\right)=\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+1)},

(300a)

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}\geq\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})}\right)=\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0}+1)},

(300b)

where the two equalities follow from the SE equation (
102
). This concludes the proof of (
296
).

F-B
2
Step 2

We next prove
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+1)},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0}+1)})\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
. Note that

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{-1}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\right)-\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\Psi^{-1}_{1}\left(\varphi\left(\left(\alpha^{-1}-1\right)\tau^{(t_{0})}_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\alpha^{-1}\tau^{(t_{0})}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)\right)-\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}

(301a)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}(1-\alpha)\tau^{(t_{0})}_{\boldsymbol{S}}-(1-\alpha)\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau^{(t_{0})}_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau^{(t_{0})}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)\cdot\left[\tau^{(t_{0})}_{\boldsymbol{S}}-\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau^{(t_{0})}_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau^{(t_{0})}_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}(1-\alpha)\cdot\left[\tau^{(t_{0})}_{\boldsymbol{S}}-\tau^{(t_{0}+1)}_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\geq}0,

where step (a) is the definition of
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+1)}
(see, e.g., (
300
)); step (b) is based on the definition of
\Psi_{1}
(see (
299
)); step (c) is again due to the definition of
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+1)}
; and step (d) is from (
300
). As
\Psi_{1}
is an increasing function, (
301a
) implies

\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\geq\Psi_{1}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})}\right).

(301b)

Similarly, it can be shown that

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}^{-1}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\right)-\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})}\geq 0,

(302a)

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\geq\Psi_{2}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})}\right).

(302b)

Combining (
300
), (
301a
) and (
301b
) yields

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}^{-1}\left(\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\!\right)\geq\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\geq\Psi_{1}\left(\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\!\right),

(303)

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{-1}\left(\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\!\right)\geq\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\geq\Psi_{2}\left(\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+1)}\!\right),

which means that
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0}+1)},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0}+1)})\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
.

F-B
3
Step 3

Step 1 and Step 2 above already imply the convergence of the sequences
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq t_{0}}
and
\{\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}\}_{t\geq t_{0}}
, under the condition
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t_{0})},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t_{0})})\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
. Clearly, the limit point must be a fixed point of the SE equations (
\varphi
and
\phi
are continuous functions):

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t-1)}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t-1)}\right),

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t-1)}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t-1)}\right).

From Lemma
23
, the above SE equations have a unique solution at
(0,0)
when
\alpha>\max\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}
.
Hence,

\lim_{t\to\infty}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}^{(t)}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(t)}=0.

F-C

Proof of Theorem
2

We start with the following Lemmas.

Lemma 22
.

Suppose Assumption
4
holds, and
\alpha>\max\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}
where

\begin{split}\alpha_{1}:=\sup_{x>0}\ \frac{\varphi^{\prime}(x)}{\varphi^{\prime}(x)+1},\alpha_{2}:=\sup_{x>0}\ \frac{\phi^{\prime}(x)}{\phi^{\prime}(x)+1}.\end{split}

(304)

Then, the following statements hold.

(i)

For any fixed
\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}>0
, the following equation

\displaystyle\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)=\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}.

(305)

has a unique globally attractive fixed point
\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})
in
(0,M_{\varphi})
, meaning that

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}\!<\!\varphi\left(\!\left(\!\frac{1}{\alpha}\!-\!1\!\right)\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}\!+\!\frac{1}{\alpha}\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\!\right)\!<\!\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}),\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}\in(0,\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}))

(306a)

and

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})\!<\!\varphi\left(\!\left(\!\frac{1}{\alpha}\!-\!1\!\right)\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}\!+\!\frac{1}{\alpha}\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\!\right)\!<\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}},\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}\in(\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}),\infty),

(306b)

where
M_{\varphi}=\sup\varphi
.

(ii)

For any fixed
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}>0
, the following equation

\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right)=\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}.

(307)

has a unique globally attractive fixed point
\Psi_{2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})
in
(0,M_{\phi})
, where
M_{\phi}=\sup\phi
.

(iii)

The fixed point function
\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})
obtained in (i) is strictly monotonically increasing
[0,\infty)
with
\Psi_{1}(0)=0
.

(iv)

The fixed point function
\Psi_{2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})
obtained in (ii) is strictly monotonically increasing
[0,\infty)
with
\Psi_{2}(0)=0
.

Proof.

Part (i):
Based on Assumption
4
,
\varphi(x)
is continuous and monotonically increasing on
[0,\infty)
. Then, for any fixed
\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\in(0,\infty)
,
\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)
is strictly monotonically increasing with respect to
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}
on
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}\in[0,+\infty)
.
Then define

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}};\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})=\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)-\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}.

(308)

For any fixed
\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\in(0,\infty)
,
\hat{\varphi}(0)>0
and

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}^{\prime}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}};\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})=\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\varphi^{\prime}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)-1.

(309)

Note that there exists a positive
\epsilon
such that
\alpha>\alpha_{1}+\epsilon>\alpha_{1}
. Then,
\hat{\varphi}^{\prime}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}};\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})<-\epsilon
and there exist one
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}=\Psi_{1}(\boldsymbol{L})\in(0,\frac{\hat{\varphi}^{\prime}(0)}{\epsilon}]
such that
\hat{\varphi}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*})=0
. Hence, (
305
) has exactly one fixed point. The corresponding
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}
for any given
\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}
is defined as
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}=\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})
. Furthermore, for
0<\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}<\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}

\displaystyle\overset{a}{<}\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)

(310)

\displaystyle\overset{b}{<}\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{c}{=}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}=\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}),

where step (a) is from the property of
\hat{\varphi}^{\prime}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}};\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})
; step (b) is from the strictly monotonicity increasing property of
\varphi
; step (c) is the definition of
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}
.

For
\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}\in(\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}),\infty)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}

\displaystyle>\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)>\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\!\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)=\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}^{*}=\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}).

(311)

Part (ii): The proof is similar to the proof in Part (i);

Part (iii): Recall that

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}},\alpha)=\alpha\varphi^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})-(1-\alpha)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}.

Then,
\Psi_{1}^{-1}(0,\alpha)=0
.
For
\alpha>\alpha_{1}
,
\Psi_{1}^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})
is strictly monotonicity increasing.

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Psi_{1}^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})}{\partial\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}}=\alpha\frac{1}{\varphi^{\prime}(\varphi^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}))}-1+\alpha>0.

(312)

Then,
\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})
is strictly monotonicity increasing.

The proof of part (iv) is similar to part (iii).
∎

Lemma
22
above is concerned with the fixed points of the SE equations in (
102
). Then, we present the crucial points for
\alpha
in the following Lemma .

Lemma 23
.

Suppose that Assumption
4
holds. If
\alpha>\max(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
, where

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x>0}\ \frac{\varphi^{\prime}(x)}{\varphi^{\prime}(x)+1},

(313a)

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x>0}\ \frac{\phi^{\prime}(x)}{\phi^{\prime}(x)+1},

(313b)

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\alpha\in(0,1);x\geq\Psi_{1}(\Psi_{2}(x,\alpha),\alpha),\forall 0<x<\sup\varphi\},

(313c)

(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})=(0,0)
is the only fixed point of (
103
).

Proof.

Note that from Lemma
22
,
\alpha>\max(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
guarantee
\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})
and
\Psi_{2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})
are respectively the only global attractive fixed point of

\displaystyle\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}\right)=\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}

(314a)

and

\displaystyle\phi\left(\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right)=\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}}.

(314b)

Furthermore,
\Psi_{1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})
and
\Psi_{2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}})
are both continuous and strictly monotonically increasing. Then, intersect points of the two curve are the fixed point of (
103
). To guarantee
(0,0)
is the only fixed point. We need
\alpha
satisfy

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{-1}(x,\alpha)-\Psi_{2}(x,\alpha)>0,\forall 0<x<\sup\varphi

(315a)

or

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{-1}(x,\alpha)-\Psi_{2}(x,\alpha)<0,\forall 0<x<\sup\varphi

(315b)

Consider that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Psi_{2}^{-1}(\Psi_{1}^{-1}(x,\alpha),\alpha)}{\partial\alpha}

\displaystyle=\phi^{-1}\left(\alpha\varphi^{-1}(x)-(1-\alpha)x\right)+\alpha\left(\alpha\varphi^{-1}(x)\!-\!(\!1\!-\!\alpha\!)\!x\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\!\alpha\frac{\varphi^{-1}\!(\!x\!)\!+\!x}{\phi^{\prime}\!\left(\!\varphi^{-1}\!\left(\!\alpha\!\varphi^{-1}\!(\!x)\!-\!(\!1\!-\!\alpha)x\right)\right)}\!-\!(\!1\!-\!\alpha\!)\left(\!\varphi^{-1}(x)+x\!\right).

(316a)

\alpha>\alpha_{2}
implies
\alpha\varphi^{-1}(x)-(1-\alpha)x>0
for any
x>0
. Then, the first and the second terms are both positive. Note that
\alpha>\sup(\frac{\phi^{\prime}(x)}{\phi^{\prime}(x)+1}:x\geq 0)
. Then, the sum of the third term and the forth term is positive.
Then, for any given
x
,
\Psi_{2}^{-1}(\Psi_{1}^{-1}(x,\alpha),\alpha)
is strictly monotonically increasing with respect to
\alpha
in
(\max(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}),1)
.
And there exist no
\alpha>\max(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
such that (
315b
) hold. Note that
\alpha_{3}
is defined as

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}=\inf\{\alpha\in(0,1);x\geq\Psi_{1}(\Psi_{2}(x,\alpha),\alpha),\forall 0<x<\sup\varphi\}

(317)

Thus, for any
\alpha>\alpha_{3}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}^{-1}(\Psi_{1}^{-1}(x,\alpha),\alpha)>x,\forall 0<x<\sup\varphi.

(318)

Then, we complete the proof.
∎

We know ready to prove Theorem
2
. Note that
\alpha>\max\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}
ensure that both (
103
a) and (
103
b) only have one globally attractive fixed point by Lemmas
22
. Moreover, as
\alpha>\max\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}
, we prove that
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})=(0,0)
is the only fixed point of the SE in Lemma
23
. The rest is to prove the SE dynamics converges to this fixed point from any starting point. In Lemma
8
, we prove that for any initialization in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
, the SE converge to
(\tau_{\boldsymbol{S}},\tau_{\boldsymbol{L}})=(0,0)
. This completes the overall proof.
